===== BOOK TWO ITEM 1: Missed a Spot — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (missed-a-spot.md) =====

Her hand went out before she was anyone, across the wide of the bed to where the heat of him belonged, and found cotton. Smooth, and cold.

Her eyes opened on the cold of it. His side lay smoothed flat in the thin morning light, and for one lurch of a heartbeat she was back in December — the door, the county road, all of it un-happened — and then his voice arrived from the night before, low against her hair while she was already going under: *farmers market in the morning. Early. Sleep as long as you like.* Her heart came down off its ledge. She pressed her face into his pillow, which held the smell of him the flannel shirt used to hold, and lay there breathing it, awake now and in no hurry to admit it.

The bed itself was an argument for staying. A king, wider than any bed had business being for a man who slept alone — who had slept alone; she corrected the tense with her whole body, stretching into the acreage of it — dressed in plain white cotton gone soft as an old shirt from years of washing, and heavy quilting over that, and the deep give of a good mattress under everything.

A month she had spent in the bed of her girlhood, a narrow thing with covers cut for the child she'd been, so that all break she had slept curled and rationed, pulling the short blankets up to her chin and surrendering her feet, in a house her father kept cold at night on principle.

This house went cold at night too — she'd hear the furnace resting, the windows ticking — but here the cold had never once reached her. Here the cold was a thing on the far side of the glass that existed to make the rest of it better: the fire he'd build in the den, the tea going orange-dark in the pot, the quilt on the porch couch and the two of them under it looking up through the tall black windows at stars laid out hard and close over the ridge, and later the length of him at her back, his heat and his solidity, a wall between her and every cold thing there had ever been. She had lain against him last night doing the arithmetic of it and getting happiness for an answer every time.

Under the bedroom smell, bread. The house had it all through the rooms already, crust and yeast, deep and bakery-brown — the breadmaker he'd set going before he left — and timed, she'd have bet anything, so that she would wake inside the smell of it. He had made her bread before; nothing in this house happened at an accidental hour. Provided for. Even his absence had been cooked in advance.

His sweats lay folded at the foot of the bed, a clean pair of thick grey fleece squared away where they had not been last night, set out plainly for her to find. She looked at them a long moment, and smiled, and left them where they were.

She had nothing else. That was the plain fact of her situation: one coat, two borrowed heels standing by the front door, and not a stitch besides in the whole county with her name on it. Yesterday the fact had been terror — buttoned to the collar, dying by inches in front of Kayla's laundry basket. This morning she turned the same fact over and found it had changed in the night into something else entirely. She *couldn't* dress. Poor thing. There was simply nothing for it but to be naked in a warm house that smelled of bread, and she threw back the covers and got up into the morning bare, her painted toes swinging down onto his floor, and stood there a moment in the luxury of it — nothing binding, nothing pinching, the air of his house moving over all of her at once.

She gave her shoulders a shake, just to feel everything move, and everything did, and she laughed out loud at herself, alone, delighted.

She took a long shower, the water hot at once and staying hot the whole indulgent length of it — everything in this house came generous and never ran out. She stood under it far past clean and let it beat on her shoulders while she ran the night before forward into the day ahead — his mouth, his hands, the truck already on its way back to her — and her own hands went slower over herself than washing wanted, soaping the length of her, lingering where the night had been, not to finish anything, only to keep the warmth of it lit. Somewhere out there he was choosing food for her. The thought poured down her with the water. She dried herself with the evening towel, the one that smelled like the day of him, and came out of the steam flushed and loose-limbed and wanting the morning to hurry.

At the sink she pulled the top drawer open — hers, the one he'd emptied without a word back in the fall — and worked a scrunchie loose from the form, deep green with the little white dots, and put her hair up. In the mirror, for one obliging moment, she dressed the girl in the glass in his sweats — grey, rolled twice at the waist, swimming on her, decent. Then she took them off again with a blink. No. The girl as she stood had her chin up and her hair high and not one other thing on, and looked, honestly, magnificent.

She went down the hall with a swing in her step. His for the taking, was the thought that carried her the first few steps — a woman kept naked in a man's house — by her own hand — available, his to take — a pleasure slave — and the thought ran a shiver through her that had nothing of cold in it.

Then, somewhere between the bathroom and the kitchen, the thought turned itself inside out. Because — she counted it off on her fingers, padding barefoot over his floors — he brought her off first. Always, every time, before he took a single thing for himself. She slept in; he rose in the dark and set bread going for her. He cooked for her and then cleaned while she sat with her glass of wine and watched him do it. He was out in the January cold this minute, buying food to feed her. He had sewn her a shirt with his own hands, and a gown.

She stopped in the hallway, bare as the day she was born, sugaring with laughter. Some slave. If anyone in this house was a pleasure slave, it was not her; she — and she gave a little shimmy on the thought, and her breasts went along with it, and she giggled — she was a pleasure princess. Not the kind of woman who is kept for a man's pleasure. The kind a man keeps *in* pleasure. Given it, morning to night, hand and mouth and stove. The distinction landed low in her, wicked and happy at once, and she carried it the rest of the way to the kitchen like something warm she'd stolen.

The coffee was made, still hot in the pot. There was real cream in the fridge, because he was the kind of man who kept real cream in the fridge, and she poured a mug and clouded it pale and then spooned in more sugar than the coffee had any right to take, smiling at the face he pulled every time he watched her do it — pained, long-suffering, entirely for show; it was his sugar bowl, and he kept it full — and drank it at the counter in nothing but her scrunchie, entirely pleased with herself.

Between the pot and the bottom of the mug she made one trip back down the hall, and spread the cream cashmere square and smooth over the foot of the bed, and stood back and looked at her work. Then she went back to her coffee, entirely composed.

The truck came up the gravel while the mug was still half full.

Less than a minute from the engine cutting off to his boots on the porch — and she spent all of it arranging herself into perfect carelessness: hip settled against the counter, ankles crossed, the mug cradled in both hands, a woman so at ease in her skin she had plainly forgotten she hadn't any clothes on. Her heart was going like a hammer. She fixed her face into idle sweetness and aimed it at the door.

The door opened on him and the cold came in around him, and he stopped.

He stopped entirely — a bag of groceries in each arm, the January air pouring past him into the room, and his eyes came up and found her and simply stayed. Color climbed his throat. "Whoa—" he said, and the rest of the sentence never arrived; and she stood there in her borrowed nonchalance and watched a man who always had the right words — few, and exact — stand in his own doorway with none at all. A man whose composure felt like a law of nature — and she had now broken it twice.

Then she watched him gather it back — visibly, by hand, the composure reassembled piece by piece like a man picking up dropped change — and he came in and let the door shut behind him and said, level as a plank, "I got mushrooms."

The laugh burst out of her, and she didn't fight it.

"You found the sweats, then," he said, setting the bags on the counter, his eyes making no promises about staying anywhere they were put.

"I did." She sipped her terribly sweet coffee. "They didn't suit."

He looked at her for a moment longer, and something moved through his face that was not composure at all. "Come here first," he said, quieter, and she went. His mouth found hers. He looked at her a moment. Then he hung the coat on its peg, washed his hands, and took the apron down off its hook and tied it on — the canvas apron, the one whose strap she'd once hooked a finger behind and pulled, the first night he ever cooked for her, and everything that had followed the pulling — and if he noticed where her eyes went, or what her mouth did, he gave no sign. He thumbed the music going from his phone — "Sara," low, Stevie Nicks singing to a kitchen with a naked woman in it — and turned to the counter and went to work.

She took her chair at the little table, coffee in both hands, and watched, because watching him cook was one of the oldest pleasures she had in this house and six weeks had not made it smaller. He tipped the loaf out of the breadmaker, tall and brown and knocking hollow, and set it on the wire rack. She asked why not slice it hot, and he said, "It needs to develop a crust," in the tone he kept for facts, and put it in its patch of morning light.

The blood oranges he did the way he did everything. Top and bottom off in two strokes, the fruit stood flat, and then the chef's knife rode down the curve of it in long paring passes, rind and pith falling away in strips until nothing white was left, only the wet dark flesh shining. Then over a bowl, the blade slid along each membrane and freed the segments whole, one by one, luscious and startling, a red with no argument in it, and the emptied husk of membranes wrung out in his fist at the end like a rag. Nothing between anybody and the fruit. It was, she thought, watching him lay the segments out, the most him thing she had ever seen done to food. The persimmons he simply sliced, thin amber rounds with their dark star at the center, and fanned beside the orange on the plates, and she saw the plating happen, the care of it, breakfast being built in layers like a case being made.

Only then the mushrooms — because mushrooms, she knew by now, waited for nothing once they started. He sliced them on the wooden board, the knife-taps making their own staccato music — a quick run of them, a rest while his hand regrouped the caps, a run again — and dropped them into butter already going quiet-to-foaming in the skillet, and the sizzle came up and the smell came with it, deep and animal, browning butter and dark earth, a smell with shoulders on it. He dusted smoked paprika over and the steam bloomed warm and smoky, campfire-sweet; a splash of sherry hissed and went up sweet and sharp at once.

Then the loaf. He took the serrated knife to it, and the crust announced itself — a crackle that carried all the way across the kitchen, the sound of a thing that had come out right — and he dropped two thick slices in the toaster, and turned back to the skillet, back to the toaster, back to the skillet — a rhythm, she found, that a woman could set a watch by, or make use of.

With the last of her coffee, in her chair, in her scrunchie. She began — idly, innocently — to make herself impossible not to look at. She reached up and adjusted the scrunchie, taking her time, arms high, everything lifting. She resettled herself against the chair back with a long sigh. When he turned to stir, she was smoothing both hands slowly down her hips as though checking the fit of the nothing she had on.

The clockwork began to slip — his turns coming a half-beat late and staying a half-beat long — and she banked each one like earnings, watching the exact man in his apron come gently unsequenced. The game was not free to play, either. Then she drew a knee up, set her foot on the chair edge, and bent to examine her own toes — and he did not turn around at all. The skillet waited. The toaster ticked on. The man stood with his back to his own cooking, plainly elsewhere, and the first thread of smoke went up behind him unnoticed by anyone but her.

She let it go a second longer than was kind.

"Something's burning," she said. And then, into his glance: "The toast, too."

He rescued it too late, and stood a moment holding the two black slices with the smoke rising off them, the smell of char cutting straight across the morning's whole perfume of bread, and looked at her — at her face, deliberately, only her face — while she gazed back with the wide eyes of the wrongly accused.

"I can put the sweats on," she offered, "if you need to concentrate."

"No," he said, before the sentence was even all the way out of her, and dropped the burnt slices in the bin and cut two more.

The second round of toast he watched like a hawk, or at least like a man trying very hard to be a hawk, and when it came up gold he buttered it while it was hot, generously, edge to edge, the butter going glassy and running, and cut each slice on the diagonal, and killed the heat under the skillet and squeezed lemon over the pan — one bright hiss — and heaped the mushrooms over, dark and glistening, and finished it with a scatter of parsley chopped so fine it was nearly a dust of green. He set her plate in front of her and the fruit alongside, red and amber — and at the last, over the persimmon fans, he pinched a few grains of sea salt from the little dish by the stove, hardly any, a rumor of salt. "They serve them this way in Japan," he said, to her raised eyebrow. "With a little salt. It wakes the fruit up." And he sat down across from her with his own, and the kitchen went quiet except for the last of the skillet ticking on the cold burner.

The first bite put a sound in her throat. There had been a time — September, a hundred years ago — when she would have called this *good* and gone on chewing; a girl raised on casserole and dining-hall trays, tasting in two colors. A fall of his table had done something to her tongue since, taught it to listen, and now the bite arrived in order and she could read every line of it. The fruit after was another country. The blood orange went off cold and sweet-tart, a shock after all that dark. She screamed. Mouth and fingers, outside and deep, the two together consumed her, and the watching part of her, with nothing left anywhere to track, let go all at once and dropped, and it whited out sight and sound both — her whole body clenching, pulsing around his fingers, her thighs shut around his ears and his name coming apart in her throat.

And then, while she was still coming down, still miles from the room, he came up the length of her — his weight arriving gently, on his forearms, all around her — and found her, and slid into her slow, into the last shudders of the one before, and began to move: long easy strokes that gathered up what was left of that peak and set it climbing again before it had anywhere near finished landing. Her arms went around his neck without her. Her hips found his rhythm from below and answered it. *What this man does to me,* she thought, the words rising through her slow as bubbles through honey, the only thought left standing anywhere. *What he does to me.*

Then he kissed her.

Their mouths met open, and she kissed him back before she knew what she was kissing.

Then the slick of it arrived: on his lips, on his chin, the wet of her carried up warm into the kiss, his mouth wetter than it had ever once come to her — and her body answered it before any of her could vote, clenching hard around him, all of her at once, the air going out of her into his mouth in one shoved breath.

Her tongue went to it. That was the first thing, ahead of everything, a small live reach toward the taste before any part of her had ruled on it — her mouth answering a question it had not been asked, curious, unforbidden, hers. For a beat she was only tasting.

Then her mind arrived and told her what it was. And behind her mind, fast, from very far down and very long ago, came the flat clean voice that had been installed in her before she could have argued with it — *no, that's wrong, that's not a thing you do* — and it reached past the tongue that had already said yes and shut the whole thing down over its head.

She turned her face. She sealed her lips. She brought the back of her hand up across her mouth, the child's gesture, and the words came out sputtering on top of it, hardly a breath.

"Ugh — gross." And then, smaller: "That's — no. Wrong."

He went still. Deep in her, his weight on his arms, entirely still — and then, carefully, he began to draw back out of her.

Her legs clamped around him before the choice had words in it, heels finding his ass, locking him in to the last inch. "No — not that." It came out breathless, ahead of its own grammar. "It's fine. Don't stop. Don't you dare stop."

A beat, his eyes on hers, reading which no was which. Then he wiped his mouth with the back of his hand, one economical pass, unbothered, easy, and something in his face gentled toward her as if she had done something young and dear rather than something unkind.

"Okay," he said, low, against her jaw. Just that. He did not push it and he did not sulk and he did not explain.

And he moved again, and it went on, the good way, the two of them — his mouth at her throat, her jaw, her temple, everywhere it had ever been welcome, and not once at her mouth, easy about it, as though her mouth could wait for her. And after a while her hand went up into his hair — the same hand that had made itself let go of it once already — and pulled him down, the way her legs had pulled him in, and kissed him. He came to her glad, and kissed her as he always kissed her, and it was all still there. And somewhere in the kiss her tongue went hunting — she didn't send it; it went — along his lip, past it, into the warmth of him, looking for something it did not name, and found — maybe — under the taste that was only him, a faint edge of sharp and salt that had not been there this morning. Or found nothing. It was gone as she reached for it, if it had been there at all — and her body answered before any of her could vote, the same deep clench, all of her at once, and it broke her open: she went over, out of nowhere and mid-kiss, her cry going into his mouth, her heels pressing into his ass, and he followed her over, emptied himself into her and gathered her whole weight in and held it.

He slept afterward. He always did — a few minutes only, the whole engine of him shutting down at once, his arm still heavy across her — and she lay along him with her cheek on his chest and his heartbeat going down under her ear, and reached over and pulled the cashmere up across the both of them. The beautiful blanket he had never said one word about, which had simply appeared at the foot of the bed sometime after she'd started staying nights, which was how he gave gifts: no card, no notes, no moment where you had to say thank you.

She drew it to her chin and lay in the luxury of it, spent three times over, warm to the marrow. The cashmere settled over her bare skin with almost no weight at all, and when she breathed, the nap of it moved against her nipples — still tender, still half-awake — the softest thing that had touched her all day dragging across them slow with every rise of her chest, and she lay very still and let it, marveling: even the blanket here knew how to touch her. She could have been petted to sleep by it. Only then did she reach back over the last hour, the way you probe a place you've bitten, gently, from the inside.

Because he had liked it. That was the thing that would not lie flat. Not tolerated it, not gotten through it for her sake — *liked* it, gone down glad and stayed glad and come up wanting to bring it to her mouth like a good thing he did not see why she would not want. She turned it over. It was not a new thought so much as a thought she had never before had to hold; it had been in the room a long time, she understood now, standing quietly in the corner of every afternoon and every night like this one, and only today had it come and sat on the edge of the bed and looked at her. *Why does he like it so much.*

And there was a smaller thing, nearer in, warm to hold: he had not come back to her mouth on his own. Her throat, her jaw, her temple, her hair — but her mouth he had left for her, patient as everything else in this house, until she came and took it herself. And when she had, he'd kissed her like nothing had happened, because — she understood, pleased all through — as far as he was concerned, nothing had.

And how it had ended — that sudden drop, mid-kiss, hardly any warning at all. She smiled into his chest. She had missed kissing him; that was what that was. She had banished his mouth, and he had respected her no, and her body had gone over the moment she took his kiss back.

His kissing her like nothing had happened was its own small astonishment, because he always kissed her after. Always. He had been kissing her after since the first night in this bed — she started the count and gave it up; it was dozens, it was hundreds, his mouth on hers a minute after his mouth had been on her, every time, and it had never once been like *that*. Which meant — and the answer arrived tidy and kind and she took it, grateful — he must have forgotten to wipe. This once. That was all it was: a man forgets a thing he has done without thinking a hundred times, exactly once, and his poor luck that the once made her sputter at him like a scandalized aunt.

And while she was still holding that, comfortable inside it, her tongue went out — quick, light, over her own lower lip. Looking for something. The same something it had gone hunting for in his mouth, if it had been anything at all. She caught it doing it. It found nothing it could be sure of, only salt or the memory of salt, already gone. She pressed her lips together and did not ask herself what it had been looking for. Under the tidy explanation, fainter, the other question kept its small light on: how many times, and how carefully, and had there always been — it slid away when she reached for it, and she was too warm and too spent and too full of him to chase it, and she let it go, and it did not go all the way; it settled somewhere under the affection and stayed, minor-key, waiting.

Tuesday, she thought, drowsy, her cheek riding the slow swell of his breathing. Tuesday there was the anthro class, and after it there would be dinner, and there would be Randi across a little table wanting every last detail of how the coat had gone — Randi, whose mad gorgeous idea it had been, who had handed her the play like a queen lending a sword. She would tell her everything. Well. She turned her face into his chest, and her mouth curled. Nearly everything. The other thing, the strange small thing — she would have to find a way to say it that didn't sound like what it sounded like. Maybe she'd just say it anyway. Randi would know what to make of it.

Randi always did.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 2: Back — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (back.md) =====

The kitchen was too small for two people to be useful in. She'd tried, the first weeks, standing to dry while he washed, and they'd bumped hips and elbows until he took the towel out of her hand and pointed her at the little table, and that was the arrangement now and she'd stopped pretending she minded. He cooked, they ate at the table because he sat people down to eat, and then he stood at the sink with his sleeves rolled and she sat naked on the lacquered seat of a chair he'd made, with what was left of her wine, and talked at his back. His back was a good listener. All of him was, but the back made it easy, made her run on longer than she meant to, and tonight she'd been running on about Randi for a while.

"—so there's no receipt, obviously, there was never going to be a receipt, and the guy at the counter is looking at her over the sweater like he's already said no. And she doesn't argue. That's the thing. She just keeps on being delighted with him. Like he's the best part of her whole errand. And I watched him fold." Vee pushed a crumb across the table with one finger. "Store credit and a bag. She let him feel like it was his idea."

"Mm." He set a pan in the rack.

"You'd have hated every second of it. There was a line."

"I'd have kept the sweater."

"You'd have worn the sweater out of spite. It was lavender."

He didn't turn around, but she could hear the smile get into his shoulders. He worked the towel around the inside of a pot, hung it, and said, in the same voice he'd have used for a grocery list, "I want to meet your people."

Vee sat up a little.

"The ones you tell me about," he said.

It arrived as *Randi*. Of course she did — she'd been saying Randi's name for ten minutes, Randi was the one she told, and there it was, arriving like a gift with her friend's name already on it. Something went up in her like the good windows in the athletic dining hall, all that gold at once.

"You could meet Randi," she said. "Properly, I mean. You two said about nine words at the coffee shop and most of them were math." She was already building it — a dinner, somewhere with real napkins, her two favorite people at one small table finally getting to find out about each other. "We could do a dinner. The three of us."

"Bring Cassie, too," he said, into the sink.

He said it with a plate in his hand, no more weight on it than the plate had, and it landed in the middle of her little dinner and sat there. Cassie. Vee opened her mouth to say *of course* and didn't. Cassie would come — that was the thing. For Vee she'd come, cap and all, and be a good sport the whole evening. And Vee ran the dinner forward and couldn't find the restaurant to put it in. The real-napkins kind of place, Randi's kind, would seat Cassie in yesterday's shirt under a pendant light, being fine about it for two hours. Anywhere Cassie would pick, Vee couldn't picture Randi's hands on the menu. She went through every table in town, and every one of them had somebody at it being a good sport, and she wasn't going to build a dinner one of her people had to be a good sport at.

She'd watched somebody be fine about it before. She wasn't going to look at when.

"Okay, so not a dinner," she said slowly, thinking with her mouth open, her one method for anything worth planning. "Cassie doesn't — a fancy dinner isn't Cassie's."

"And Meg," Pace said. He was scrubbing something now, in no rush about it. "The one whose boots you wore up the mountain."

Vee stopped with her glass in the air.

She'd said that once. Maybe once. Somewhere on the trail in October with her thighs burning, a half sentence about the boots being Meg's and a half size big, the kind of thing you say to say something, and it had gone wherever those sentences go. Except it hadn't. It had gone home with him and been put somewhere, apparently, on a shelf with her friends' names on it, and he'd just taken it down one-handed without turning around.

"You remember that?" she said.

"Your heel came up out of the right one when you pushed off." He rinsed. "And Kayla's the one who can talk the paint off a wall?"

That was hers. That was her own line, she'd said it about Kayla probably four times, and hearing it handed back in his flat certain voice did something to her that she would have been embarrassed to describe out loud. He had been listening. Not the polite kind, where a man keeps his face pointed at you while he rests. All fall, all those nights she'd run on at his back about her floor, her people, the small dumb news of her life — he'd kept it. All of it.

"She is," Vee said. "She'd talk the wall's paint into telling her things."

"Then it can't be a dinner table," he said. "Tables have ends. Somebody ends up at one."

And the whole thing turned over at once, the small dinner opening out into something with everybody in it — Kayla and Meg, Meg's Danny, Cassie planted happily at the edge of it with her arms crossed, Randi in the middle of them all, and Pace. Her worlds, in one room, at the same time. The laugh started up out of her before she knew it was coming.

"All of them," she said. "You'd do that? That's — you understand what you're volunteering for. Kayla alone is a lot of person."

"I'd like it."

"And Theo, maybe. He's a friend from my English class last year, he—" and the rest of it came out over the little ledge before she could decide about it. "He asked me out once. Last spring. It's fine, it's nothing, we never even—" She stopped there, not sure why she was explaining a thing nobody had asked her to explain.

"Bring Theo," Pace said, and reached for the next pan.

That was all. He never looked around, never asked the question she'd braced for, and she sat there feeling something she couldn't have named settle in her, warm side down.

She was deep into the roster — who'd come, who'd bring whom, whether Kayla could be trusted around the story of the shirt — when the other thing surfaced. It came up as a picture, whole: a game running on three walls and a fourth screen over the bar, the sound pooling in the middle of the room, and Pace across the nachos with his fork in his hand, squaring it to the edge of the table, turning it, squaring it again. *I listen as hard as I know how and I still can't hear you.* The picture didn't want anything from her. It just stood there until she'd seen it.

"Will you pick the place?" she said. "You know the area. I'd just be guessing off which sign looks friendliest."

The water shut off. He dried his hands on the towel, taking his time about it, and when he turned around at last he was leaning back on the counter looking at her, and there was something in his face she'd have called happiness if he'd let it any further out.

"Let me handle it," he said.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 3: Unpacking — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (unpacking.md) =====

The radiators were back on for the semester and overdoing it, the whole floor smelling of hot dust and somebody's microwave popcorn, and Cassie's window stood open its two inches — defended territory, all winter, every winter — so the thin line of cold off the sill found Vee as she came in from the suite bathroom in her robe with her hair up in a towel, and she stood in it a second on purpose, clean and boiled pink. She'd missed it. That was the strange sweetness of the night, that she had missed a draft.

Cassie's duffel sat on the lower bunk half-emptied, white athletic socks in one stack and graphic tees in another, the unpacking suspended somewhere in the middle in favor of the desk chair and the book. She'd driven the last leg through the afternoon, she'd said, and been in the room an hour when Vee came home, and now the room had both of them in it again, both lamps going, and it felt like a thing switched back on at the wall.

"Anything interesting happen this weekend?" Cassie said, to the book.

"Cassie." Vee sat down on the edge of the bean bag, which took the rest of her without being asked. "Cassie."

Cassie set the highlighter down. She turned the chair around, the whole chair, and folded her feet up under her where they never reached the floor anyway, and gave Vee the entire front of her attention.

"We talked," Vee said. "Friday night. I drove out there and we — Cass, we *talked*, and the whole stupid month just came down. Like a tent with the pole pulled. It's — we're—" She gave up steering it and let her face say it instead, which her face had been wanting to do since the door.

"Okay," Cassie said, soft, and waited, which was the question.

"And then I just — stayed. The whole weekend." Vee pulled the towel off and shook her hair down, damp on her shoulders. "He'd set the bread machine going before it was even light. The whole house smelled like a bakery. And he was already gone to the farmers market when I got up — Saturday, the farmers market, in January — and he comes back in with a bag in each arm and stands there in the door and says, 'I got mushrooms.' Like a man reporting from the front."

"Mushrooms."

"On toast. Cassie, I can't explain it. It was the best plate of food I have had in my life and it was mushrooms on toast." She was up on her knees in the bean bag now, hands going. "And he does this thing with the oranges — blood oranges — he takes the whole peel off with a knife, around and down, in strips, so there's no white left anywhere, and then he cuts every section out of its little skin, so what you get is a bowl of just the — the jewels. And he salts the persimmons."

"He salts the persimmons."

"Three grains. 'They do it that way in Japan.'" She had his flat delivery down cold, and Cassie's mouth went sideways at it, which from Cassie was applause.

"Didn't bring a bag?" Cassie said.

"I left it in the car." A beat. "There wasn't anything important in it anyway."

"And your clothes?"

"Eep—" said Vee, and then her mouth set off without her. "No — okay, so Saturday night he did a load of laundry, because that's — he *does* that, he just does things, and my sweater was in it. The new one, the cranberry one. And something happened, the water was too hot or, I don't know, wool, and it came out about the size of a doll's, and the dye went into my good pants, so everything of mine was — and that's why Sunday morning I was in his t-shirt, which comes down to about here on me," a hand at her thigh, "which is why he burned the toast."

"He burned the toast."

"Only the first round."

Cassie considered her from the chair, feet folded, the highlighter turning end over end in one hand, uncapped nothing.

"Pace," she said. "Shrank your sweater."

"To a doll size."

A silence about the length of a held breath. Then Cassie laughed — put her head back and actually laughed, the rare full one she never spent on her own jokes — and Vee went too, helplessly, both hands coming up over her face, the two of them laughing in the overcooked room with the cold line of air coming in over it, and the record stood exactly where Vee had left it, corrected by nobody.

"How was your break?" Vee said, when she could.

"I wrapped presents for four days." Cassie set the highlighter down. "My mother saves boxes. There's a closet. You wrap a thing, and she looks at the wrapping, and goes and gets you a better box." She considered. "We made Christmas cookies. They were pretty. They'd have been better made out of Play-Doh."

"You ate them anyway."

"I had a civic duty." She tipped her head at the ceiling. "My brother-in-law got snowshoes. He wore them in the house."

"In the *house*."

"Scratched the floors from the door to the kitchen. My sister made him take them off, and he stood there holding them like she'd towed his car." A dry pause. "It was all baby otherwise. The baby is fine. The baby is a baby."

Vee laughed into the collar of her robe. Under the laugh she could feel the weekend still on her like sun she'd been out in — a house where the water came down like it meant it, where the mornings smelled of bread and she had moved through the rooms like a dream she was having on purpose. The shower she shared with Cassie and the two girls next door had run its polite trickle tonight, three kinds of conditioner crowding the little ledge and Cassie's shampoo-and-conditioner-in-one, a suitemate's towel on the hook and the toilet an arm's reach past the curtain, and she'd stood in it under water that was simply less — less pressure, less private, less hers — and been happy in it anyway, happy the whole time. The room went on around her the same, ordinary, in no hurry, asking nothing — the stacks of white socks, the ticking heat, the old tea rings on the desk. She was reaching for the towel where it had slid when Cassie said, in the same register the snowshoes had gotten, not looking over:

"My mother says thank you for the card."

Vee's hand stopped partway to the towel and went, without her, to the collar of her robe instead.

"She wants to know when she gets to see you again."

"Oh," Vee said, and heard how small it came out, and didn't fix it. Something had gone through her middle that she had no intention of examining tonight, warm and specific, like a hand cupped briefly at the back of her neck. "Tell her — I don't know. Spring break. Sometime. Tell her I said hi." Which was not the size of it at all, and Cassie, who could hear the size of things, only nodded and recapped the highlighter, done with the subject the exact moment before it would have needed anything from either of them.

She went back to her book. Vee traded the robe for the flannel shirt and brought the collar up over her nose on the way into it — him still, but going faint — and decided, buttoning, that he would just have to wear it for her this weekend. She climbed the ladder to the top bunk and got under the covers. The bunk was exactly one girl wide, and when her arm went out across the covers it found the cold painted cinderblock a hand's width off and stopped there, flat against it — two nights of all that acreage, the heat of him at her back, and now this. She left the hand on the wall, and the wall was only a wall: he was out there west of town in the wide bed, hers again, and the week would go and Friday would come. Below her a page turned; the radiator ticked its way down; the window let its thin cold line in, and she was asleep before she decided to be.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 4: Across — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (across.md) =====

The dining hall doors were locked, and taped to the inside of the glass was a sheet of printer paper with the holiday hours on it, which were that there weren't any.

"No," Kayla said to the paper. She tried the door again, as if the paper might be lying. "No. They can't. It's a *Monday*." She turned around to the two of them with her whole face fallen in, curls swinging, one mittened hand still on the handle like a woman at a deathbed. "I have been thinking about waffles since I woke up. I planned my *morning* around it. I didn't eat anything so I'd be hungry, and now I'm so hungry, and there's nothing, there's nowhere, we're going to have to eat granola bars over the sink like animals—"

"There's the vending machines in Slusher," Meg said.

"Meg." Kayla put both hands over her heart. "I am not having break*fast* out of a machine. It's the last day. Classes start tomorrow. This is the last morning of my life and it's going to be peanut butter crackers."

The campus behind them had that refilled, unstarted feeling, everybody's cars back in the lots and nobody with anywhere to be until tomorrow, the cold bright and clean. Vee looked at Kayla, hungry and bereft in her mittens, and felt the morning organize itself.

"Get in my car," she said. "I know a place. Let's get something special — first breakfast back." And then, before either of them could start walking: "It's a little expensive. Is that okay?"

Kayla wanted to know if they had waffles. They had waffles.

The place had a sun porch along its south side, a long room of glass and white-painted muntins where the late-morning light came in and lay on everything without asking — the cloths, the people, the coffee cups — and the whole room was warm and loud and smelled of butter. The girl at the front took one look at the three of them and got three menus off the stack, and led them back into the light, and Kayla talked the entire length of the room — to Vee, to Meg, to the room itself, *look at this, oh my God, look at it* — and they got a table against the glass, and the sun came onto the cloth and sat down with them.

Meg settled into her chair, looked down the porch one whole slow time, and delivered her verdict. "Okay. This is a place."

The menu was a single heavy card. Vee knew what things cost here, and read the right side of hers anyway, out of old habit, and put it down; there was Christmas money in her purse that had been waiting for an occasion, and this was one, and she didn't do any arithmetic at all. They ordered coffees all around, and then Vee said, since it was an occasion, "Mimosas?" — and Meg said sure before the question mark had landed, and Kayla, who was not twenty-one, ordered a fresh orange juice, mournful about it, and when the flutes came cold and beading hers came in a flute too, which repaired her feelings completely. She leaned into the table and opened fire.

"Okay," she said. "Friday. The shoes."

"What shoes," Vee said, into her coffee.

"What *shoes.* The shoes, Vee. Friday night, the coat, the *heels* — I'm standing there holding my laundry like an idiot and you go by dressed to kill somebody specific, and I say don't do anything I wouldn't do, and then *nothing.* All weekend, nothing. Three days." She sat back. "I have been so patient. Ask Meg how patient I've been."

"She has been unbearable," Meg said.

"So?" Kayla's whole face was open, delighted in advance. "Tell it. All of it. Start with the shoes."

So Vee told it. They'd had a fight before break, she and Pace — a bad one, and then a month of not talking — and she'd decided she wasn't doing that anymore, any of it, and Friday night she'd driven out to his house without telling him she was coming. She laid it out with her hands: the county road, the dark, the porch light coming on. And him opening the door — and she gave them his voice, flat and level, the courtesy in it. "'It's late. Is everything alright?'"

"Stop," Kayla said. "In the doorway. What did you say."

"Nothing." Vee took a slow sip of her mimosa and set the flute down. "I flashed him on his front porch."

The porch took the noise fine; it was a loud room. Kayla's shriek turned three heads and she didn't notice, both hands up, actually rising an inch off her chair — "You did *not*. In *January* — Vee. VEE. In front of *God* — no wonder you had the coat buttoned up to your — you were standing there *talking to me about my socks*—" and she was off, three sentences downstream and going, doing the scene from her own side now, the laundry basket, the socks, herself narrating underwear to a woman on her way to commit a crime, and Vee laughed along and let her run, and never had to say one word about the drive.

Then Kayla came down off it, leaned in over her flute, and asked the real one, lower. "And did he...?"

Vee smiled hugely, and nodded. "Yeah..."

Meg waited for that to settle. Then she lifted her flute. "And it worked."

"It worked," Vee said, and the three flutes came together over the middle of the table, Kayla's orange juice in with the rest. "We talked all night. It's — we're good. We're better than we were." Which was the size of it she'd brought, and it sat on the table looking exactly true.

Meg nodded slowly, level as ever. "That's a big apology."

"It was a big fight."

"No, I respect it. I do." She had a sip of her mimosa. "In October I had the worst period of my life and I was a hag to Danny for five days. Like, hall of fame. He brought me a heating pad and I told him the cord was stupid." A pause for that to be appreciated; it was. "And he was sweet the whole time. Never once took the bait. So when it was over I got him his favorite — maple bacon, from that place he likes, a whole dozen — and sat through all the Matrix movies with him. All of them. Again." She picked her cup back up. "So I get it. Sometimes you have to make it right in their language."

"Danny would have rather you borrowed Vee's high heels," Kayla said, to giggles all around, Meg's included.

"The maple bacon was a gesture."

The waffles came, then, and the egg things, and for a while the talk went where it wanted — classes tomorrow, whose schedule was a war crime, whether the bookstore line would be worse today or Wednesday — and the light lay across the table and moved, and Vee sat in the middle of it eating her eggs, her people on either side loud and easy, the last morning of break turning out to have been rescued after all.

She waited for a lull, and didn't get one, and told them anyway, over the top of one.

"So — Wednesday night. He's taking us all to dinner. Everybody. You two, Cassie, and Randi, Theo maybe. He wants to meet you."

The fork stopped halfway to Kayla's mouth.

"Meet us," she said. "Pace. Dinner. Wednesday — that's two days, Vee, that is two *days* — okay. Okay okay okay. I'm coming to your room Tuesday, no, tonight, we have to figure out — I need to know what this dinner *is*, is it a restaurant restaurant, what are we — Meg, we're doing hair Tuesday—"

"Where's he taking us?" Meg said.

"He's handling it," Vee said, and heard how much she liked saying it. "He's got the place. It's his treat, all of it — he was very clear."

"Meet the boyfriend," Meg said, and let herself smile. "Wednesday works."

Kayla had already left Wednesday and was somewhere out ahead of it, planning in the unbroken stream, and the two of them let her run and finished the coffee, and when the check came Vee asked for it split three ways, and the server brought the little terminal around the table and they tapped their phones to it one after another, deliberating the tip buttons out loud, nobody letting anybody cover anybody, and the girl at the front told them to come back.

Then they were out on the sidewalk in the cold, the three of them squinting, the porch behind them a long lit box of glass with its light and its people and its butter smell shut up warm inside it, and Kayla was still going — Tuesday, hair, what does one wear to meet a man who owns a *house* — and they walked to the car through the bright empty holiday morning, and the cold wasn't anything, and tomorrow the semester would start, and Wednesday was two days off, and Vee had the middle of it all the whole time.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 5: Covering — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (covering.md) =====

Her body swayed dangerously at the little burst of speed, and Vee made herself slow down. The new bra was doing a better job of showing her breasts off than of controlling them — pretty rather than practical, like everything from the after-Christmas sales, and she had known it when she hooked it on. She wasn't late. The opposite: she was early, only not as early as she'd meant to be, because she had spent more time dressing and redressing than any first day of class could justify. The cream wrap blouse had won in the end — the one with the blue flowers gone soft at their edges, as if someone had painted them wet — and she had tied and retied the sash until the knot sat right at her hip, tails hanging loose. Randi was always glamorous, always beautiful, and Vee was not going to sit across from that in her Tuesday clothes.

She had skipped the dining hall without thinking twice — confident, without any discussion, that there would be dinner after, that Randi would hear her stories over a table somewhere. Randi always made time for Vee.

She posted herself outside the classroom door with eight minutes in hand — floor wax and radiators, full dark already on the Drillfield behind her — and watched the corridor for Randi. She was running on a deficit of their hugs, the loss accumulated over the small weeks of break that had dripped by — paid against just once, and she'd cried through that one — and there was gossip besides that wouldn't keep until dinner, and then they'd walk in and pick their spot together. First day was when you chose your seat for the semester and held it; that was the convention, and if Dr. Marsh was anything like her reputation, the right seats would matter. Students funneled past her in ones and twos, chins still down from the cold. No Randi. At two minutes to seven she gave up the door and went in — and Randi was already there.

Back third of the room, aisle seat, her coat folded over the chair beside her like a reserved sign, a pen already out. Her ponytail was tied off tonight in the green-and-gold silk scarf — *the* scarf, the one Randi had looped at Vee's bare throat in October and taught her the knot of, the one that had gone along to say sorry and had worked — and Vee was absurdly glad at the sight of it, without asking herself why. Randi had beaten her by who knew how long. She looked up and found her at once — she always found her at once — and the smile that came was the one that came before anything was arranged on it.

"Good to see you, girl." Randi's hand caught hers as she dropped into the held seat and squeezed, once, warm, and Vee squeezed back, and there was time for exactly that much, because the door at the front opened and the professor walked in.

Dr. Marsh was tall, of no age Vee could have sworn to, and built like a marathon — long and spare and efficient, nothing carried that wasn't needed. She was dressed for a hike that had been out of style for half a generation, if it had ever been a style: zip-pocketed trousers, a fleece vest over a checked shirt, boots that had seen actual ground. Her hair was short and silver with a single black streak through the bangs, and she moved like a woman with somewhere better to be who had decided this was it.

She put one canvas bag on the desk, did not open it, and faced the room.

"Before the syllabus," she said, "a question.

"A hundred years ago Marcel Mauss published an essay on gifts. He'd been reading the ethnographers who worked among the Māori, and the Māori had told them something he never got over. A gift carries what they called *hau* — the spirit of the one who gave it. And the hau wants to go home. The thing you were given is inhabited, and it pulls back toward its giver, and that pull is why a gift can never really be free.

"You're all modern people. You're free to file that under mysticism." She let a beat go by. "But then explain to me why you can't regift the sweater your grandmother knitted. Explain why a woman selling her wedding ring feels like she's selling a person. There's no law. There's no reasoning to it you could put on a page. And you're held anyway. Something is riding inside the object." She looked around the room once, quick, taking a kind of attendance, rocking slightly forward on the boots as if the lecture were a trailhead. "By the end of this semester, you'll be able to tell me what it is."

She did not tell them. She picked up the chalk and wrote her office hours instead, and Vee, who had come in braced for a syllabus voice, sat forward without noticing she'd done it and stayed that way for an hour and twenty minutes. Beside her Randi's pen moved and stopped and moved. Neither of them raised a hand, and it was ten past eight before Vee looked at the clock the first time.

The restaurant was new that fall — modern Italian, three blocks off campus, stretching itself a little above student budgets — Pace's suggestion, offered the moment Vee asked; he knew restaurants. Where the newness could have felt off-putting, it felt instead ready. The narrow old building made the most of itself: tables in the basement, tables on the ground floor, and a loft up a steep little stair, under the rafters. Just inside the door stood a jukebox, chrome and black, with clear pipes curving up both sides — water in the pipes, a thin stream of bubbles climbing through them, lit green from within. It was playing something with an accordion in it, quietly, to nobody.

"Up," Randi said to the girl at the front, and they went up, and got the corner booth in the loft with the whole warm room below them.

They shed their coats. Vee hung hers on the hook at the end of the booth and turned it, carefully, lining-to-the-wall as she did — the good olive coat, buttoned all the way down tonight, over the cream wrap. The wrap crossed low, the neckline went further south than any note-taking required, and the loose knot at her hip was the only thing on her tonight doing any controlling.

Randi took one look and put her hand to her heart.

"Okay. *That.*" Her eyes went down it and came back up, frank and delighted. "That's new. That's a neckline with somewhere to be. Turn—" the little finger-twirl, "—just let me stare at your hotness."

Vee laughed, delighted, turning.

Randi was in oatmeal cashmere worn open over cream lace, her everyday studs, the green and gold riding above it all in her black hair, and it took no thought at all to see that everything on her was quietly, seriously good, the same as ever — new, maybe, some of it, from wherever her break had been, and none of it asking to be noticed. Randi ordered a bottle of red for the table — the conversation would need it, she said — without looking at the price. The wine came, and the glasses were filled, and Randi folded her arms on the table and leaned in with the look of a woman owed a story.

"That text nearly killed me," she said. "I hear nothing all weekend, then three lines Sunday night. Start at the beginning."

So Vee started at the door — at the door itself, standing in the cold on his dark porch, none of the rest of it, because the rest of it was not the story she was telling. "He opened it and gave me the polite voice. *'It's late. Is everything alright?'*" — and Randi nodded once, slow, because the wall was in the plan; they had talked about the wall. "And I didn't say one word. I showed him."

"Hmm," Randi said.

"And the wall came down. All of it, all at once — you could watch it go. And he took me inside."

"Hmm."

That was the line she'd saved — she'd said it to the windshield twice on the drive, *you could watch it go* — and it bought her a Hmm. Vee's smile stayed up. It just stopped being attached to anything.

Randi reached over and laid her hand flat on Vee — high on her chest, that middle ground between collarbone and breast, the whole palm warm through the new silk.

"This did it," she said, warm as a verdict — and on the way back her fingers set the crossing edge of the wrap a quarter-inch truer, and were gone.

Vee didn't linger on the fight or the month of silence — Randi had had all five weeks of that already, had held her through the worst hour of it, and there was no bringing that to this table. What she wanted to give her was midnight.

"And at midnight he made me pancakes. From scratch — flour and the whisk, butter going glassy on top of the stack, tea he'd made me without my asking. At midnight." She was two sips into the second glass and the kitchen was arriving whole. "And I sat there and ate them wearing—" she lifted one hand and turned it over, showing the nothing in it, "—the blanket. And the heels. He said the heels stayed on."

Saying it lit her face from underneath; she felt the heat arrive and kept going anyway. "And then he came around the counter and got down on one knee, next to my chair, while my mouth was still full, and said he'd missed me. And his voice—" She stopped and had some ice water. "His voice didn't make it all the way through the sentence."

"Midnight pancakes," Randi said, soft, a hand coming to her heart, and she meant it. "I'm so happy for you, girl." She was smiling at Vee over the top of her wine glass. One fingernail was working the edge of it, the rim's thin edge under the nail, back and forth.

"On his knee," she said. "On the floor."

"On the floor."

"You're going to be insufferable all spring." It came out fond, entirely fond, and she set the glass down and topped up Vee's without asking.

The talking went easy after that, the way it went when the two of them had a table and nowhere to be — and twice a week now there would be this, class letting them out into the cold at 8:20 with the evening legitimate and paid for.

"A boy went down on one knee for me once," Randi said. "It's cheating. It ought to be illegal."

"Tell me."

"Summer. My parents had rented a house at the shore — this was the era of renting, we were trying the whole thing on; it came with a maid, which my mother adored — and there was a boy who did the lawns. And we'd been looking at each other for weeks. Chatting over the hedge, very costume drama. And finally he asked me out, and I was charmed, and I was enjoying being asked — taking my time with it, you understand — and while I'm standing there enjoying it, he goes down on one knee. On the grass. And asks me again from down there." She spread her hands: *what is a girl supposed to do.* "You can't say no to that. It isn't physically possible."

"Where did he take you?"

"The boardwalk fair. We rode the merry-go-round. He won me a teddy bear — which I'm sure cost him three times what the bear cost — and he was unbelievably sweet, and it worked on me. Completely."

"Oh, I love him already." Vee had both hands around her glass. "I see why you went out with him. What happened with him?"

Randi looked at her for a second. Then she laughed — one note lower than her usual laugh — and this time she topped up her own glass.

"Well," she said. "Since you ask."

"A few weeks in, my parents were out for the day — gone, hours — and my room had a bed I'd have had to fold him in half to use. And down the hall was the good bedroom. Ocean view. A bed with guest parking." She was doing it with her hands now, laying out the house in the air. "So we used it. Obviously. First time — first time as an *us*, I mean. And afterward I showered and I dressed and I floated around the rest of the day being extremely pleased with myself, and at no point — at *no point* — did I count my underwear."

Vee's hand had come up over her mouth. "No."

"The maid did the beds every second day," Randi said. "Except she didn't, that day, because it was her short day, which I'd have known if I'd ever once looked at the little schedule on the refrigerator. So they were in there. In the good bed. My parents' bed." She said it lightly, brightly, and drank, and the shape of the story stood there for a second, ready to be laughed at.

Vee was wincing for her, half laughing, both hands still around her glass. "Did they find them?"

"They found them."

Two words, still bright — but the fun had gone out of the brightness somewhere, and Vee heard it go. She wasn't laughing anymore; she was aghast, aghast on her behalf, the parents, the bed —

"How did they *tell* you they'd found them?"

Randi's glass stopped an inch off the table.

"They didn't," she said. "Tell me." And then, after a beat that had nothing bright in it, she went on, and her voice had come down out of the story's key without her seeming to decide it. "The boy got invited to Sunday dinner. Which I took as a good sign. We're at the table, all four of us, and it's fine, he's doing fine, and my mother goes out to the kitchen and comes back in and sets them down next to my plate."

The glass was still in her hand. She was pushing the rim's thin edge in under her fingernail, slow, not looking at it.

"Laundered. Folded. Robin's-egg blue." The color was climbing her neck, up out of the oatmeal cashmere, and she wasn't performing anything now, she was only looking at a point just past Vee's shoulder where a dining room evidently stood. "And she says, 'They're not mine. They're your color.'"

"Randi."

"She'd had them washed," Randi said. "That's the part. She'd had them for *days.* She waited for him." She looked down at her own hand and appeared to find the wine glass in it and set it down. "My color. She knew my color."

The loft went on being warm around them, plates somewhere below, the accordion faint under everything.

"What did you say?" Vee said, soft.

"Nothing." One word, flat as a dropped card.

"And he sat there," Randi said. "Through the whole rest of dinner. This boy who'd gone down on one knee in the grass — he sat across from my mother and he barely ate. Pushed things around. Answered when spoken to." She turned her glass by the stem, once. "He quit that week. Found other lawns, I suppose. I never saw him again."

The server appeared at the top of the loft stairs and asked about dessert. "We're going to need a while," Randi said, and smiled him back down — and by the time she turned around the memory was gone and something warmer had come up in its place, aimed at Vee.

"How late did you sit there?" she said. "Wrapped in the blanket, your man down on his knees because he's too happy that you're back to talk. How late?"

Hearing it said in Randi's voice brought it back whole — she had cried that night, happy tears, the pride and the happiness all one thing — and her throat closed on it now, one beat, without her permission. Then she smiled. "I was a bit stuck."

"What do you mean? Did something go wrong?"

"Not exactly. More like a mistake went right."

"Do tell."

"I was so nervous driving out that I forgot to bring an overnight bag."

"Wearing your man's clothes can be sexy."

Vee's eyes went sideways as she smiled.

Randi sat up. "You *didn't* wear your man's clothes all weekend."

"Not a stitch."

"Oh, *now*." Randi leaned in, and her eyes had their wickedness back. "You gave me the after-school-special version. Back up and tell me what really happened. And don't start at his door. That's not where a night like that starts." She reached for the bottle and poured for them both, and settled in.

So Vee went back — all the way back, to her dorm room at seven o'clock — and told it the way it had actually gone.

"I'm getting ready," she said. "I've got the underwear drawer open, and a hand on the black everyday bra, and I catch myself — no. Pace deserves the pretty one. So I pick up the good lace instead. And then I'm standing there holding it, and I start laughing, because he isn't getting that one either. He isn't getting any of them." She turned her glass by the stem. "I put it back and I shut the drawer on all of it. Every neat little row of everything that wasn't invited."

"Oh, I *like* you," Randi said.

"And that's getting dressed. I'm done before I've started. So I sit down on the bed to do the shoes, and I think — a girl puts her shoes on last. Always. Last thing, at the door, keys in your hand. And there I am, zipping them on first." She held up one finger. "First. And only."

"Which shoes."

"Your shoes."

Randi put both hands flat on the table, quietly triumphant, and said nothing at all.

"And then the coat over — nothing. Buttoned to the collar. Decent to the knee. It's the coat I wear to church, Randi. I've worn it to meet my mother."

"Stop, I'm dying. The drive."

"Twenty minutes of county road, in the dark, in January, in a coat and two shoes that mean business. And I was very ready to meet Pace."

Vee looked down at her lap. She took a big drink of her wine, and a breath — and then she reached back and turned the coat around on its hook — the dark bloom on the pale satin, Friday's, held out into the light — and showed Randi the lining.

"Very. Ready."

Randi's eyes were very dark and liquid, staring. Vee could hear her breathing.

"Wow," Randi said finally, hoarse and low. And kept staring.

"And…" Vee said, letting the coat go, "…and somewhere past the last of the lights, there were lights. Behind me. Blue and red — the whole ceiling of the car going blue and red — and one whoop of the siren."

"*No.*"

"And every thought in my head was one word. Caught. Nothing under the coat, twenty minutes of — very ready — all over me, and a man with a flashlight about to want my license and registration."

"*God.*" Randi had Vee's wrist across the table without seeming to have reached for it. "What did he—"

"He went past. He was never for me. Over a rise and gone." She let a breath out, the same one she'd let out on the gravel that night. "I pulled over anyway. Sat there shaking with both hands on the wheel. And the scare didn't take one thing off it. It wound it tighter."

Randi said nothing for a moment. Then she tipped her glass an inch toward her. "And then his door."

"Then his door. The porch light was off. Nobody was expecting me. There was music going somewhere back in the house — low, this slow sad horn — and for one second I thought *someone's there*, and then I knew better. That's just what the house sounds like when I'm not in it."

"And he gave you the polite voice."

"That part was true. *'It's late. Is everything alright?'* And I didn't answer him. I took the two edges of the coat—" her hands did it in the air, small, "—and I opened it. January, on his porch. All of me."

"And the wall came down."

"The wall didn't just come down. It *crumbled*." Vee's chin was up; under the table her knees had pressed themselves together. "He looked at me like a starving man who'd just been told he's forgiven. And then he was kissing me. And then he was kissing lower."

"Outside? In January?"

"He took me over right there." Vee's voice had gone quiet. "With his mouth."

"This is *so good*," Randi said, to nobody in particular.

"And then he was inside me. On the porch. He carried me in like that."

"While you were—"

"Yes." There was no apology anywhere in it. "We didn't make it past the couch."

"You'd make Mae West blush."

Randi topped up both glasses, unhurried, like a woman banking a fire. "Now the morning," she said. "You woke up where?"

"Alone." So Vee told her Saturday. And it was easy to tell, was the strange thing, easier than it had any business being — the empty bed and the house full of the smell of bread, the sweats folded at the foot of the bed that she had looked at and left there.

"I took a hot shower. He keeps that house toasty warm when I'm there — I'm pretty sure it's so I'll wear less. Well." She turned a palm up: exhibit A. "This time it worked better than ever. I'm standing in his house naked, waiting for him to come home, and I think — *a woman kept naked in a man's house, his for the taking. A pleasure slave.*"

"Vee, the pleasure slave." Randi giggled. "It has a ring to it."

"That's what I thought. For about four steps. And then I start counting." And she counted it off on her fingers, for Randi to see, exactly as she'd counted it that morning. "He brings me off first — always. He gets up in the dark to set bread going. He cooks. He cleans. He was out in the January cold that very minute buying food to feed me. So I stop, in the middle of his hallway, bare as the day I was born, and I think: *some slave.* If anybody in that house is a pleasure slave, it is not me. I'm—" and she gave the little shimmy, right there in the booth, and it did what it had done in the hallway, "—a pleasure *princess.* Not a woman kept for a man's pleasure. A woman a man keeps *in* pleasure."

Vee preened. Randi squealed and thumped both hands on the table, and somewhere below them heads turned, and neither of them cared.

"That's going on your crown."

"And then he came home from the market and I was arranged at his counter wearing a scrunchie," Vee said, "and he stood in the door with a bag in each arm and went — " she did the level voice — " 'I got mushrooms.'"

Randi put her head back against the booth and laughed the real laugh, the one that got all the way out before she could dress it, and Vee ran on downhill into the breakfast: the apron, the blood oranges peeled down to jewels, the persimmons with their three grains of salt, and herself at the table making a project of being impossible not to look at — the arms up, the foot on the chair edge — and the first thread of smoke going up off the skillet.

"You burned his toast."

"*He* burned the toast. I was sitting perfectly still."

"You were a public hazard."

"I offered to put the sweats on. He said no so fast he stepped on the end of my sentence." Vee was flushed and laughing and a long way from campus, the morning's whole education assembling itself again in the retelling.

"Go back," Randi said. Her chin was in her hand. "The foot on the chair. You checked your toes — with the man right there at the stove?" Her eyes had their wickedness in them, patient. "Paint it for me. What exactly was he not looking at?"

"So I set my heel up on the chair edge," Vee said. "Knee up. And I bend over my own bare leg and I study the frosted plum like there is nothing else on my mind anywhere in the world. Toes pointed. Taking my time over every one of them. And behind me the skillet goes quiet."

Somewhere in the painting the heat had stopped being remembered and was simply there, low and insistent under the silk.

Randi reached across the table and took her hand.

"How much could he see?"

"Everything."

"Could he see you were ready?"

A breath.

"Yes," Vee said, quietly — and the part after breakfast was next, already arriving, and she needed a minute before she could say any of it.

"Hold that thought," she said, and went down the little stair with her pulse in odd places.

The stall had a proper latch, and she threw it.

The light down here was the same hard blue as every restroom light, a light that found everything and forgave none of it, and she had stopped caring somewhere on the stairs. She didn't sit. She set one palm flat against the cold partition and slid the other in past her waistband, down, and found herself soaked — of course, always, her body a full course ahead of her permission — and there was nothing to do about the shame of that but use it.

She found the small needy knot of herself and circled it, slow once, and then not slow. The weekend came with her hand: the porch and the cold and the two edges of the coat going open; the chair edge under her heel and the skillet going quiet behind her; the build came fast and mean, a whole hour of tablecloth underneath it. Her breath went to pieces against the partition. And at the top, right where the drop began, Randi's face arrived behind her eyes — the tonight face, eyes dark and liquid, asking *how much could he see* — and the question changed as it asked itself, stopped being a question at all: *He could see you were ready. There's no way he couldn't.* Her hand ran on. The face said it again — *there's no way he couldn't* — and Vee went over hard, teeth set, knees dipping, the cry swallowed down to one long breath through her nose, her whole body clenching and pulsing around nothing, her palm sliding half an inch down the cold laminate.

She stood still after, hand stilled, forehead nearly to the partition, letting her ears clear, the last pulses walking themselves out under her fingers. Because that had been about Pace, obviously. All of it. About Pace.

The third restroom she'd touched herself in — she counted the practice room; of course she counted the practice room. At the sink she ran the tap cold and held her wrists under it, and put herself back together, and promised the girl in the mirror — who was flushed, and glossy-eyed, and did not look the least bit sorry — that next time she would control herself.

The loft had changed while she was gone. The lights over the basement stair were off, the room below half empty, a boy in an apron setting chairs seat-down onto tables along the far wall with the practiced apology of a place that would like to go home, and the check was already on the table in its little tray. Randi was pouring the last of the wine into Vee's glass.

"Things were really heated," Randi said. "It's a good thing you got relief."

Vee's whole face stopped.

The heat fell out of it all at once, everywhere, and the loft tilted a degree and stayed there. *She knows.* It arrived the way the blue lights had — whole, certain, out of nowhere: quick hadn't mattered, quiet hadn't mattered, she had come back up the little stair put together and glossy-eyed into a woman who could read her through a floor, and Randi had just said it out loud. At a table. In a restaurant. With a boy stacking chairs twenty feet below.

"After driving out to see Pace." Randi held her eyes, pleasant as a hostess. "He took care of you, but good. Tell me I'm wrong."

The floor came back under her one board at a time.

"You're not wrong," Vee said, and drank the wine.

It was ten o'clock. They had shut the place down on a school night, on the first school night there was. They split the check at the little terminal, and went down the steep stair with their coats on and out past the jukebox, its green water still climbing its pipes for nobody, into cold that took the breath and gave it back sharpened.

The sidewalk was empty. Streetlight, their breath in it. Tuesday and Thursday, this, all spring — neither of them said it, and it stood there between them anyway, settled, a done thing nobody had ever decided.

"Tomorrow," Randi said, like a present she was allowed to shake. "I can't wait to properly meet Pace. You're gone for that boy." Vee glowed, and didn't argue it. "After all you've told me, I want to see him again."

"Come here," Randi said. "I'm proud of you. You got what you wanted." And she kissed her goodbye, as they did — the mouth, soft, in no hurry, the small drawing-back and then the second press with its little more in it — and then didn't quite let go, and the hug went on inside both their coats, warm in the cold, the bitter-orange of her rising off her collar. And against Vee's ear Randi giggled.

"What?"

"When you came to my room after Christmas," Randi said, "you cried so much you made my neck wet. Right—" she brought a hand up between them and pointed, awkward, elbow pinned by the hug, at the side of her own neck, "—here."

"I'm sorry for making a mess," Vee said, and without any thought at all she bent and put a light kiss on the soft, warm skin where Randi pointed.

Randi flinched.

"—*tickles*," she said, laughing, a hand coming up to the spot and rubbing it, "I'm ticklish, girl, don't you dare weaponize that. Go home before we freeze," and she squeezed Vee's arm through the coat sleeve and turned her loose.

"Thursday," Vee said.

"It's a date."

The walk back was six minutes with the wind behind her. Vee kept her hands in her pockets and the collar of the good coat up and went along through the lamplight with the evening loose and warm in her still, the wine, the loft, Randi's face at *he could see you were ready* — and, arriving in its own time, the small flat fact that the closing had gotten her out of it. The part after breakfast had been next. It had been in her mouth. And the chairs had gone up on the tables and it had not happened, and somewhere under her breastbone was a little unweighable stone of relief at that, which made no sense, because it was nothing, it was a funny story about a kiss that had gone sideways, she'd have told it in thirty seconds. She'd tell it Thursday.

She had told Randi everything since October. She would tell her this too, obviously. Thursday.

Vee knew it was going to be a very good semester.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 6: Among Friends — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (among-friends.md) =====

The place was twenty minutes out on a road she'd never had a reason to take, and from the parking lot it was nothing — blacktop, a low long building gone the color that wood goes when nobody paints it, a sign she couldn't read until they were nearly under it. She had the address memorized from handing it out to everybody twice. *Where even is that,* Kayla had said, thrilled, and Vee had said, *no idea, Pace picked it,* and defended the place like a lawyer, sight unseen, on no evidence at all. They'd come out half an hour ahead of everybody, because it was theirs — his name on the reservation, her people driving twenty minutes into the dark on their say-so — and the lot was thin — a few trucks scattered toward the far end, and one car parked square to the door, and Vee knew it before she was done looking at it. Twenty minutes out, on a road with no reason on it, and Randi had found the place first.

Inside, it was pine. Every wall of it, boards gone a deep honey color that fifty years of other people's good nights had rubbed matte, and the whole room was warm with it, warmer than paneling had any right to be. A tile floor, boot heels and chair legs on it, a speaker somewhere up in a corner playing somebody's steel guitar at the volume of a conversation. No screens — a fact that went by like a room's ceiling height, half a second, gone. Along the back wall a buffet ran under heat lamps — brisket, chicken, corn, green beans — and at the cold end, slaw and a tub of potato salad — and to the side, three round tables had been pushed loosely together, and on one of them, tented, a folded sheet of white butcher paper said VEE — three letters, black marker.

And behind the sign, already on her feet, was Randi. Of course she was. She beat everyone to Vee's things, always had, arrived at them first and fully, a standard other people's RSVPs could only wave at — and here she was, first even ahead of the hosts, at the chair that faced the door, a cold beer already sweating in front of her like she'd been drinking beer all her life, and she'd gotten the room almost exactly right — a chambray shirt with the sleeves cuffed back, the blue doing what blue always did to her eyes, slacks, and then the shoes, little heels a full register too nice for the tile, because there was a floor below which Randi's closet did not go. A few tables across the room were at their dinners and their beers, and none of them looked up, and Vee laughed out loud anyway.

"You're impossible. We came early. We wanted to be here first."

"You did. It didn't work." Randi came around the table already smiling.

Vee had thought she might have to do something tonight. Manage a seam somewhere. She'd carried the thought in with her like a folded map. And here was the room warm and easy, her name on butcher paper, her best friend first through a door she herself had just walked through — and the only thing left to manage was the thing she'd been saving, the thing she wanted done first, before a single coat came through that door.

"Come here. Come meet him. You've technically met him, but that was ninety seconds of standard deviation, it doesn't count." She was pulling her along, laughing. "Randi, this is Pace."

It came out short even as she said it. *This is Pace.* Like he was a lamp. There was more to say and she hadn't quite had the standing to say it, and in the little gap her sentence left, Pace put out his hand.

"Vee's boyfriend," he said.

He said it the plain way he said everything, a man supplying a fact, at a volume for three, and it went through Vee like the first swallow of something hot. There it was. Out loud, in a public room, in front of the one person she told everything to — a word he had never once needed anywhere, spent here, on her. Randi's hand closed hard around hers, hard enough to feel the bones, and Vee squeezed back just as hard, because of course — she got it, she was getting to stand right next to her best friend at the exact moment the best friend heard it confirmed, and if the grip was fierce, well, so was being happy for somebody.

"The boyfriend," Randi said, taking his hand, her voice all warm coin. "Finally. She hides you very well."

"Not anymore," he said, and pulled out her chair.

And that, Vee understood a little later, had been everything she'd braced for, done and dusted before a single guest arrived. The folded map went wherever unneeded things go. The night stood open in front of her with nothing left in it to manage.

Cassie's SUV announced itself from the lot, suspension first, and then the door brought them all in at once — Kayla, Meg, Danny, Cassie last with the keys, coats coming off into a pile, Kayla narrating the buffet to people standing next to the buffet.
