"It says *Vee*," Kayla said, to the room, to everyone, already halfway to the brisket.

Theo came alone, ten minutes behind, with the entrance of a man who'd checked the address twice and sat in his car a minute, and by the time he was through the door the room had a running start on him.

They came to the tables in waves off the buffet, and Pace met each wave the same. Kayla arrived with a loaded plate and her free hand already out — "Vee's boyfriend," he said, shaking it. He said it to Meg, and to Danny at Meg's shoulder. He said it to Theo, flat and friendly, and to Cassie, who shook on it once, like closing a deal. The same two words every time, a man with exactly one credential and no plans to produce another, and by the fourth telling the tables were grinning ahead of it, waiting for it, and he never varied it and never once seemed to notice he'd become the night's running joke. Vee heard it every single time. It landed somewhere low every single time. At the buffet, alone with the brisket, she caught herself saying it under her breath, trying it on with her own mouth, and made herself stop before anybody saw.

Dinner organized itself around the buffet, which meant it never organized at all, and Vee watched it not organize with a pleasure she couldn't have explained. Nobody was seated. Everybody was seated, but a different everybody every twenty minutes — the buffet kept dealing them out and gathering them back in new arrangements, Kayla returning with a second pass at the brisket and a fresh audience, Meg and Danny splitting a plate of corn with the calm of the long-settled. And through it Pace moved. That was almost all he did, that she could ever have pointed to — he moved, between the tables, with a pitcher or without one, and wherever he stopped, something got easier.

The buffet dealt her out a while, and then it dealt her back in, and for a stretch she just sat — her plate in front of her, Pace on the far side of the table, and the room's currents washing people up to it. Danny arrived first, with brisket and the look of a man who'd been working up to something all night. He'd heard it through Meg, who could only have heard it through her: that Pace lifted. He said the word *powerlifting* like a man handling somebody else's tools, and asked what Pace's numbers were, and Pace said, "How's your bench progressing?"

Five words. Vee watched them work. Danny sat down like the chair had been pulled out for him and was off — the one-rep max in the fall, the plateau, the grip change that broke it, all of it delivered to a man who leaned in on his forearms and asked the next question, and the next, and never once produced a number of his own. By the time Danny got to creatine — lowering his voice, like it was a medical matter, which apparently it was — he'd been talking for ten minutes and would have sworn they'd had a conversation about lifting. She never did learn what Pace benched. Neither did Danny.

Theo washed up to the table on the next current, shoulders still up around his ears somewhere, and slotted in at the edge of the bench talk with the smile of a man auditing a class. Pace let a lull come, and then said, "Vee says you know jazz. Where does it start, for you?"

And Vee watched a key go into a lock. *For you* — the question had no wrong answer built into it, nothing to flunk. Theo looked at Pace like nobody had asked him that in the entire year since the class, which was probably true, and then his shoulders came down and his hands came up, and he was building it right there over the table — Dixieland with one hand and the blues with the other, bringing them together in the air, the collision that made the whole thing go. Danny asked something. Theo answered it with both hands. When Kayla hauled her off toward the corn a while later, the two of them were still at it, and Pace was leaned back out of the lamplight with his arms crossed, in it and not in it, a man watching two strangers he'd introduced to each other get along.

She caught pieces of the rest of it from her orbits. He asked Meg something about her boyfriend's roommate — he had the story's whole geography, the roommate, the incident, details Vee had forgotten telling anyone — and then he leaned back out of it and let Meg have the room, and Meg, fork in the air, letting every sentence finish, was funnier than Vee had ever managed to make her sound. Kayla blew past her at the corn with a report, low and urgent, as if from a war: he'd asked about her sister's wedding — the *dress* part — a story Vee had needed two hours to receive and Kayla barely remembered telling. He set them up. That was as close as Vee got to a word for it, watching from the slaw end of the buffet. He walked around handing people the first line and then he stood back and laughed at their second one, and everyone he left was standing a little taller than when he got there, and not one of them could have said why.

She was in earshot for exactly the start of the Cassie one. Pace took the chair next to her with a pitcher and said, "What do you give the Bricklayers this year?" — straight, no wind-up, the team's whole ridiculous name in his grocery-list voice, a name he could only have had from Vee — and Cassie's eyebrow came up under the cap and stayed there a second, recalibrating something. Then the buffet moved Vee along.

When she looked up next, from the far side of the room, Cassie was *talking*. Cap pushed back, Pace in the chair next to her with his forearms on his knees, and whole paragraphs coming out of her roommate. Vee couldn't hear a word and didn't need one, because she knew this story like a song from its first bar — the flat sweep of the hand for the roommate who couldn't get along with her, *with her*, a thing Cassie still relitigated like a bad call; the shrug for her own roommate, who'd quit school by October and left her with an empty bed; and then the cafeteria, the tray set down across from a stranger's tray, two problems solving each other over bad pizza. Cassie glanced over mid-story, found her looking, and didn't stop telling it. Her roommate, who had watched every man in Vee's vicinity for two years like a goalkeeper watching a corner kick, sat there in a roadhouse giving Vee's boyfriend the best of their two years — glowing, insofar as Cassie glowed, which was drily, at low wattage, and unmistakably — and Vee stood holding her sweet tea and felt something enormous and quiet go by underneath her, like a whale under a boat.

She didn't go over. She wanted to keep seeing it from exactly here.

The twinge came later, and it was nothing.

She was coming back from the buffet line and Pace was at Randi's elbow with the pitcher, saying something she couldn't hear, and Randi laughed — head tipping, the ponytail swinging with it — and for one bad half second something went through Vee with a stinger on it. It had a shape she knew from Christmas, from a phone that didn't ring and a kitchen she'd never seen: him, easy, somewhere she wasn't, being wonderful at a woman. Her hand was at her collarbone before she knew it had moved. And then the rest of the room caught up — this was *Randi*, this was her own doing, she had put these two people in one room herself and would do it again tomorrow — and the stinger backed out of it, and she carried her potato salad to the table and by the time she sat down she was smiling at them both. He was being good to her friends. That was the whole thing she'd wanted. It was going right, and apparently some corner of her didn't know how to watch a thing go right without checking it for the trapdoor.

Winter weeknights empty a room early, and this one obliged. It was a Wednesday and everybody's mornings started early, and by nine the tables had thinned to coats over chair backs. Kayla left in a flurry of hugs, having extracted from Pace an actual promise about the brisket place's cornbread. Theo shook Pace's hand and said something that made them both laugh and went out into the cold with Danny and Meg. Cassie went last of that wave. She'd pulled her cap down and had her keys already turning on one finger, and she stopped by Pace's chair on the exit line. A beat, the eyebrow at half. "You can stay," she told him, and pushed out the door with her keys still spinning, and Vee watched the taillights of the SUV bounce out of the lot on the tired suspension.

And then it was three of them, at one round table, in the warm pine quiet. The buffet lamps were off at the far end. Somebody had turned the speaker down another notch, or the room had just gone quiet enough to make it seem so, the steel guitar walking along under the last of the conversation, and they were taking the night apart the way you do at the end of one that went right — Kayla's promise extracted about the cornbread, the dry *you can stay* Cassie had left him with, who'd laughed at what.

"Randi said something you should hear," Pace said. He was tipped back a little in his chair, hands loose on the table edge, the ease of a man whose evening had gone the distance. "She said, 'Brunches with Vee are the highlight of my week.'"

For one heartbeat Vee's eyes went to him — flat panic, the reflex of a girl who felt guilty in rooms she hadn't entered yet — because she knew what she and Randi got up to at those brunches, and *knows* was suddenly a live word. But there was nothing in his face except the pleasure of a man delivering a compliment that belonged to someone else. Then the heat came up her throat anyway, and across the table Randi was watching it climb with open delight, her eyes gone bright, brighter than the lamplight called for.

"My weekends weren't the same before you," Pace said, to Vee, plainly, at his volume for three.

And he leaned back out of the light exactly as he had all night for the others.

"Oh, I know they weren't," Randi said, eyes flashing, already reaching to top off Vee's glass. "It's wonderful to finally meet the man Vee can't stop talking about. You should hear how she goes on about you at the brunches." Vee made a sound of protest and put a hand over her eyes, the blush arriving whole this time, no panic in front of it, and telling the whole truth she didn't entirely mind — nobody embarrassed her like Randi, and nobody else was allowed to.

"And I told him the truth," Randi said. "The brunches are the highlight of my week." She looked at Vee then, the whole way, the teasing going out of her voice between one word and the next, and Vee sat pinned in the brightness of it, laughing a little, saying *oh no*, because it was turning true. "What I didn't tell him is that meeting Vee was the highlight of my year." She picked up her glass and lifted it, small, no speech in it, just the two of them and the man at the edge of the lamplight. "To my beautiful best friend."

Vee touched her glass and drank and had to look at the table for a second, too full to aim her face at either of them.

Randi set her glass down. She didn't say anything else. She'd gone quiet — a glazed sort of quiet, her eyes somewhere in the middle distance of the tabletop, her fingers turning the glass where it stood, a slow quarter at a time. Vee knew that quiet. She'd seen it a handful of times, always at the same kind of moment — when a story of hers had really landed, when something she'd told got all the way in — and she was privately, foolishly proud of being able to do it, to move Randi, who moved whole rooms for a living and was so rarely moved herself. Vee watched her best friend sit there in the low light and loved her so much it ached. She reached over and put her hand flat over Randi's wrist, over the little cold line of the bracelet, and Randi came back from wherever she'd been and smiled at her, and the smile got loose from her a little, more of it arriving than she seemed to have planned.

They broke it up not long after. Pace was gathering glasses before either woman asked him to; the pine room had that end-of-the-reel feel, warm and done. In the lot the cold hit like a dropped curtain, their breath showing, Randi's car chirping somewhere off in the dark, and Randi turned on the blacktop and pulled Vee in.

The hug was the usual hug, wool and bitter-orange perfume, and the kiss was the usual kiss, the mouth, soft, and the small second press that was only what they did, months of habit now, nothing in it a parking lot couldn't hold.

"Dinner tomorrow," Randi said. "I want the whole debrief of your own party. Love you, gorgeous."

And she was gone across the lot, quick steps ringing on the cold blacktop, and Vee stood a second in the wake of it. Pace was three steps away by the truck with his hands in his jacket pockets, looking at the sky like a man checking for weather. He'd seen — he must have seen, the lot was twelve feet across — and there was nothing on him to say what he made of it, girls being girls, the kiss and the endearment, and a wondering went through Vee, light as a moth: what that looked like from over there. What he thought about it. If he thought about it. Then she crossed to him, and the wondering blew off across the blacktop with her breath, because she took his face in both her hands and kissed him, there in the middle of the lot, longer than a parking lot asks for — a whole evening of watching him be wonderful at her people finally landing where it had been headed all night. He received it the way he received everything, steady, opening to her, and it wasn't enough. She pressed him back a step into the truck, and found his hands, and pulled them up under her sweater herself, cold off the January air against the warm skin of her waist, and her whole body answered its own boldness — her back arching her into him, her breath going ragged against his mouth, her nipples gone hard for the cold or for him, no way to tell and no difference. The lot was empty and dark and twenty minutes from anywhere, and the night she'd been carrying behind her ribs all evening, all its lamps lit, turned out to have been waiting for exactly this.

He opened her door and she got in, and he came around and got in his side, and she did not let him reach the ignition. She went across the bench seat instead, into the dark of the cab that smelled like him, and got his belt open with hands that weren't entirely steady and weren't going to be talked out of anything.

She took him in her mouth and heard the breath go out of him, long, like a man setting something down. He was heavy on her tongue and warm and already hard, and she was greedy about it — there was no other word, she didn't reach for one — greedy with the whole night, the room and the butcher paper and *Vee's boyfriend* said six times to six measuring faces, all of it going into this, into the slow deep work of her mouth in a dark truck in an empty lot. One of his hands gathered her hair back off her face and held it, not steering, just holding; the other found its way under her and under the sweater, pushed her bra up out of its way, and filled itself with her breast, and when his thumb dragged across the nipple she moaned around him and felt him feel it. He said her name once, low, half of it lost. She stayed with it, hungrier the longer it took, listening to him come apart by degrees — and then the hand in her hair went still. Stopped moving altogether, just held, forgotten, every part of him pulled down to one place, and she knew what that stillness was and worked him through it and took what he gave her, all of it, swallowing around him while his hand sat weightless and stunned in her hair. That stillness. She could live on that stillness — the man who did six things at once, down to zero.

She stayed a moment more, her cheek against him, his heartbeat enormous under her ear. Then she sat up, and pushed her own hair back, and said, "I need you to take me to bed now."

"Yes, ma'am," Pace said, not quite evenly, and started the truck.

The vents came on cold, straight onto her flushed face and down the open collar of her coat, and before she'd thought anything at all she was on a mountain road in September — the fan loud, the air cold, *it'll only blow cold till the truck warms up*, both of them soaked and her body shuddering small and his hand, when she'd taken it, trembling in hers. He'd been shivering that whole time and hadn't said one word about it. She looked over at him now, dry and warm and easy behind the wheel, one wrist on top of it, nothing in his world trembling, and the wanting came up through her so plainly it was almost calm.

"You were —" she started, and gave up on the sentence, and picked another. "They loved you. All of them. Cassie said you could stay."

"I liked her. She argues honestly."

"Kayla's going to tell the sister's-wedding story at your funeral." She watched the trees go by, her blood still down somewhere low and humming, in no hurry to climb back up. "What did you and Randi end up talking about? I kept seeing you two laughing and I never once got there in time."

Pace took a curve, easy, and she watched the corner of his mouth in the dash light.

"We get along," he said. "They share an interest."

"Who does?"

"Randi. Your friends. All of them." A mile marker went by. "You."

Vee looked at him a moment, then out at the road, and let it warm her from the middle out, the plain and obvious truth of it, too sweet to poke at: her people, and the thing they had in common was her, and he'd noticed, because he noticed everything. And the warmth kept going. It went down. She reached over and took his hand off the wheel — he let her have it, driving on the one wrist without a word — and drew it across the seat and put it up under her sweater, where her bra still sat pushed above her breasts from the parking lot, and he understood her the way he understood everything, no searching, his big hand closing warm over her bare breast and kneading it slow, catching the nipple and pulling until her breath went. She slid down an inch in the seat and let her knees fall open and felt it, the slick heat of herself against the lace of her thong, the little she'd worn on purpose, wet since the lot, wet since before the lot — and there was another dark road in her all at once, the county road in the black fields, her own hand where no one's hand should be at speed, hungry for him with the whole state of Virginia between them; and the mark she'd left that night, soaked into the pale lining of the good coat, the dark bloom of herself she'd never be able to explain to a soul. She'd never tried. She'd kept the coat.

Bed was twenty minutes away and the night was *now*. She heard herself make a frustrated sound at her own jeans — at the fact of them, the stupid sturdy fact — and then her hands went to the button.

She undid it. The zipper was loud in the cab, louder than the heater, and he didn't look over and she was glad he didn't look over, because her face was burning and she was doing it anyway — hips up off the bench seat, the denim worked down her thighs a graceless honest inch at a time, a girl undressing herself in a moving truck on a county road, and when she settled back the old vinyl of the bench seat was cold against skin the thong did nothing to cover, and there was nothing in any of it she could call an accident. Then she took his hand from her breast and put it where she needed it.

He touched her over the lace first, light, the road running empty and black ahead, and found the narrow strip of it already soaked — she heard what it did to his breathing when he found it — and he rubbed her like that, slow, through lace gone useless, until her hips were moving and her head was back against the seat. It was her that pulled it aside. Her own fingers, dragging the sticky lace off herself and holding it out of his way, an offering she had to keep making, choosing it again every second it went on; and his fingers came onto her bare and slick and she said something that wasn't a word. He knew her. That was the unbearable sweet center of it — no searching, no asking, the same slow circles her own hand had used on that other road and been denied, only it was his hand, and it was not stopping, and the truck ran on steady under his one wrist the whole time like the road itself was something he was doing to her. She came with her boots braced on the floorboard and her free hand clenched in his sleeve and the mile markers going by uncounted, came hard and long and loud in the little warm box of the cab, and he drove her through it, easy, all the way down the far side of it, until her hand unclenched and lay flat on his forearm like something washed ashore.

He drew the lace gently back into place. She sat there with her jeans at her knees and her heart slowing and watched the trees go by, and didn't fix anything, not yet, riding claimed and easy and openly undone through the dark between the trees with the heat ticking, and if some small sentence in her was still standing at a window somewhere, watching a glass turn a slow quarter at a time, it couldn't have made itself heard tonight over her own blood, and it didn't try.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 7: A Clean Plate — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (clean-plate.md) =====

At four she swam.

The pool was half empty, the water its winter blue, and for an hour and change there was nothing in the world but the black line and her own breath. It was the one stretch of any day her head went quiet — her body doing work it had known since she was twelve, needing no opinion from her about itself.

She climbed out loose and clear-headed. Then the old drill, fast and automatic: cap off, a rinse in the pool showers to sluice the chlorine before it set into her hair or the suit, sweats over damp skin, and home.

The real washing she saved for the little bathroom off her room — the water came out of that showerhead like an apology, but the door locked, and among the suite's crowd of bottles her own two waited: the clarifying shampoo that pulled the chlorine, the heavy conditioner it took to talk her hair back down after. The gym showers were for surviving, and tonight was not that kind of night.

And then the part of the day she'd quietly built the rest of it around. She took her time at the closet.

*It's a date,* Randi had said Tuesday on the sidewalk. Only the second table, Vee told herself — the dinner after class, nothing she'd let herself make more of. But a table like that still earned deciding what Randi would see across it, and she stood in front of her clothes with the same keyed-up gladness she saved for getting dressed for Pace.

Because tonight she finally had the whole weekend to hand across — all of it, the best of it, saved up two days and gone nowhere — and Randi was the one person alive built to take a story like that at full value. Tuesday they'd almost gotten there; she'd told it as far as Saturday's breakfast before the place stacked the chairs on the best of it and sent the rest home in her pocket.

She'd been turning that ending over ever since, polishing the parts, the way you carry a thing you can't wait to give away. There was one small wrinkle near the finish she couldn't quite call herself — a strange little thing, everything gone sideways for a second in the middle of everything good — but that was a footnote, one question for the one friend who'd know the answer. The rest she was dying to tell.

Not the wrap again; Tuesday's verdict on that was in. The bias-cut skirt, then — the January find, a dark floral with a green thread run through it, thirty-one dollars on its second markdown and no plan to confess it across a white cloth — and the toffee sweater from the same afternoon, fine-gauge, with a good stretch to it, which was the point.

The arithmetic had run one way since high school: anything that fit her chest drowned her waist, so she'd bought for the chest and lived in the tent and called the tent her size.

Stretch changed the math. She'd carried the smaller size to the register on the strength of it and declined to total the rest — you shopped with your eye and not your budget, and nobody across a table could tell which one had done the choosing.

She turned once at the mirror, the knit going everywhere it was sent, and decided she was pleased and left the deciding there. Her face she did in the good light.

No dinner first — that had answered itself before she thought to ask. Thursday was a class and then a table with Randi at it, and a girl didn't eat before a table like that. She crossed the Drillfield with the good olive coat buttoned to the collar and the cold coming clean off the open dark, hungry in the pleasant, banked way of a woman who knows exactly when and how well she's going to be fed.

Randi was already there. Of course she was. Back third, aisle, the coat folded over the saved seat, and she lifted it clear as Vee came down the row and patted the chair, beaming.

"Hey, gorgeous."

"Hey, you." Vee dropped into the held seat and thumbed her coat open, leaving it on against the draft off the big cold hall. Randi's hand found hers and squeezed and let go, just like Tuesday, and then her knee came to rest against Vee's, and stayed, the ordinary nothing of a shared armrest. At seven exactly the door opened and Dr. Marsh came in with the canvas bag.

She set it on the desk as she had on Tuesday. Then she opened it, which she had not done on Tuesday, and the room noticed.

What came out was small. She held it up between two fingers so the lecture hall could fail to see it, and then she came around the desk and down into the front row and put it into the hands of a startled boy in a hoodie.

"Pass that back," she said, and returned to the desk. "Gently. It's older than the pyramids at Giza and considerably better made.

"It's a comb. Boxwood. It's for lice." She let that sit. "Every person in this room knew a version of that before I said it — you looked at a row of teeth cut finer than any comb you'd buy for hair, and something in you knew. That knowledge is older than every city on earth. Your entire species used to spend its evenings doing what that comb does, with its fingers, in each other's hair.

"A baboon budgets a fifth of its waking life for grooming. One-fifth. The fleas are gone inside ten minutes — the fleas were never the point. It stays the hour. It goes and sits with another baboon and puts its hands in the fur, and no baboon has ever been taught to do it, and no baboon has ever skipped it, and if you take it away from them they sicken. Watch who grooms whom in a troop for one week and you can draw the whole politics of it without learning a single call.

"You're all modern people. You shake hands." She rocked forward on the boots, a hiker sighting a ridgeline. "One-fifth of every waking day, gone from the budget. Nothing is free. Somebody in this room, by spring break, is going to tell me what you spend it on now."

She picked up the chalk and wrote the reading on the board, and did not answer her own question, and the comb went hand to hand up the rows behind it like a slow rumor. Vee watched it travel — each pair of fingers taking it carefully, keeping it a little while, giving it up — and somewhere around the middle of the room it stopped traveling, absorbed into some row that couldn't bear to part with it yet, and Marsh lectured on over the top of it and never once asked for it back.

The rest of the hour was cleaner fish — the little wrasse that pick the parasites off bigger fish that could swallow them whole and never do, a whole reef economy of trust and cheating and who comes back — and it was riveting enough to keep her mostly out of her own head, where the whole good evening sat banked and waiting to be spent.

Halfway through it Vee worked the roll of Lifesavers out of her coat pocket — it lived there all winter, wintergreen, gone soft at the foil — and thumbed one loose and tapped it against Randi's wrist. Randi's hand turned over. The candy went into the palm and the palm closed and neither of them looked away from the front, and for a while the faint cool of wintergreen came off them both, one lecture, one flavor.

At 8:20 the hall let them out into the dark. The comb, as far as Vee ever knew, was still somewhere in the middle rows.

Outside, the cold took their breath and handed it back sharpened. They went off the edge of campus toward the little restaurant, the lamplight thinning behind them, the sidewalk theirs — the small dark one and the tall red one, Randi's heels bringing her nearly to Vee's shoulder.

"So." There was a smile already loading in Randi's voice. "You should have heard him with Meg and Kayla."

"I *know*—" and then it came up out of her all at once, because she'd been full of it since Wednesday and here at last was the one person built to take the whole thing. "Randi, I didn't know how he'd be. I'd only ever seen him with his country crowd, the two-step people — I had no idea what he'd do with mine. And he was better than I hoped. So much better than I hoped."

"He was a *hit.* I keep telling you."

"And *boyfriend.*" Vee laughed, still not over it. "He said it right out — you and I heard it the same second, the first time either of us ever had — and then again, and again, all night, in front of everyone. Like he'd been calling me that for years."

"You didn't know that was coming?"

"Total surprise to me. I about fell over." She was grinning at the dark sidewalk. "You should've seen how I thanked him."

And her voice went down on the last of it, dropping under the wind into the register kept for one person, and Randi — who heard the drop and knew exactly what lived down there — hooked their arms together and drew her in close, hip to hip, two heads bent, the cold street made a private room.

"Tell me you thanked him."

"We didn't get out of the parking lot." Vee pressed closer, the words tumbling now. "You'd barely pulled off. I walked him back into the side of that truck and took his face in both hands, and — Randi, I have never once in my life just *decided* like that. I put his hands up under my sweater myself. Both of them. And then I got the door open and went across the seat."

"Across the *seat,*" Randi said, low, delighted.

"Got his belt undone with my hands not even steady, and I did not care, and I leaned down and—"

—and the restaurant was there. Warm windows, the jukebox's green water climbing behind the glass, the door breathing heat onto the walk. It came up on them like a hand laid over her mouth mid-word.

They pulled up short and stood a second, both flushed, both a little unstrung, a good way down a road and hauled up by a destination neither had been watching for.

"…We're here," Randi said.

"We're here." Neither of them moved. Then they laughed — a groan of protest in Vee's, the story yanked out from under her — and they went in.

Inside, the jukebox was playing strings tonight, something slow with a violin walking through it, the green water still climbing its clear pipes for no one. The girl at the front took two menus off the stack at the sight of them, and didn't ask.

The corner booth in the loft had waited. They shed coats fast, both of them, like people with somewhere to be — the somewhere was the story, still standing open back on the sidewalk where the door had cut it off — and Vee reached to turn hers lining-to-the-wall on the hook, the old habit two dinners old, and was in too much of a hurry to land it. The coat swung open on the way, pale satin out, and for a second before she caught it, there it was under the loft light: the dark bloom of herself, soaked into the good coat and never coming out — the body telling on her before she'd said a word.

She turned it to the wall, fast, and Randi — down over her own coat, then coming up bright — hadn't seen. Only Vee had. The coat had remembered, and shown her, and she'd shut it away against the wall like a drawer closing on a photograph, and her face was warm before Randi had said the first thing.

Then Randi got a look at her and put her hand to her heart, and the look went down and came back up, frank as an appraisal and twice as happy.

"*Girl.* It's criminal what you've been hiding from the rest of us." And she set her hands on Vee's waist, both of them, warm through the fine knit, and held her there at arm's length a second, the way you hold a thing up to the light to see it properly.

And Vee stood there being held and looked at, Randi's hands warm on her waist through the fine knit, and a warmth climbed her face and turned into a smile — pleasure, the whole distance of it.

They settled into the booth, and the girl who'd brought them up asked what they wanted to drink, and Randi ordered the red without opening the list, the same as Tuesday. She came back with it and poured them each a glass and asked after their food, and neither of them had so much as touched a menu.

"The special," Randi said.

"The special," Vee said, a half-beat behind, with no idea on earth what it was, and the girl looked from one of them to the other and went back down, and neither of them had thought to ask.

Randi settled back with her glass, and she had not forgotten where the sidewalk had cut Vee off. "You'd leaned down," she said, easy, holding the place open like a thumb in a book. "In the lot. The roadhouse lit up. Go on."

And Vee gave the version that crossed a white cloth clean. "My head went to his lap," she said, light, "and he *came apart.* This big steady man — his hand came into my hair and forgot it was there, and I felt the whole of him just go." She reached for the clean word: "It was a lot."

And even saying it she felt how small she'd made it — the true thing folded down to something you could set on a cloth — and felt, without looking up, that Randi had the size of exactly what was missing.

Randi let that sit, and set one finger down on it. "Did you take it all?"

And the question put her body back in the cab — leaning over the bench in the dark, her bra still shoved up above her breasts and her nipples singing where his hands had left them, the soap and musk of him in her nose, the hard fact of him in her mouth. "Yeah." Half of her was still down there; the word came from the other half.

"Didn't spill a drop?"

And that dropped her into the end of it — the pulse and the flood, the taste of him gone flat and hot across her tongue, and how she'd swallowed every bit of it *proud,* proud to have undone a man that steady. Her mouth went dry, actually dry, and the wine was no help for it, and she shifted in her seat before she could stop the shift. It was true — every word of it true, and she'd done it in a parking lot and not cared who saw — and she could not make her mouth say it back across the cloth.

"You *did* it," Randi said, low, delighted and tender and without a scrap of mercy. "And you can't even say it."

She went to laugh and it didn't come out as one — a breath with a shake through it, her hand not steady bringing the glass up. She drank, and still didn't say it, and it didn't help.

Then, quieter, from behind the glass, the part she could stand to give: "I told him to take me to bed. He said *yes ma'am,* and started the truck." The sly came back with the ground under it. "Only I couldn't wait that long."

"That's my girl."

"So he drove me home." The sanded version again, her eyes going soft on it. "One wrist on the wheel the whole county road, never a word. And I couldn't wait. I put his hand where I needed it."

"Where you needed it." Randi turned her glass a slow quarter. "Over your jeans."

"…I'd gotten them open." Barely out.

"You'd gotten them open. In a moving truck." She wasn't going to let her round it. "And then?"

And Vee, pinned again, gave it — but small, close, the way you hand over the true thing. "It was *me* that moved them aside. My own hand. I held myself open for his the whole way, kept holding." She heard herself and pushed on before she could stop. "And he knew exactly, no hunting, no asking — and I went off on that county road with my boots braced on the floor and the mile markers going by, and I did not care who was out there." The last word barely made it out. "Loud."

Randi let a breath go that wasn't quite steady.

The food came up the stair then, two plates of the special, whatever it turned out to be — a dark braise over soft polenta — and Vee, who'd swum a mile and gone cold through and eaten nothing since noon, fell on it and ate like she meant it. It was good; she said so, once, and let it go. A student place's braise, hot and gone. Her whole mind was still up the county road. Randi took one small, exact bite of her own, set the fork down, and left it.

Randi turned her glass by the stem, slow. "And you still owe me Tuesday." The bawdy note came into it, low and warm. "You left me with your foot up on the man's chair and the toast on fire, and they turned the lights out on me. I need to know what the pleasure princess did with her slave. Court's back in session."

Vee laughed, and it had all her appetite in it. "Where was I—"

"Breakfast. The toes. He could see everything."

"Right." She had some wine. "So — breakfast. He'd done these mushrooms, Randi, I don't even know what he puts in them, some pan and butter and his special spice, and I ate the whole plate and told him so, twice, mouth full — I could taste every separate —"

Randi was giggling.

Vee stopped, the story mid-air, puzzled. "What?"

"No wonder," Randi said.

"No wonder what?"

"Your enthusiasm." Her eyes were dancing. "You do like a flavor."

"…Yeah?"

"Did you swallow all of it," Randi said, losing it now, "and wipe your mouth?"

And Vee laughed — caught full in it, hand flying to her chest, mock-scandalized and thrilled clean through at the wickedness of it. "Those are two completely *separate* —"

"Are they?"

"They are *adjacent!*" She waved it off, delighted, and the story pulled her back down into the house. "*Anyway.* So breakfast ends. And he gets up and clears, because he clears — he cooks, he clears, it's a whole liturgy — and he ties the apron strings tighter and runs the water hot, and he'd made me tea, black with the honey stirred in, it's just at my elbow, it appeared there at some point — things appear in that house. And I'm sitting at his counter in nothing, drinking my tea, watching this man do dishes."

"In the apron."

"In the apron. And Randi, I don't know how to explain what happened next except that his back is — he has a *good back*, and the kitchen's all warm water-noise and clinking, and I'd been fed within an inch of my life, and somewhere in the middle of watching him scrub a pot I stopped being a person having a nice morning." She set the glass down. "It arrived all at once. Low. Like a front nobody forecast."

"So you told him."

"I did not tell him one word." Vee held up a finger. "I pointed at the dish rack and said, 'Missed a spot.'"

Randi's mouth curved.

"There was no spot. He bends down to look — this precise, serious man, auditing his own dish — and I lean in over him and I…" her hand sketched it, a slow pass in the air, "…brushed my breast along his cheek. Slow. Like it was an accident that took two full seconds."

"You've done that before," Randi said. "That exact crime."

"Once. Up on the riser, with the pins, last fall — you got that story." Vee's chin came up. "It's a classic for a reason. And his hands come up out of the sink, wet, warm, and they close on my waist — and I lean back out of range and I wag my finger at him. One finger. Side to side. No."

"After you started it."

"After I started it, entirely. And Randi." She leaned in. "He looked *puzzled.* I have been looking at that man's face since September and I have seen it do maybe nine things, and I have never seen it puzzled. It was like catching an eclipse."

"So you have the wet hands, the apron, the puzzled look." Randi turned her glass a slow quarter where it stood. "And a decision to make."

"I'd made it. I got off the stool and walked out of the kitchen. Didn't say a word, didn't look back."

"*Naked,*" Randi said, sitting up like a woman at a parade. "The pleasure princess. Processing to her chambers, bare as the day — and the slave *follows —*"

"The slave followed," Vee confirmed, gone regal to match her. "I heard the water shut off behind me, and the towel hit the counter, and then his tread."

Randi laid the back of one hand to her brow. "Off to be royally—"

"*Don't* you—"

"—*screwed,*" Randi finished, serene as a saint, and Vee's mouth came open around a laugh she had no say in, aghast and delighted in the one breath, because it was terrible, and it was perfect, and it was exactly what happened.

Her voice went back down into it then, the telling pulling her inside the house. "And in the bedroom I turned around and put my hand up. Palm out. And he stopped like the hand had touched him. From across the room."

Randi was very still, arms folded, all her lights aimed.

"And I sat down on that cashmere blanket, and I looked at him standing there in a flowered apron with his hands still damp, and I did this." She made the little swirl in the air, a circling of two fingers, *all of it, off.* "And sat back to watch."

"And?"

"Quickest I've ever seen a man get out of an apron. It went over his head in one move like a bird leaving." The loft's lamplight had her flushed now, or the wine did, or the story did, all three drawing on the same account. "And then I pointed at what I wanted."

"Pointed." The bawdy had gone out of Randi's voice. She'd set the court down and gone still, watching Vee's face, and she didn't say anything else.

"Here first." She touched the inside of her wrist, the white part, where the veins run. "And he crossed the room and put his mouth on the inside of my wrist and *stayed there.* Randi, who stays? Everything I know about men says the wrist is a toll booth — you pay it and drive on. He parked."

Randi didn't say anything. Her fingernail found the rim of her glass and rode it once, slow, the thin edge singing almost too low to hear, and she drew her hand back into her lap.

"And then his mouth just — traveled. Up the arm. Places nobody kisses, the crook of the elbow, higher — I pointed once more, at my breast, and even then he made me feel it coming from about a mile out — and after that I stopped pointing, mostly. It stopped being a thing I was directing and started being a thing I was surviving. He went down the wrong side of everything on purpose. I aimed him one place and he'd detour — the hip, the leg, the backs of my knees.

"He kissed my *toes*, Randi. Individually. Each toe got its own kiss like it had done something. The frosted plum, all ten. A sound came out of me I'm not reprinting."

"Reprint it."

"A trill. Like a phone from the nineties." She had both hands around her glass and wasn't drinking from it. "And by the time his mouth actually arrived anywhere, I'd been arriving for half an hour. And he — you remember the oranges. The blood oranges, the peel off in strips, every section lifted out of its skin, no white left anywhere, all that patience just to get at the — that's what he did. That's what it was like. He opened me like the orange."

She heard herself land it — *like the orange* — and her body was way out ahead of her: pulse up in her throat, the flush gone down past her collar, everything in her pulled tight and humming, and she was holding her face still over the top of it.

Somewhere below them the room made its noise and left the booth alone. Randi didn't laugh, didn't hand anything back — she held still, her eyes staying on Vee, and let the quiet sit.

She reached for her wine, and heard herself start to fold it up small, tuck it back to a size she could sit behind. "And then — you can fill in the—"

"Don't you *dare.*" Randi hadn't moved, but the fond had gone out of her and something level had come in behind it, the tone that never once sounded like a dare. "Don't you fade to firelight and leave me wondering. You just made it smaller. Give me the real one — the harder one."

And Vee's face did the thing she couldn't call back, because the real one was right there, present-tense, running through her again as if the booth had gone and turned into the bedroom, and she gave it up.

"There was nothing polite about it," she said, quieter, and not tidy at all now. "That's the part I keep not being able to say right. He wasn't — attending. He was *at* it. Noisy. Like the last of something he'd been promised. And I went — the first time was before he'd so much as — with nothing, Randi, from his mouth and the fact of him, legs in the air like a cartoon. And the second one whited me out. Sight and sound. Gone."

"God." It came out of Randi low and sincere, her bright register nowhere in it.

"And then he came up the whole length of me while I was still — everywhere — and slid into me slow, and I'm lying there thinking, in these words, *what this man does to me.*" She stopped. Drank. Set the glass down with care, and the punchline assembled itself in her, the one she'd built on the walk home Tuesday, the one she'd polished in the shower since, thirty seconds, funny, closed. "And then he kissed me."

"As he should."

"With his mouth *wet.*" Vee's hands came up, presenting it, the whole absurdity. "With — me. All over it. I'm kissing him back before I know anything, and then it arrives, the taste of—"

—and it arrived now, in the booth, saying it: the wet of it back on her own tongue before she could stop it, present-tense and real, and the bit fell out of her hands. She sat a second with her mouth a little open, nothing funny in her face at all.

Then she got hold of it again, fast. "—it's *me,* is the thing. I'm tasting *myself,* Randi, off my own boyfriend's mouth, in the middle of the — and my whole body did what I can only call a full-system objection. I turned my face. I put the back of my hand over my mouth like a Victorian."

She did it, hand up, the gesture rerun for the loft as farce. "And I told him — this man, mid — I told him, '*Ugh — gross.*' And then, because apparently I wasn't done, '*That's — no. Wrong.*'"

She had it all delivered now, the whole shape, her own outrage arranged on the table like a party trick, and she went for the closer.

"I mean — obviously. Right? Gross. Wrong. Somebody had to say it. And the very best part is I wouldn't let him stop what he was — I'm objecting on decency grounds while actively holding the man in place, I want that on the record — and he just wiped his mouth, one pass, like a gentleman, and said 'Okay,' and carried on being magnificent, and that was that." She was laughing, mostly. "You think it's gross too, don't you."

Randi considered the question exactly as long as it deserved, which turned out to be longer than Vee had budgeted.

"I don't."

It was flat. Not cold — level, mild, a fact set down on the cloth between them, and Vee's laugh came down off its step and stood there in the loft with nowhere to go.

"You — hm." Vee blinked at her. "You've never—?"

"I have."

"…Did you like it?"

Randi picked up her wine, considering, the lamplight on the deco lines of her, and when she answered it was in the voice she used for restaurant recommendations.

"It was powerful," she said. "It turned me on."

And that dropped Vee back into the bed — sheened in sweat, pulsing, the pulse going and not stopping, him just slid inside and opening her slow, and his mouth, barely up off her sex, coming down on hers. She felt the wet of his kiss all over again, and clenched hard to it, right there in the booth, before she could stop herself. Her body remembered the whole of it, and had no complaint.

Randi looked at Vee, her eyes listening.

"But it's gross," Vee said, too loud — too loud for a settled thing, too loud for how little Randi had spent saying the opposite — and she couldn't take the volume back.

Randi looked at her, pleasant as a hostess, and asked her only question.

"How does he act?"

"He—" Vee stopped. The morning was right there: the mouth that came to her wet and in no way sorry, the one economical pass of the hand, the *Okay* against her jaw with the smile under it. "He likes it. Actually—" and the word was out before any part of her had vetted it, "—he's enthusiastic."

"Mm," Randi said, warmly, the sound of a woman finding everything in order, and ate the bread.

And that was all. No verdict, no lecture, no little bow on it. The server came up the stair for the plates, and Vee looked down at hers as it went — polished clean, the fennel thing gone entire, the chop reduced to its bone; she had eaten all of it without noticing she was doing it — and Randi asked the server what the violin piece downstairs had been, and nobody at the table thought anything else worth saying about Saturday morning, apparently, ever again.

The talk went on being easy. Class Tuesday, the reading, whether Marsh assigned like a woman who'd forgotten students took other courses. It was fine. It was lovely.

And under it, at the bottom of her glass, Vee kept arriving at a slightly wrong feeling — that she'd handed the morning over and was still holding it. She had another sip of wine and put it where she put things, and it went, mostly.

They split the check at the little terminal. Ten o'clock again — they'd done it again, shut the place down on a school night, and the boy downstairs was stacking chairs with his practiced apology, and they went down the steep stair with their coats on and out past the jukebox and its climbing green water into cold that had teeth in it now, the real end-of-January kind.

The sidewalk had them to itself. Streetlight, breath.

"Same time Tuesday," Randi said. "I'll hold the seats. You bring the candy."

"It's a deal."

"You picked well, you know." Randi said it easily, apropos of nothing, the way you finally name a thing you've meant to say for weeks. "I don't think I ever told you that. You picked well."

And Vee glowed and took it, and only the smallest part of her went to the one word that wasn't quite true — she hadn't picked Pace so much as looked up from her books one afternoon, Randi across the table and Cassie one over, to find him already halfway across the floor at her, the whole thing decided before she'd known there was a thing to decide. A good day he'd walked in and made a great one. But you didn't hand a compliment back on a technicality.

And then Randi kissed her goodbye, as they did — the mouth, soft, the little pause, the second one after it — and drew her into the hug, a good one tonight, long, coats crushed together, Vee's cheek against Randi's cool cheek, the bitter orange finding her, green at its edge.

And Vee was already looking at the neck. She was smiling before she'd decided to, remembering last time — Randi pointing to the very spot where a whole December's crying had once soaked in, the kiss she'd set there without thinking, the flinch, *don't you dare weaponize that.* And Randi, who must have forgotten, tipped her head to settle the hug deeper and laid the whole of it open — the hollow where the neck turns down into the shoulder, warm straight out of the cold — and her hair, lifted on the breeze, brushed Vee's cheek. And Vee kissed it. Just did. Vee's mouth found the way again on the memory of Tuesday, and the kiss was landed before any of her had convened on the question.

Up close it was all small things: Randi's breath uneven, close over her ear; the skin soft and warm against her lips. And under her mouth Randi's whole body did a small quick thing, a catch and then a rise, and the rise went on a beat too long —

"*Ticklish,*" Randi said, light, laughing it off, her shoulder coming up to take the spot back. And Vee, pleased with herself, let her have it.

"Thank you," she said, into the cold, a beat behind her own mouth. "For the good advice."

"Only for you, gorgeous." Randi's arms tightened once, a full-stop of a squeeze, and let her go. "Now walk fast, it's arctic."

"Tuesday."

"Tuesday."

The walk back was six minutes with the wind coming at her this time, so she put her chin into her collar and leaned on it.

The evening rode along inside her coat where the cold couldn't get at it — the comb, the verdict, *you picked well*, the court and its whole warm session — and with it, keeping pace, having apparently walked out of the restaurant with her, came one sentence in her own voice, arriving again at its own intervals, like a song that gets in.

*He likes it. Actually — he's enthusiastic.*

She'd said that. Under oath, more or less. And somewhere below it, level, mild, taking up almost no room at all: *I don't.*

The wind shoved at her the whole six minutes, and the sentence kept up with her easily, and Vee walked fast, and let it.

The sash went down hard over the draft. The glass gave one small rattle in its frame, then held, and the room began at once to belong to the radiator.

She got ready for bed by stages. Earrings into the little dish. Sweater over her head. Skirt unzipped and stepped out of, tights peeled down and balled together for morning. In the suite bathroom she washed her face, brushed her teeth, worked the pins out of her hair and left it loose. When she came back, the cold line along the floor was gone.

The room had gone past comfortable while she was away. Heat stood in it. Cassie's side looked baked and briefly abandoned: the empty hook, the open book, the desk lamp still lit over nobody. Vee turned that lamp off and chose Pace's flannel.

The smell had gone thin by Thursday, but inside the collar there was still enough of him to find. She held it to her face once, then slipped her arms into sleeves that fell past her hands. It was the softest thing she owned, the worn flannel settling over her shoulders and trailing luxuriously along her bare sides. She rolled each sleeve back twice until her hands were free. She put on nothing else. Three sizes too large, it would cover her to mid-thigh when she buttoned it.

She left every button open and climbed into bed with the plan already settled.

The radiator clicked and knocked below. With the window closed, every small heat stayed where it was put. The open flannel lay along her sides, its nap moving over her ribs whenever she breathed. Her hair lay heavy along her neck. She lifted it free and spread it over the pillow.

Not urgent now. Smaller than that. A slow, low throb that answered the pressure of her thighs and went on after she eased them apart. She lay still through another one.

*Greedy.*

Small and hot. The pulse tightened around it.

Above her in the white morning, his mouth wet, his eyes on hers. No apology in him. No recoil. He had looked at the whole slick fact of her as if it were true, as if it were fine. As if the plenty of her required no pardon before he brought it to her mouth.

The wet kiss had closed her hard around him before any objection came. It had happened again when she told Randi and the kiss returned, the same deep clench with nothing inside her. Her body had given the same answer twice. She wanted to find it again.

She drew the flannel closed and buttoned it. The enormous flannel covered her at once, respectable to mid-thigh.
