===== BOOK TWO ITEM 11: Hangover — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (hangover.md) =====

The light was wrong before anything else was. It came at the window from a low angle that meant late morning, not the honest gray she'd have earned by getting up when she'd meant to, and it found the backs of her eyes and pressed. Vee lay still and let the day assemble itself one careful piece at a time, because turning her head seemed like a decision that ought to be made slowly, after review.

His side of the bed was cold. Pace was up. Pace was always up, she was learning, by some hour that kept no honest name either.

On the nightstand, where she'd find it before she found anything else, stood a tall glass of ice water, the ice long melted to slivers, a ring of sweat around its base soaked into a folded square of paper towel he'd put down so it wouldn't mark the wood. She got up on one elbow and drank it down without stopping, and it was the best thing that had ever happened to her.

The night came back to her out of order, the way a dropped deck comes back — not the game, just the cards. She'd fallen asleep in the truck; that one was near the top, the heater ticking and her cheek on the cold window and then nothing. Somewhere lower there was the bathroom, the hard white light of it and him crouched in front of her with a glass in his hand saying *drink this, all of it,* patient as a man watering something. Two small brown pills in her palm. A gap where the walk from the truck to the door should have been, that she reached for and didn't find. And then, last, the things that had stuck: his hands drawing one of his own undershirts down over her head, the soft worn white cotton of it swallowing her to the knees, the smell of his soap in it; being steered by the shoulders to the bed; his mouth, once, on her forehead, in the dark, after he thought she was already gone.

She lay in the shirt now. She hadn't undressed herself. She hadn't done one single thing for herself since the third cider, and here she was in his bed under his blanket with water inside her and the worst of it already handled, and she had a sudden vivid sense of how much of the night must have been just him, quietly, doing all of it.

She got herself vertical. His robe was on the back of the door where it always was, terrycloth gone soft with washing, and she put it on over the shirt and tied it and followed her nose.

The smell reached her in the hall and got better the whole way down. When she came into the kitchen he was at the sink with his sleeves rolled, and on the counter between the table and the sink, cooling on a rack, was a loaf of bread, dark-crusted and studded, that had not existed when the world ended last night.

"You made bread," she said. Her voice came out with gravel in it.

"You were asleep for a while."

"You made *bread.*"

"Cinnamon raisin." He said it as if that settled the strangeness of a man baking a loaf from scratch while a woman slept off three ciders in his bed. He turned from the sink and looked at her in his robe and his shirt and whatever her face was doing, and something moved at the corner of his mouth and he let it go. "Sit. You need to eat before you decide how you feel."

She sat. The counter was between them, and she was glad of it; she was assembled out of spare parts this morning and wanted a table's width to hold them together. He put things in front of her one at a time, the way he did everything — a mug of tea going gold with honey, then two slices of the bread, buttered and gone translucent at the edges, then eggs, soft, folded, salted exactly right. She started on it before she'd meant to and made a sound over the first bite of the toast that she'd have been embarrassed by on a better morning.

"I like her so much," she said, when she surfaced. "Sheri. I like her *so* much."

"She likes you too."

The words landed with a little echo on them, and Vee heard, faintly, that she'd said this before — leaning on his shoulder in the truck with the fields going by, *I like her,* small and drunk and certain. She'd said it then and she was saying it now, and the second time was somehow funnier, being sober and ruined instead of drunk and sincere. She let out a laugh, and then a real one came up behind it, because a picture had arrived — Sheri planted in front of her on the taproom floor, feet set, taking her time down the whole length of her, that appreciative soundless *oh* at the shoes.

"She *liked* me," Vee said, and put a hand flat to her chest, and laughed some more. "That look. In the taproom. She looked at me like—"

"That way too," Pace said. "She can be like that." He said it without a flicker, easy, matter-of-fact. He'd caught exactly what she meant; he always caught it. He just never pretended not to have.

"No—" Vee got herself back. "No, we're good. It was—" She waved the fork. "I liked it, honestly. Best thing that's happened to my legs in a Virginia January." She ate another bite, warm all through in a way the tea hadn't done by itself, and the ease of it, the whole soft domestic ordinary morning of it, opened the next thing in her the way one thing always opened another with him.

"I'd love to meet your other friends," she said.

He'd turned back to the sink. He didn't turn around for it. "Sheri is my friend," he said, to the window.

Vee smiled at his back and reached for her tea. "Of course she is." It wasn't an answer to anything; it was the kind of thing you said while a real answer came. "She told me, last night — how good you were to her, when she came out. When everybody else—" She stopped, because it was Sheri's to tell and she'd already carried more of it out of that taproom than she'd meant to. "You mean the world to her. Do you know that?" She drank. "But I meant your *other* friends. The guys."

He rinsed a pan and set it in the rack, and there was a pause in him that she'd have missed on any morning her head wasn't turned all the way toward him. Then he turned around and looked at her across the counter, and said it plainly, the way he said everything he'd already decided not to dress up.

"Sheri's my friend," he said. "My one friend. There isn't anyone else."

The kitchen went very slightly still. She sat with it and waited for it to rearrange into something she recognized, and it wouldn't. Nobody had *one* friend. One friend wasn't a number a person had. She looked for the joke in his face and there wasn't one, and she looked for the door he'd left himself and there wasn't one of those either.

"But—" She set the fork down. "But everybody likes you. You're—" She heard herself and kept going anyway. "I *saw* you. At the party. People you'd never met, people who aren't anything like you, and by the end of the night they were all standing around a little taller and none of them could've told you why. You're the most comfortable person with people I've ever — you could have *friends,* Pace. Anyone. Why?" It came out more helpless than she'd meant it. "Why don't you have friends?"

He considered it with the honest attention he gave every real question, the second or two it took him to find the actual answer and not the near one.

"Women misunderstand it," he said. "The friendship. It gets awkward." He said it flat, and let it be small, and didn't sharpen it.

He didn't have to. Vee sharpened it herself, sitting there — finishing the missing word in her own head where he'd left it out. *Your* friendship. They misunderstood *his.* It was never his face. A man who paid attention like that — who came into a room and was entirely inside it, as present over dishes as he'd be in the dark — a woman couldn't stay just his friend for long. It went one way or the other.

And she thought, wincing a little, of Theo, whom she'd kept at a careful friend's distance all last spring while he found reasons to fall into step beside her, and how there'd been a season of awkward in it before it settled, and how she'd been the one holding the line then. She knew the shape of it. She'd just never stood on his side of it.

"And the men," she said. Gently. She kept her voice a place he could put things down. "Not them?"

"Different reason." He leaned back against the counter, arms crossing, in it and not in it. "I went to college at fifteen."

"I know."

"Men make friends by competing." He said it like a fact he'd handled a long time, worn smooth. "Shoulder to shoulder, all measuring — thirteen, fourteen, fifteen, that's when it happens. When I was fifteen it didn't work because I was a boy in a building of grown men, off the end of every scale they measured on. Too small. Already somewhere else with girls. Too far ahead in the head to lose to anybody the ordinary way." He ticked it off with no heat. "You make a friend from the middle of the pack — you lose to him, and lose again, and come out the other side friends. I was never once in the pack."

He turned the next one over with no more weight than the first. "And when I was a man — eighteen, when the ones my own age finally showed up, freshmen — it didn't work because by then I was done. I had the patent. Money of my own, which is worse than having none. The house. They were starting the thing I'd already finished, and you don't build a friendship across that either." His hand turned over. "The window opens once. Men don't start at twenty-two — I didn't, anyway. That door's on a timer."

"That's—" she started, and he wasn't finished, and she let him have it.

"And the ones already there, when I was small among them," he said, and here the voice went the last degree flatter, the careful way he carried a thing that was full, "either weren't safe or weren't welcoming."

He didn't say a name. He didn't have to say a name.

She held very still with her tea going cold in her hands and did the arithmetic she hadn't been asked to do. *Weren't safe.* She had a rock in her, from a January night by a black window — *less than staying,* he'd said, when she'd asked wasn't he scared to leave home at fifteen, so plainly it had taken her a second to understand it was the worst thing he'd told her all night. *I was not a good fit where I was.* And Sheri, last night, low across a taproom table with her volume dropped like a coat: *went bad with his daddy. He hasn't been home since he was sixteen.* The pieces slid together and made a thing she was never going to be shown directly, and didn't need to be.

"It's a small thing to explain it with," he said, misreading her quiet for doubt, or not misreading it, "but people underrate it. Friendships get deep in the ordinary questions. *What are you doing for Christmas.* *Your folks coming up.* *You want to bring anybody.*" He put his hands flat on the counter and rocked his weight into them. "Every one of those has a gap under it, for me. You can't hand those back and forth easily, so the thing they're the doorway to doesn't get built. That's most of it. It isn't tragic. It's just a door that didn't open, and then you're grown."

*It isn't tragic.* He believed it, the way he'd believed the two years with Daphne had been luck. He set it in good order and the good order was the tell.

And the enormity of it came over Vee slowly, from underneath, the way the drink had come over her last night — late, all at once, and more than she'd counted. She thought about her own life, the actual marrow of it. Meg, whose closet she raided for anything that had to be braver than she was. Kayla, who reached across a dining-hall tray and took half her fries as tax, every time, without asking. Cassie, who'd hear out her whole heart on a Tuesday afternoon and say *oh, Vee,* in the voice that had already caught the part Vee hadn't said out loud. Randi, who she'd laughed with helpless on the carpet of a shoe store, who got the secrets, who did the triage on every heartbreak. One person couldn't be all of that. Nobody could hold that much of somebody.

He'd never had any of it. Not one. Not ever.

Because he'd had the one. Only the one, doing the work of all four, in a building full of grown men where there was no one else his own age at all — Daphne. And she'd carried more of him than a person should have to, and — Vee made herself look at it head-on — she'd had that much of him to begin with only because of the thing about it that was wrong, the thing Vee still would not set a word to. She tried to hold it as he held it, the best two years he'd had, and not as the ugly grown-up word that kept forming under her tongue and that she kept pressing back down. She tried to see it as he had lived it, and not as the wrong shape it had actually been, and she could only manage the two of them stacked and unreconciling, both true at once, and it hurt to hold them both. And then Daphne had gone — *sensibly,* he'd said, *it would have been stranger if she'd stayed* — and when she went, he'd had no one. Not one person left. No Randi to bring it to. The one had been all of it, and then the one was gone, and there was nobody left in the world to take the heartbreak to.

She could see the shape of it, she of all people — she'd been a freshman not so long ago and could still smell the dorm carpet. Eighteen was a whole world and she'd lived every inch of it: the cafeteria trays and the tables you fought your way onto, the roommate you loved or endured, freshman English at nine on no sleep, the whole loud clumsy business of starting — everyone starting at once, nobody having done any of it before. That was the year they *handed* you people your own age, a hundred to a lecture hall, forty to a dorm floor, all of you raw in the same way at the same time. It was where you got the ones you kept.

She set Pace down at eighteen beside all of it, and he fit nowhere in the picture. Not the lecture hall — a seminar of eight, and him the youngest in the room by years, teaching himself to cook because there was no dining hall and no one to eat with. He would not have sat in a single class with one person his own age — not one room, not one hour of that whole shared, starting year. The ones his age were a whole degree behind him, down in the world she'd just walked back through; the ones beside him were grown, and had done their own starting-out while he was still a child. He belonged to neither. He'd been handed a whole life a person is meant to grow into slowly, years before the growing, and left to eat it alone at a table for one.

*Not home since he was sixteen.* And she understood then how a boy got four years ahead of everyone his age so fast — you didn't go home for the summers; you stayed, and took the terms the others spent on a lake somewhere. She saw the campus empty around him one Thanksgiving, just for a second, and then set the image down before it could become something he'd have hated her for feeling.

She looked at him now, leaned against his own counter in his rolled sleeves — the man who'd baked her bread while she slept, and left water where she'd reach for it, and taken his mother out of the answer, months back, when she'd asked what his mother called him and he'd handed her his own name instead, *Peter,* and let that be all of it. His one friend on this earth a five-foot woman who could never want him — the only kind, she was sure now, the world had ever quite let him keep.

She got up. She came around the counter that had been between them the whole conversation, and she didn't say she was sorry, because sorry would have asked him to hold still and be comforted for a thing he'd handed her as plain fact. She just crossed the kitchen and put herself against him, both arms, all the way around, her cheek flat to his chest, the way Sheri had held him in the doorway of the taproom, with her whole weight committed.

His arms came around her after a moment. Not fast. He set his chin on the top of her head and looked out over it at something past her, the window or the far wall or the middle of the morning, and he wasn't happy and he wasn't sad. He was somewhere else with it, thinking, the way he got, and he held her and let her hold him and didn't try to make either one of them feel better, and outside the low light came in wrong and bright off last week's snow, and the bread cooled on the counter, and neither of them said anything for a while.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 12: Barely Stings — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (barely-stings.md) =====

The place Randi sent her did not look like a place that did what it did. It looked like somewhere you'd have a facial and an eighteen-dollar salad — low light the color of honey, a woman at the front desk with a voice pitched for libraries, a bowl of green apples by the register that nobody was meant to eat.

Two girls waited ahead of her on the low couch, both her age, both on their phones. One had come to be looked at: the fresh lashes, the balayage, the nails still tacky, a girl assembling herself for a room to turn toward. The other Vee understood only after a second, like a word surfacing in a language you almost speak — she had come to be touched. She had the loose warm look of a girl going home to someone, and she was here doing the private maintenance of that, the part nobody saw, the part that was for one set of hands. Vee sat down between them and understood which one she was.

"Can I get you something while you wait?" the desk woman asked. "We've got prosecco, or a white — the sauvignon blanc's nice."

She almost laughed. Almost said it — *you're going to want me drunk for this?* — the reflex right there, the joke that would set her a half-inch above the moment and let her look down at it. She took the wine instead. "The white. Thank you."

It was cold and a little sweet and she drank a third of it faster than wine was meant to be drunk, and felt it land, low and loosening, and was glad.

He had asked her three nights ago, in his kitchen, in the plain voice he used for facts about the world, and he had said it looking straight at her. He always looked straight at her — as if no part of her needed a man to find somewhere else to put his eyes.

"I'd like you waxed," he'd said. "By the weekend. Bare." And then he'd asked her — because he always asked; that was the thing about him that undid her, that a man who could simply have told her made it a question every time and waited for her to answer. His eyes stayed on hers. "Will you?"

"Yes," she'd said. Just yes, out ahead of her, her mouth answering before her mind had caught up to it — while she was still somewhere back down the sentence going *bare?*, going *the weekend?*, going *why.* He never told her why. He asked for the thing itself, clean, and left the place where the reason should be for her to fill, and she filled it, every time, with *yes, yes, tell me when.*

The fear came after. It always came after now, in private, once the yes was spent and couldn't be called back — that was nearly the point of it, the fear arriving too late to be any use. She'd lain awake with it. How much it would hurt. Where a person even went. What she'd have to do.

That part she'd asked Randi.

"Vivienne?" A different woman now, in the inner doorway, smiling. "Come on back."

The room was small and very clean, a bright that got into her teeth after the honey light out front. A table with paper pulled down the length of it. A cart. A warmer with something in it going slowly molten. She was handed a gown that was not really a gown and told to take everything off below the waist and lie down, and the woman stepped out to let her do it, which was somehow worse — the courtesy of the privacy, as though privacy were coming at all.

She folded her jeans. She folded her underwear inside the jeans where it couldn't be seen, which was ridiculous, and she knew it was ridiculous while she did it. She climbed up. The paper was loud under her. No stirrups — she'd braced for stirrups, some gynecological horror of them, and found none, just the flat table and her own two feet and the long light overhead, and she lay back under it with the not-gown across her lap and waited, and her mind, casting round for anywhere to be that wasn't here, went to Randi.

Tuesday, after stats, at Chi Latte — the yellow room, the white cups, the small ringing everywhere of spoons going round the insides of cups, the two of them up at the high window table like things in a display. Randi had that way of taking a piece of news that made you feel you'd handed her a present.

"Oh, you're going to *love* it," she'd said. "God, the first time. Don't let anybody but Sondra touch you — Draper Street, you ask for her by name, you tell them I sent you. She'll get you a glass of something first, she's civilized about it." She reached over and broke a corner off Vee's pastry — Randi never ordered her own, only ever *for the table,* or off whatever was in front of you.

"Does it hurt?" Vee had asked.

"Barely stings." A hand laid over Vee's on the table, warm, brief, sure. "So worth it, baby. You'll walk out of there feeling like the most expensive thing in the room."

*Barely stings.* Vee had believed her as she believed everything Randi told her about the country they both lived in that Randi had a passport for and Vee was still learning the language of. Randi was always one road further along. It was restful, being told.

Sondra came back snapping on gloves. She was warm at first — *how're we doing, first-timer? we'll take it easy* — warm in the professional way with a clock running under it, and then she settled onto her stool at the foot of the table and drew the not-gown up and away like a napkin lifted off a plate, and the warmth folded itself down into something level and unhurried and without any interest at all, and asked the question.

"So what're we doing today, hon. Full Brazilian, or—"

And there it was. She had to say it. Out loud, to a stranger with a good haircut, in a bright room, she had to put the want into words and hand it across.

"Um." Her voice had gone small. She heard it go small and couldn't stop it. "Everything. All of it. Bare."

Sondra nodded. Wrote nothing, changed nothing in her face, reached for the warmer — the nod you'd give a coffee order, and that was the worst of it, that the sentence which had cost Vee everything to say cost this woman nothing at all to hear. A word came up from the back of her mind in her mother's register, the flat kitchen voice that had been naming things for her since before she had names of her own: *the kind of girl who.* She shut her eyes on it. It stayed where it was. It always did.

The first of it was almost pleasant — warm, the wax laid on thick with a wooden stick, high up along the top, a spread of heat where she was coldest. A cloth pressed over it. A pause. She had time to think *that's not so—*

Sondra tore it off.

"*Fuck—*" It was out of her before she knew her mouth had opened, loud in the small room, and it hung there. She never talked like that. Under everything she was still a girl who said sorry to furniture. But no joke was quick enough for this, nothing clever to get out ahead of it — the wax took the words off with the hair, and what was left underneath with nothing over it was just that, just the plain animal size of it.

"Mm-hm," Sondra said, unmoved, already laying the next stripe. "First one's the surprise. You settle."

She did not settle so much as learn. Each strip its own white shock, and between them the deep radiant sting of where the last had been, and the woman working inward — patient, methodical, top and edges and then in, toward the center, toward the parts of herself she had no polite names for. And somewhere in it, she could not have said when, the thing she'd been holding out got in.

She thought about him. Of course she thought about him; he was the reason she was on the table. She was doing this for him. Was being taken apart and made smooth for him, would walk out of here bare because he had asked her bare — and the thought was meant to be a comfort and was the opposite of one, was a lit thing, it moved in her low and wrong, here, in the cold room, under the bored hands of a stranger, and she was appalled to feel it and felt it anyway.

Then a smaller horror on the heels of the first, a practical one: what the woman could see. From down there. Whether the body had gone and told on her already, done what it always did — announced her before she could get a hand over its mouth. She lay very still and did not let herself look at Sondra's face for the answer.

That was when she squirmed — a flinch inward, one knee wanting to close, her hips lifting off the paper — not from the pain, or not only, but from the other thing, the wanting-to-hide of it. And a hand came down flat on her thigh and pressed her back down to the table, firm, exact.

"Don't move."

It was not unkind. It was not anything. It was a hand keeping a part of her still so a job could be done to it, wanting nothing from her, and that was the whole trouble — because her body knew that grammar. Knew a hand pressing it down and telling it to hold still, had learned that grammar in a warm dark room from a man who meant it, and here was the shape of it with everything true scooped out of the middle. The wrong hand. It held her the exact way she wanted to be held and it did not want her at all, and what it did, pinning her under the flat of its palm, was open a hole in her a mile across where the right hand should have been. She lay under the wrong hand and ached for the right one and gave the woman nothing — no sound, no flinch, kept the whole lit ruin of herself back behind her teeth where it belonged to him.

Then the last of it, the worst of it. Sondra's fingers took hold of her — moved her, spread her, set her where she needed her with the flat impersonal competence of a woman handling a thing that wanted handling — and laid the wax where Vee was most herself, and Vee stared up into the light and breathed and could not breathe, and the strip came away, and then the other side, and it was so far past the earlier pain that it lived in a different country, a white place with no words anywhere in it. Her eyes ran. Not crying. Just the body wringing itself out at the corners.

"There," Sondra said. "That's the hard part behind you."

And Vee let herself believe her — let the relief come up through her like water, *it's over, I did it* — and that was the false floor, because Sondra capped the warmer and set down the stick and reached for a fresh cloth and said, easy:

"Okay. Roll over for me, hon. Onto your front."

Vee didn't move. "…Over?"

"The back and the rest of it. It's all the one service." Mild, already waiting, a gloved hand light on Vee's hip to start her turning — as though this were a thing everyone knew, a thing that went without saying, a thing only Vee had somehow failed to understand she'd been agreeing to.

There it was. The part Randi hadn't said. *Barely stings,* and the glass of wine, and *the most expensive thing in the room,* and not one word about this — about rolling over, about the rest of it, about a part of herself she had honestly never once in her life thought of as a thing that got *done*. She could say no. The word was right there. She could sit up and say no, that's enough, that isn't — and she did not. She'd already said yes to the whole of it, in a bright kitchen three nights back, without once asking what the whole of it was; she was already here, already bare in front, already so far past every line she'd have thought to draw that another line seemed like a joke, a thing for a girl who had any lines left. So she rolled over.

She put her face down in the paper. She couldn't see the woman now, couldn't set her own face, could do nothing but lie there and be turned and adjusted and opened and wait, presented, blind, for wherever the hands went next. No wanting in this one. Nothing lit, nothing for him, nothing to keep back. Just the plain fact of what she was letting be done to her, and the small ruthless voice out of the kitchen naming it, and the wax going on, and Vee holding still — holding still on her own now, nobody had to tell her — while it came off.

And then it was over, truly over, and Sondra wiped her down brisk and not unkind and gathered the strips and peeled off her gloves and told her to take her time getting up, nodded at the lotion on the cart, and went out.

The room ticked in the quiet. Vee lay a while. Then she sat up, slow, and swung her legs down, and — alone now, no one's hands on her, no one watching — put her own hand there, light, on the bare astonishing smoothness of herself, and the sound she made was not one of pain.

It was so smooth. Smooth in a way skin was not meant to be, new, unbearable, and she drew her fingertips over it once and went weak at how it felt — and then she was not thinking about her own hand at all. She was thinking about his. About the weekend, about the moment he'd find her like this: his hand, the right hand, the one she'd kept everything back for, learning by touch what she had gone and done for him. She wanted it so badly it was its own kind of ache, a cleaner one than the other. She got dressed around it.

She left the second glass they offered. She walked out through the honey light past the girl still waiting to be looked at, and she was — Randi had been right about that much — the most expensive thing in the room, and no one in it knew but her.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 13: Still Life — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (still-life.md) =====

The drive back from the salon took seven minutes. Vee spent six of them sitting very straight.

In the dorm shower she turned the water as hot as it would go and stood under it until the bright room and the paper on the table and Sondra's gloved hands had gone thin around the edges. The sting had settled to a low radiant tenderness. When she washed there, carefully, the skin under her fingers was so smooth that her hand stopped.

Bare. Entirely. The water moved over her with nothing to catch on, and when she passed her palm across herself again the feeling went straight through her knees. This was what his hand would find. His cheek. His mouth. She had thought about his mouth on the drive; she had been thinking about it for three nights, if she was honest, ever since he asked. But now her own hand showed her how much she had failed to imagine. His lips would go where her fingertips went. His tongue would have all this impossible smoothness under it. She put one hand flat to the tile and stood in the water breathing, more aroused than she'd ever been while doing nothing but getting ready.

Getting ready for him. That was what the shower was. What the whole afternoon had been.

She made herself turn off the water.

Her panties were worse. The first pull of cotton up her thighs brought the fabric directly over skin that had never met it before, and she had to stop with them halfway and wait out the clean soft drag of it. When she got them the rest of the way on, every step moved them. She put on the green silk, then her coat, and carried the new feeling down the hall and out to the car with her, secret and continuous.

Pace opened his front door before she knocked.

He came out onto the covered stoop as though the last three feet were too far to leave between them, smiling already, and she went into him hard enough to rock him back on one heel. His arms closed around her. He laughed into her hair and lifted her clear off the boards, once, a great unnecessary hug that had no sex in it except that it was him and her and everything between them had sex somewhere inside it now.

"Hi," he said when he set her down, still holding her.

"Hi."

He looked at her face. Just her face first, glad and direct, his hands warm through the back of her coat. She saw the next look arrive only after that one had finished: the small darkening when she pressed closer and he felt what the greeting had done to both of them.

She kissed him.

The kiss went in through the door with them. She pushed it shut behind his back, kissed him across the foyer, and kept him moving when the hall opened on the left. He followed laughing, one hand on her ass and the other finding her breast through the coat, past the bathroom, past the office, around the corner. She knew where she was going. She had spent the whole drive knowing.

But she liked it — that was the truth under the nerves, and for once she let it stand. Any other night a skirt this short would have put her hand to the hem and her eyes to the floor, too much of her showing, all of it asking to be looked at. Tonight the skirt was Randi's. Randi dressed like this and walked into rooms as if she'd built them; in Randi's skirt the wanting wasn't hers to answer for. She caught the slide of the sheen in the strip of mirror — the bare bright length of her leg, the shape the cloth made of her — and held her own eyes there instead of dropping them. She turned once more just to bring back the hot, low pull of it: of being about to be looked at and for once not minding. Of wanting to be.

"No Pace tonight?" Cassie asked the textbook, turning a page she wasn't reading.

"He gets me tomorrow." Vee smiled. "Tonight I'm Randi's date."

Cassie's eyebrow went up a half-inch and stayed there. Whatever it was for, she didn't say it, and Vee, already most of the way into the night, didn't ask her to.

---

The room was the one cool place in the building, because Cassie kept the window cracked its two inches against a furnace nobody could throttle; the hall, when Vee stepped into it, was the furnace winning — heat standing in it like water, close and total, so that she was glad of the bare legs before she'd gone ten steps. She went down it in the borrowed shoes and was surprised again, as she'd been surprised all evening, by how they held her. High and firm and built, a real spine in them, nothing like the soft flat things she owned that let her foot sprawl and do as it liked. These told the foot what to do. She'd expected to suffer in them and instead she stood up out of them an inch taller and better made, her own ankles somehow more like ankles, and she carried herself down the hot hall like that, held.

The elevator took her down with a lurch and a sigh and threw her back at herself in the brushed steel of the doors — a warped strip of bare leg, of the skirt's oil-on-water sheen, of dark hair down — and then opened on the foyer, where she had stood a dozen times waiting for Pace to come in out of the cold and find her. Tonight someone else was already there, in the spot by the desk where Vee's eye went looking for him out of pure habit. Randi, waiting. And the sight of her stopped Vee where she stood.

She had the aqua on — strapless, bare to the shoulders, cut high on the thigh — and she wore it as only the very small and very finished can, so that it looked engineered around her and not bought. Her shoulders were pale and bare and exact, the arms of a girl trained to hold a line and hold it; the run of her from jaw to collarbone to the straight top edge of the dress was all one clean unbroken thing, a dancer's body said in a single sentence. Her hair was off her neck entirely, drawn back into a French braid so tight and precise it didn't read as braided so much as inlaid — laid down strand over strand, every pass identical, a thing of lacquer and geometry, gleaming like something machined. Nothing soft on her anywhere. No scrunchie tonight; this was the other Randi, the one the ordinary days never got.

And the color. Vee had known, pointing at it in the closet, that the aqua would do something for Randi's eyes — but not this. The dress and the eyes were the same startling pale water-blue, the blue running straight up out of the neckline and into her face until you couldn't say where the cloth stopped and the girl started, and Vee just looked, and went on looking, until she heard herself.

"Your shoulders. Randi — look at you. That dress is unreal, you're unreal, I hate you a little. I genuinely do."

Randi laughed, lit up, and reached out and took a hank of Vee's hair and ran it slow through her fingers like money. "Listen to her. Look at this color, baby — you cannot buy this, girls pay a fortune to fake exactly this and get it wrong. And you're wearing it *down*." She drew it once over Vee's shoulder and stood back to look, pleased with her. "Gorgeous. I'm going to stand next to you all night and live off what spills."

Vee's hair was down and flat-ironed and still wouldn't fully obey — it had a will in it, curving where it wanted, warm and red and alive against all that lacquered black — and under Randi's hand the old easy pleasure rose in her, of being someone's good thing, handled and approved, and she grinned like a fool. And close like this she caught the rest of it: the mouth done in a deep berry drawn so smooth you couldn't find the seam where line met fill — steady-handed, unhurried, expensive in a way Vee could price to the dollar and never once pay; the white gold at Randi's ears, small and hard and throwing light; the one stone at her throat on a chain so fine it vanished; the plain heavy band always at her wrist — all of it the same cool pale metal, chosen to lie quiet against that cool pale skin and never fight it. Even her jewelry agreed with her.

They coated up at the doors. Randi first and fast, hugging a soft wrap coat to herself and already mourning the walk, because Randi ran cold, a tiny girl who felt every degree of it and made it known; Vee got into her own and was fine, the tall one, the warm-blooded one of the pair. Then out — and the cold came straight up under the scrap of skirt and pebbled the whole length of her bare thighs into gooseflesh, a fast involuntary accounting of just how little of her the thing covered. She laughed, her breath going white. "It's not a skirt. It's a rumor."

"It's working, isn't it." Randi took her arm and pulled her in, warm all down that side. "Cold's good. Cold gets us inside faster." And they went toward it like that, arm in arm and out of step and laughing at nothing, the campus hushed and lamplit and mostly theirs, the brick breathing its winter up at them, the bar a block off past the gates with its windows already gone gold — two girls dressed for the party and not the cold, the whole night still in front of them.

The bracelet was at Vee's eye again where their arms were linked, catching the streetlight, that plain heavy band that never came off. "I love, *love* your bracelet," she said.

Randi laughed.

"What?"

"Because, gorgeous — the bracelet is called Love." She turned her wrist so the light slid along it.

Vee leaned to see. It sat close around the wrist, no clasp she could find, surely too narrow to clear the round of Randi's hand. "It looks too small to come over your hand. How do you even take it off?"

"Oh, you don't." Randi said it lightly, fond, already looking ahead to the gold windows. "It's a real commitment. Taking it off requires tools."

And Vee laughed, charmed, and tucked it away with the thousand glamorous things Randi knew that she didn't, and they came up on the light with the cold at their backs and the noise of the place leaking out under the door — talk and laughter and low music beneath it, the warmth of a room already full — and Randi reached for the door.

The door gave, and the cold dropped off her back, and the warmth she'd heard leaking under it took her whole — heat and gold light and the close press of a room filled past comfortable, the talk of it breaking over her louder than any music. No one was dancing. The middle of the floor lay open and lit and empty, a stretch of board with no reason yet to stand on it; whatever it was that started the dancing hadn't started. Everything was at the edges — the bar going two and three deep, the knots of people, the bare arms and bright dresses and the sweet warm reek of a hundred perfumes.

And then they were inside it and it closed over them, and a sister caught Randi by the wrist and said something delighted into her ear and was gone, and another put a cup into Vee's hand — "you look frozen, here" — so that she had a drink she hadn't gone looking for and the warm enclosing fact of being, for one night, let right inside.

The cup was running off a galvanized tub at the end of the bar, the sort of thing you'd haul ice in, and the punch in it was the deep pink of something a house would serve out of a trash can on purpose. Vee drank. It was sweet and cold and easy, the warmth of it arriving a beat late and low, and it tasted like just what it looked like, which was nothing — party punch, a hundred of them, the kind you don't keep.

Randi's mouth came to her ear, bright with it. "You know what's in that?" Vee shook her head. "Chambord. She runs *Chambord* through it — forty dollars a bottle, God knows how many of them — into a tub, where not one soul in this room will taste it or thank her for it." She pulled back enough to give Vee the look, fond and withering at once. "That's Brooke all over. She'll spend a fortune to have you think she didn't spend a dime."

And Vee, who could cross a department store and read the price off anything in the air, who knew to the dollar what she would never have, turned the cheap pink cup in her hand and could not find the money in it to save her life. She'd have drunk it down and never known. It took Randi to tell her what she was holding.

A boy with a beer made his way over through the crush — tall, easy, a face she half-knew from some class or hallway, no name to go with it — and got close enough to be heard. He'd clocked the skirt from across the room; she could tell by how carefully he kept his eyes up now that he'd arrived.

"You here with anyone?"

Before Vee could find the answer, Randi was there — a hand settling light at the small of her back and staying — and gave it for her, easy and bright. "She's here with me."

He took it in: his eyes going from Vee to Randi and back, the redhead and the little brunette, Randi's hand where it was, and the slow arrival of a question he couldn't quite get to. "Both of you," he said, thrown. "Neither one of you—" and didn't finish it, and laughed at himself instead, the laugh a good-natured boy gives when he's walked into something he can't quite read.

And then they were both laughing — Randi first, delighted, pulling Vee into it with the hand at her back; and Vee caught it and laughed too, the easy conspiratorial joy of being on Randi's side of it, the two girls a boy couldn't work out. He lifted his beer, conceding the thing, and let the crowd take him back. And Vee — spoken for, taken off the market without lifting a finger, Randi's hand warm and sure against her spine — didn't think of him again.

She came at them out of the crowd smile-first — Vee got the teeth before anything else, an almost-blue white, then the butterscotch tan and the gold lamé and the lacquered blonde of her all at once — and swept Vee into a hug before she could set her cup down, then held her back at arm's length by the shoulders to look at her.

Vee started to say her name.

"Hello — you're *Vee*." Brooke had it before she could spend it. "Randi's *new* friend." A squeeze of the shoulders, delighted. "Stats, the two of you, all fall. And the smoothie shop — that cute little cardigan, I never forget a thing — and then you came by the house one Friday, oh, *early*, half of us still in our robes, and you needed our Randi like the world was ending."

Vee felt Randi's shoulder come warm against her arm — there, where a second before there had been nothing.

She was lit all through: the president of the whole house carried her little history by heart. And Vee had braced, without quite meaning to, for the next of it, the day two weeks back when she'd come in off the cold too wrecked to get out a hello, braced to hear it read out into the room. But Brooke hadn't touched it. The gratitude went through her warm on top of everything else, and she thought that Brooke, whom she'd always found a little frightening, was kind after all. "It's such a good party," she said. "Thank you so much for having me."

"Of *course*, sweetheart." The smile widened by some exact amount, the whole face switching on behind it. "Any friend of Randi's." And the gold of her turned and closed back into the crowd, leaving the warmth where she'd stood like something you could smell.

The low music had been thickening under everything, a floor laid down a little at a time, and then the lights dropped and a beat came up out of the dark big enough to feel in the breastbone, and the whole room turned to it at once with a noise that was half a scream. The empty middle was suddenly the only place to be. Randi had her by the hand already, towing her toward it, lit up — "Come on. Come *on*—" — and the floor took them both.

---

After a few sets they came off the floor flushed and laughing, holding each other up by the forearms, and went to the bar for something cold. Randi ordered a margarita, and Vee — watching the salt, the pale cloudy green of it, the sweet-and-sour that would carry the liquor down so gently you barely met it — said she'd have the same. She had meant to drink champagne tonight; she'd decided on it days ago, the way you decide what kind of girl you'll be at a party you're nervous about. She was glad already she'd followed Randi instead.

The music made talking a contact sport. You got a sentence in the gap between songs, and after that there was only the cupped hand, the mouth brought to the ear, the half-shouted nothing you laughed at because the laughing was the point. Randi said things into Vee's ear and Vee felt the words more than heard them, the warm shape of the breath, and said things back, and they sat close the way the room made everyone sit close. At some point a second margarita was in front of her; she hadn't seen Randi order it. She had never been so easy in her own skin in a room full of strangers.

They'd kept to the edge of the floor — the middle was a crush, but the edge gave you room to move, and it looked out over the long tables and the clots of people who'd come to watch more than dance. Which was the trouble with the skirt. It climbed. On the floor it climbed by the song, and every inch it took was an inch handed to all those faces past the edge, the ones not dancing, the ones with nothing to do but look. Vee kept a hand low on her thigh to hold the hem down and brought her arms up only in quick bursts before snatching them back. She'd put the green thong on that night, after her shower, getting ready for these exact people, when it had been nobody's business but hers. By the hour, on this floor, it was coming nearer to being everybody's. She got good, over the night, at the arithmetic of it — how high was still a joke, how high stopped being one — and she kept half an eye down and a hand always ready.

Then the song came on that the whole room had been waiting for. A shriek went up; the floor pulled bodies out of every corner; and Randi was on her feet with both of Vee's hands in hers. "Oh — no, you can't miss this," she said, walking backward, pulling, and Vee went, because the alternative was not going, and there was no version of the night in which she chose that.

She'd been dancing small for an hour, elbows in, the radius of herself kept modest, and Randi wouldn't have it. "No. Like *this*." She spun in behind her and put both hands warm on the curve of Vee's hips, the roundness of them, and moved them — wider, looser, an arc Vee would never have given herself — so the dancing got bigger and the skirt got braver in exact measure. And Randi's hands stayed where they were. There was nowhere left for Vee's own hand to go that wouldn't have looked like just what it was.

The song climbed into the part where everyone's arms go up, the whole floor a field of them, and Randi's hands left her hips and went up the sides of her — slow, over the thin fabric, past the soft give beneath her arms, along the undersides to her wrists — and lifted them, and held them in the air. Her thighs were open to the room now, the iridescent skirt giving them almost nothing, and there was nothing Vee could do about it, because Randi had her hands. Randi fit against her back, the damp silk of her, the heat through it, and Randi did not so much keep the beat as work it into her — a small restless wriggle of hips that traveled up through Vee whether she wanted it or not — and somewhere under the noise and the heat and the two margaritas, her body was working through something her mind had decided not to check.

Then Randi turned her — a flourish, hand finding hand, her fingers going through Vee's and closing, slick palm to slick palm — and danced her out to arm's length and pumped their joined hands back and forth, big and ridiculous and joyful, and ran Vee's whole body on the end of that grip, and Vee let herself be run, laughing, helpless with it. The hem was high. She could feel how high by the cool that reached the very top of her thigh, the give of air where no air should have been, and she knew that she had crossed some line a few inches back and was still going, because the only way to stop was to let go of Randi's hands and walk out of the bright middle of it and be, again, a careful person at the edge of a party. She did not let go.

The song broke. The floor cheered itself hoarse. Randi reeled her in by the hands she was still holding — close, closer than even the noise required — and put her mouth to Vee's ear, so that Vee felt the lips, the actual lips, brush the rim of it, and a shiver went down the side of her neck and into her shoulder before she could rule on whether she'd allowed it.

"Your panties," Randi said, warm, amused, only for her, "are showing."

Vee's hands came down and dragged the hem to where it should have been, too late, far too late. How long had it been up — a whole song? Longer? The edge of the floor was all faces, and she'd been turned to hand every one of them the back of her, the green, the almost-nothing. What had they seen? How much had she shown? The thong didn't even try to hide the roundness of her bottom. How much of her center had they seen? Some girl dancing up past the edge of her own skirt, flushed, letting another girl run her around by the wrists — what kind of floozie did that, in front of a room, and didn't even feel it going? She felt it now. It climbed her face, hot, and she waited for it to be only awful. It was not only awful. Underneath the shame, low, where she didn't look, something turned the heat over into the other thing, the thing she had no business feeling about a roomful of strangers and the back of a borrowed skirt — and she stood there in the broken-off music with her hem in both fists, found out twice over: once to the room, and once, worse, to herself.

"Hey — *hey*. Don't." Randi caught her chin and brought Vee's eyes up off the floor and onto her. "They already looked — and believe me, nobody minded." The grin came up then, bright, conspiratorial. "There's Harrison. Let's put on a show — make him wish he were *me*." And she was already turning Vee by the hand, back toward the room.

Harrison was across the room — a tall boy with a beer and a face Vee half-knew and didn't need to, and that was the whole use of him: he was over there, and he could be performed at. Randi tucked in against Vee's side, hip to hip, tipped her head toward him — the oldest joke at any party, two girls being silly for a boy — and then, grinning right at Harrison, she laid her hand flat on Vee's ass and took a slow, theatrical handful of it. A bit. A punchline. Vee felt the grip of it through the thin skirt, the warm certain spread of Randi's fingers, and understood that the joke and the thing were not going to be the same size.

Then Randi's other hand came up to her jaw and slid back into her hair and closed there, and drew her down — Vee was the taller of them; she had to lean, had to give herself to the angle — and then, for a moment, did nothing at all.

She held Vee there, a breath short of her mouth, and the held inch became its own long event. Vee had never had Randi's face at this distance, and her eyes, asking no permission, went over it as they had wanted to all autumn and never let themselves — the dark certainty of the brows, the lashes, the mouth half-open and waiting, near enough now to be the only thing there was. Randi's breath came warm across her lips, and Vee's lips moved toward it on their own. And the smell of her arrived all at once and from everywhere — something floral and expensive in her hair, the perfume beneath it that Vee had been quietly cataloguing for months without admitting she was keeping the file, and under all of it the warm sweet near-nothing of Randi's own breath — and it turned in Vee like a key, and the music stepped back, and the room stepped back, and there was a suspended moment in which nothing in the world was happening except that she was about to be kissed by Randi and wanted it more than she had let herself want anything.

Then Randi closed the inch and touched her mouth to Vee's, and held it there, still — just the press, just the warm fact of it — and let Vee feel it, and feel herself not pull away.

It started like the goodbye, the familiar shape of it, the mouth that had been finding hers at the end of every brunch — and then it didn't stop where goodbye stopped. It went on. Randi's lips formed slowly against hers, opening, unhurried, thorough, and Vee's parted to meet them before the question reached her, months of small uncounted wanting surfacing all at once; and a sound came up out of her, low, helpless, lost in the music.

Because it was not just a girl, not just the bit. It was Randi. Randi's hand fisted warm in her hair; Randi's other hand stayed low, kneading, owning the curve of her through the skirt; and Vee leaned down into it and gave it back, her breasts crushed to Randi's, the smaller hard points of her pressed to Vee's own, the thin fabrics between them the only thing she could still feel that she wanted gone. Time had quit behaving. The kiss had been going on forever and had only just begun, and she could not have said which, and the not-being-able-to-say was the whole of where she lived now. There was no floor. There was no Harrison, no show, no word for what this was or wasn't, no right and no wrong and no one watching — there was the seam of their mouths and the slow generous work Randi made of it and the heat letting down through her until she had gone slick and aching with it, her body making the same humiliating announcement it always made, no way now to call it back, and making it for Randi — and her hips pressing once to Randi's before she could stop them, asking.

Randi's tongue came then — not a demand, a *please* — and touched Vee's, and withdrew, and returned; and Vee opened to it and met it, and the taste of her arrived, salt and lime and under it Randi, particular, the specific *her* of her, and it went through Vee in one long electric line and she heard herself moan into Randi's mouth and could not have found the part of herself that would have minded. She wanted Randi's hands under the skirt. She wanted Randi's mouth lower, Randi somewhere they could never be in this room — a want so far past the size of its excuse that it frightened her even as she sank further into it. Her own hand had gone up into the warm weight of Randi's hair without asking her and pulled her deeper, and she did not take it back.

When Randi finally drew back it was by degrees, unhurried even in the leaving, the lower lip last, a final small print of pressure Vee felt go all the way down. And the world returned in the order it had gone — her mouth, then the taste of Randi on it, then the music, then the lights, then the faces, all those faces she had utterly forgotten — and she could not make herself climb back up into caring about them, because her body was still wide open and ringing and clamoring for the rest of it, for everything, for what it had been handed the smallest taste of and would now have to do without, no knowing for how long. Her mouth was still parted. Her heart slammed like she'd run. She had not been performing — somewhere in there she had stopped entirely — and the want the kiss had let off its chain did not go meekly back on it.

Randi came back from it a moment behind. She didn't speak — only looked at Vee, and drew a breath, and shook her head, blinking. Then the grin came up. "Oh, that was *perfect*." She had Vee's hand again, squeezing it. "Did you *see* him? He's going to be thinking about that for a week." She put her mouth to Vee's ear, warm, close, where it had been all night. "You really did a great job putting on a show. I almost believed it."

Then the swat on the arm, light, fond, and she was already turning them back toward the noise and the drinks, the whole night handed back neat and folded: two girls who'd teased a boy and done it well.

Vee took it, because it was the only thing held out to her. She heard herself say *god, his face* in a voice that knew how to do this even now, and laughed, and the laugh came out sounding like the room. But her mouth still had the taste of Randi on it, and her body had not been told. It went on ringing under the easy voice, open, still sure there was more coming, with nowhere to put any of it. She knew almost nothing, standing there in the noise — not what she'd done, or what it had been, or what came next. She knew she had kissed Randi. She knew it had been good. She knew she wanted to do it again so badly her hands were not quite steady, and that there was no version of the night in which she could ask.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 15: Boyfriend — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (boyfriend.md) =====

She walked back. The campus was never quite empty, but the paths were mostly hers now, lamplit and hushed, the party already a block behind and falling away. The cold came up off the brick and never reached her. Her body was warm clean through, lush and alive, strumming with the evening, and it had not settled and gave no sign that it meant to. It had given her away in that room full of people, Randi's mouth on hers and Randi's hand low on her, and it had not taken it back. Under the borrowed skirt, the whole way home, it stayed with her.

It was late when she let herself in. The room was dark and cold the way Cassie kept it, the window cracked its two inches on the January outside, the lower bunk breathing slow and even, Cassie long down and gone. Vee got the skirt off over her hips and laid it across the back of the chair, and stood a moment looking at it lying there, iridescent, and shook her head at that bad, troublesome thing, as if it were the only thing that had misbehaved tonight.

Then the thong. Where it had soaked through it had stuck to her, along the sides. She slid a finger up under the side seam and drew it slowly down, loosening the fabric from her skin as she went. And drawing it down was how she came onto the new bare of her — her fingertip running down over it and stopping there, without being told to, before she'd thought to be ready for it.

A day bare now, and still a surprise, still that new astonishing smoothness — that Sondra's terrible patient hands had made and Pace had spent the afternoon learning by mouth, the thing she'd gone and done for him and no one else. She was wet under it and had been for hours; the evidence of it appeared on her fingers. And the picture that came was not the afternoon, and not his hand at all. It was the dancefloor. It was Randi's hands moving her wide, Randi's mouth at her ear and then not at her ear, and — sharp, absurd, impossible to stop — the thought of what Randi would make of *this,* the new smoothness, what Randi's mouth would do to it if it ever got down there and found her bare as this, made this way, an offering with the wrong name on it. Her hips tilted toward the idea before the idea had finished being one.

She stopped. She took the hand back and held it against her own breastbone like something she'd caught stealing.

*I have a boyfriend,* she told the dark, and it was such a strange new word to reach for, *boyfriend,* a word for a girl who had one and only one, a plain straight fact of a word, and she reached for it the way you reach for the rail on a stair. She didn't need to do this herself. It was silly to do it herself. She had somebody who was better at it than she would ever be, who was twenty minutes down a county road and would open the door.

She was already reaching for the skirt again.

She stepped back into it — Randi's, the stretchy iridescent knit, off the back of the chair — and drew it up, and it settled on her the way it had all night, close and bright and not enough. *He'd want to see me in this,* she thought, and did not look at the thought too hard. She left the thong where it was, stuck to her, never got the rest of the way off — she could have been out of it in a second and she wasn't, and she wanted it there and wouldn't ask herself why.

She got her coat and her keys, quiet, and Cassie never stirred, and Vee let herself out into the furnace hall and pulled the door soft behind her.

---

The Corolla took the cold badly and so did she, bare-legged on the seat her father wanted her to keep the mileage off of, the heater breathing lukewarm on her shins a mile too late to matter. Town went by shut and orange. Then the campus lights fell behind and the road went to the black county straightaway, only her high beams and the flat dark fields either side, the white line coming and coming, and she drove it faster than she should have and did not once turn the radio on, because she did not want anything in the car with her that might start asking questions.

His driveway was the cut in the hill you missed if you didn't know it, and she knew it now and still nearly went past, braking hard onto the gravel and doubling back, tires crackling up to the pad. The white truck sat where it always sat. The little house held back through the trees with no light showing, none, not even the low lamp — dark all the way through, and cold-looking in a way she felt and didn't stop to read, and the porch sconce black over a shut door.

She rang the bell before the sense could catch up to the wanting — and then again, too soon for manners.

A long nothing. Then a lamp somewhere, and a nearer one, and the bolt, and the door came open on him in boxer shorts and nothing else, his hair pushed sideways from the pillow and his face still half in sleep and going from *who* to *her*, even as she watched.

"Vee." The name came out of him glad, even half-asleep, before he was awake enough to wonder why — and then he took her in, the bare legs, the skirt, the hour, the whole wrecked look of her, and got as far as, "It's the middle of the—"

"I can't wait," she said, over him, into him, her hand already down inside his boxers and closing on him. "I need you." And she did. She needed him the way you need a wall at your back — the plain male fact of him, the size and the heat and the flat certainty of what he'd do to her — to crowd the other thing out of her head, to fill her so full of him there'd be no room left for the picture that would not leave her alone. That was all this was. She was wound up, was all. She was just — she was just horny, a body that had been going all night and wanted seeing to. She kissed the rest of his sentence back into his mouth and walked him backward into his own dark house.

---

She was not careful. She had come to him wired before, more than once, too keyed-up to wait, and he had always liked it, the nights she couldn't get through the door fast enough. She told herself this was that. But this had a different engine in it, an ugly one she'd never run at him before: she was not trying to have him. She was trying to get free of something, and her body went about it like a fight.

He caught up fast, the way he always did, but she did not let him do the thing he did — the slow reading, the mouth and the hands, the once or twice he liked to bring her over before he was ever inside her. Not tonight. She did not want to be tended, did not want to be read. She wanted — no, she needed — him hard and inside her, nothing before it. She took him down the short hall past the black doorways and around the corner to the bed, and there was nothing tender anywhere near it — it was hard and it had teeth. She drove herself down onto him like she could grind the thing loose against the plain fact of him, work it out of her, leave it somewhere in the dark between their bodies. Not once, on any other night she'd come to him wild, had she needed him to drive someone else out of her head. She came with her face turned into his neck so she wouldn't have to see him see her.

He held still under her after, still inside her, one hand open on her back, not stroking, just resting, and she felt the attention come up in him in the dark, unhurried and complete.

"What was that?" he said.

"What was what?"

"That." A beat. "That wasn't you wound up — I know that one. That was angry, Vee." His hand stayed open on her back, not moving. "Did something happen at the mixer?"

She didn't answer right away. She kept her face in his neck. He already knew — not *what,* but that there was a what; he'd felt it in how she'd used him, and there was no folding that back up. And under the being-caught, worse than it, was the other thing: some part of her was already reaching for this, dying to set the whole weight of it down in front of somebody. She cared, terribly, who the somebody was. There was one person in the world she told her secrets to, one she'd have carried this to — and that was the one person on earth it could never go to, because it *was* her. So it had gone nowhere, all night, only built; and now here was Pace, who'd felt it in the dark and asked, holding open the one door there was. The caught part of her and the dying-to-tell part reached for it together.

The other one would sooner have gone in the ground with it. *This does not get said,* it said, flat, from behind a shut door. *Not this. Not ever.*

He waited. The waiting did what his hands did, and the door came open anyway.

"It started as a joke," she said into his skin, low, paying it out a piece at a time. "For Harrison." A breath. "We kissed."

"Harrison," he said. Just the name, handed back.

And it landed wrong the instant he had it — a boy's name in her own mouth, at a party, in the dark — and she understood too late what she'd built him: a clean little picture with a *him* in the middle of it. Pace lying here believing she'd kissed some boy. It wasn't that the picture was worse than the truth. It was that it would hurt him — a false thing lodged in his chest, hurting him for no reason — and she couldn't leave it in there. The truth tore loose to kill it.

"Not *him.* God, not—" no floor left anywhere, "—it was Randi. I kissed Randi." It cost her the whole breath. "Or she kissed me—" and then the part that made it hers, the part she made herself say too, "—and I kissed her back."

"It was a joke," she said, once more, quiet, like setting a stone down. And the real thing, the one that had no words she'd ever once let herself use, pressed up hard behind her teeth and did not get out — and what came out instead, quieter still, was the truth wearing the joke's own clothes. "It didn't stay a joke."

---

He hadn't slipped out of her, not once — and somewhere in the *it didn't stay a joke* he'd gone hard again, and gone still, and she could feel the whole of him up inside her. She should have been annoyed. He'd gone hard off her shame, off the worst thing she had. But she wasn't. She was glad — glad in some low, steadying way that grounded her, put her back down into her own body and made it hers again. That part still worked, then. She was a girl on top of her boyfriend at one in the morning, and it was simple and it was *straight,* and she could stand on it to say the rest.

She lifted her face out of his neck and looked at him.

"How did this happen?" he said.

Not a scold. Not *how could you.* He asked it easy, almost curious. There was a current under it she felt through where they were joined, low and steady, and it was only a man who'd heard *two girls* and wanted the rest — the ordinary greed of that, nothing she couldn't handle. So she handed him the ordinary version, the one with the exits built in. "It was the joke, I told you — the Harrison thing, it was a *bit,* we were just—"

He slid her up the length of him. He'd done it a hundred times, his hands moving her, helping her work herself on him, and she loved it in the moment, the long slow friction of the drag up and the drag back. So she took the lift for what it always was and rode it up, easy. And then at the top he stalled her. Held her there, off all but the head of him, his strong hands cupping her ass, not letting her back down. That was new. She pressed against them to sink onto him again and couldn't, and a sound came out of her, disappointed, before she'd thought about it.

"Tell me" — his voice easy — "the real story, and I'll give you what you want."

It took her a second to catch the shape of it: that he meant to make her buy it back, inch by inch, pay for her own pleasure in the one coin she had. A laugh got out of her, nervous, high. "That's *cheating,*" she said, thick, nowhere near offended.

"A little." He wasn't sorry at all — the warmth of him with a new thing folded in, a hunger he wasn't troubling to hide — and it only turned the screw tighter. Something in her leaned its whole weight on the rule he'd set — and it held.

"*Fine.*" She rolled her hips against the hold, testing it, and gave him something true to see what it bought. "I went as her date."

She'd meant it flat — it was only the word for the thing, a plus-one, a favor. But he let her down an inch for it, one exact inch, and the inch made her hear the word again, hear it the way he'd just heard it: that she had gone on a *date.* With Randi. It came back warmer and worse than she'd said it, and she slowed on him, feeling it, feeling him feel it.

"It's the mixer that kicks off spring semester. She gets a plus-one, and she spent it on me." It came easier once it was moving. "She could've asked anyone. She said guys you have to manage all night, and me she just gets to *have.*"

He pushed her all the way down onto him for that, slow, the whole length, till she was seated and full and breathing around it. Above her, his face took plain pleasure in it — another game, running just as he liked. And it happened again, worse: she heard what she'd just said, what it meant that Randi just got to *have* her, and the heat climbed, and she could not have told you how much of it was the thought and how much was him buried to the root in her. Then he drew her back up, emptying her out for the next answer.

"And the skirt?" He leaned to one side and let his eyes go the length of her, with appetite, then came back up. "That scrap you had on tonight — that's what you wore to it?"

"It's hers." She got a degree of him back for it. "She pulled it off her own rail and put it in my hands and said it'd look better on me anyway." The pride came up in her even now, even here, dumb and warm. "Legs like yours, she said. And that ass."

"I agree." And he stroked up into her, one long deliberate pass on the word *ass,* so the compliment and the fact of him landed together and she made a sound she hadn't planned to make. And his hands, cupping, spreading her that last bit down onto him, tipped her into the wet heat of herself where he could feel exactly what the telling was doing to her. He had her pleasure in his two hands now, portioning it out — and underlining, with a stroke, with a degree of depth, the parts that ran the hottest. Or the most shameful.

"And then?"

"We danced." The floor went out from under the true answer and she snatched at the short one. "I kept the skirt down all night, one hand on the hem. Until — there's a song. The whole room waits for it. It came on." She stopped there.

He heard the middle go missing. He slid her up off him, all the way up to nothing, taking back everything the last few answers had earned her.

"What happened with the song?"

"We danced bigger."

Three words. He didn't give her the inch back. His hands stayed where they were, holding her just off him, and he waited; and being held there — open around just the head of him, the whole rest of her empty and clenching on nothing — was a question of its own.

"What does *danced bigger* mean, Vee?"

"It just got — looser. She moved me around."

She'd tidied it, and he heard the tidying. He didn't stall her so much as simply stop — held her where she was, a hand's breadth off him, and waited; and the stopping was worse than any question, because it turned her back to look at the thing she'd smoothed over. *She moved me around.* That wasn't it. That wasn't what had happened.

She tried to move on him anyway, to steal the friction off his hands, and they didn't allow it.

"She got behind me and put her hands on my hips," she said, quieter, truer, "and moved them herself. Wider than I ever — an arc I'd never have let myself. She made me bigger than I'd dance."

He gave her that — more than an inch, a slow inch and then another, a reward with a slope to it, more for the truer thing. She heard herself breathe out.

"And the skirt rode up some."

Stopped again. Held. And in the held moment she heard the word the way he'd heard it — *some* — and had to look at how much some had really been.

She pushed at his hands, got nowhere, gave it up. "She took my hands and ran them up my sides and lifted them over my head and held them there, so I couldn't hold the skirt down, and my thighs were — open. To all of them. And I didn't—" the reconsidering caught her mid-word, the thing the stall had turned her around to see, "—I didn't try that hard to get my hands back."

That one cost her and it bought her more than the others. He drew her down a long slow way onto him for it, and she took him with a sound she didn't recognize as hers — and felt the flex of him inside her, once, needful, a tightness crossing his face to go with it. And now she could hear things she hadn't — her own breathing gone ragged, and the wet sound of him moving in her, and she could not have told, could not for anything have said, how much of that wet sound was his and how much was Randi's.

"She was against my back the whole time." The parts about Randi's body came easier than the parts about her own; she gave the easy half. "Damp — the silk of her, the heat coming through it."

A hard inch for that, quick and deep — and then he waited, right at the edge where she always cut them off: herself.

She pushed at his hands, got nothing, let them win. "And I wanted it." There it was, out — and once it was out the rest came with it. She'd had a thong on and nothing else under the skirt, and every time Randi swung her wide the whole room got her bare ass, and the front of the skirt was so little she didn't know — didn't want to know — how much of the rest of her it gave up. "They could see all of me. And I wanted it."

He gave her a couple of strokes for that, slow and full, and she rode them with her eyes shut.

"Were you wet?"

The question walked straight into the shame of it and stood there.

"I don't know." He gave her nothing for that, held her exactly where she was.

"...Probably. I think so." Still nothing, and the nothing was unbearable, and the truth came up to end it. "There's no way I wasn't."

He gave her three strokes for it — one, and a second, and a third, deep and unhurried — and they landed right in the middle of her trying to say the rest, so that "the room seeing me like that, her hands making me—" came apart in her mouth, broken over what he was doing to her, and she lost the room for a whole second, lost everything but the two facts of him moving in her and the thing he was drawing it out of.

And then — with her open and ready and the last of it right there to give — he stopped. He wouldn't let her finish it, or move, or come. The disappointed sound that got out of her had something else all the way through it.

The denial doubled the wanting: how completely he had her, how he'd taken the whole tangle and narrowed it to this — difficult, but at least clear.

"Then Harrison." Back to the exits — the joke, the show, the safe shape of it — and she felt his hands settle, ready to stop her the instant she tidied. "She said, let's put on a show, make him wish he were me. And she turned me around and took a — a handful. Of me. Grinning right at him. A bit. A punchline."

"A handful where?" he said — the tease plain on him now, grinning, because he knew, and wanted it in her own mouth anyway.

She tried to move down onto him and couldn't, and the wanting of it made her reckless. "My ass. She took a handful of my ass."

He gave her most of a stroke for it — most, not all, because he could hear there was another shape under this one and meant to have that too. It left her short, aching for the last of him, and she caught his face in her hands and kissed him, fierce and open, trying to take him deeper through the kiss itself, trying to drop onto him while his mouth was busy — and got nowhere, his hands holding her exactly where he wanted her — except his fingers had drifted, she felt it, closer to her seam than the holding called for, pulled toward the heat of her.

"And then?" he said, against her mouth.

"And then she kissed me."

She gave him the plain shape one more time, the last of her cowardice, and braced for the toll — for his hands to stall her, hold her off him until she paid in the truer version.

That wasn't what happened. His hands slid to where she needed them and his hips came up under her, slow and deep and dead-on, and the pleasure climbed so fast and so clean that she lost the thread of the room reaching after it. This wasn't the game. He wasn't trading her inches for truths anymore. He was taking her somewhere, and taking her fast — and she thought, *he's going to let me have it,* and let herself go up toward it.

He ran her right to the edge and stopped. Held her there shaking, over the drop, and would not tip her. And waited.

So she talked. "She put her hand in my hair," she said, the words coming apart. "And it started like the goodbye — we had a goodbye, the little one, end of every brunch, dozens of them — and then it just didn't stop where the goodbye stops."

He held her at the brink and gave her nothing, and the need of it drove the next of it out of her.

"My mouth opened before I decided anything. Her tongue — it wasn't a demand, it was a *please.*"

He gave her a little for that, a few deep strokes, right when she was already at the edge, so she climbed straight to the top and hung there and made a sound — needy, asking, a question.

"You finish when you've finished," he said, and began a slow, shallow rocking inside her.

She should have felt something about that — him rationing her like this, doling her own pleasure back to her a coin at a time; annoyance, at the least. She didn't. It felt right. It only made her body needier, greedier, more his.

So she gave him the rest, because there was nowhere else to put the need. "The taste of her. Salt and lime and under it just her. Her chest against mine—" the words were coming in pieces now, timed to the shallow rock of him "—the little hard points of her, through the two thin nothings we had on—"

He drove up into her once, full and hard and all the way, and it pushed the air out of her in a sound. Then back to the shallow rock, slow, patient, holding her under the edge — and his breath, she heard now, had gone ragged too.
