"My own hand went into her hair," she managed, "and pulled her down. I wanted her mouth lower. I wanted her somewhere we could never—"

He had her at the top again and held her there, and she understood at last what the last coin was. Not the kiss. What the kiss had been to her.

"I wanted it," she said, wrecked, done, past all of it. "I wanted the kiss. I wanted her mouth on me. It was so hot, Pace, it was so — I've never—"

He drove her over. All of him at once, nothing kept back, and she came apart on him, gone where he'd sent her. He went a beat after, up into her, and gathered her whole weight in against him.

She came back to herself in pieces, from a long way off. His mouth first — they were kissing, still or again, she couldn't have said when it had started, slow and open and unfinished. Then his heart, hammering into her breast where they were crushed chest to chest, going like it had somewhere to be. Then the sweat, hers and his run into one, slick everywhere they touched, stinging where it had gotten into her eyes. She was trembling and couldn't have said for how long. When he softened and slipped out of her she felt herself open and run, warm, everything the two of them had just made sliding down the inside of her thigh and onto him, hers and his together — and she let it, and didn't move.

For a little while there was nothing in the room but the two of them, and the third one she'd carried in on her mouth. Salt and lime — she could still taste her, under everything, past the coming; it had not washed out. Her whole weight was down on him now, the man, solid and hers, and the woman was still in her mouth, and she could not have told, even now, which of them she'd been chasing at the end.

---

After, the pressure was gone and the fear came in where it had been.

"God," she said, and dropped the register down into something rueful, safe, a girl making fun of herself, because that was the rail on the stair. "My — my sex brain. It took the whole thing too far. It started as a joke and my dumb body didn't get the memo." She laughed, and it came out thin. "She's my best friend, Pace. She's the best friend I've ever had. It was a stupid party." She heard herself and kept going anyway. "And I came straight here — I wasn't going to sit on it, that's not — I don't keep things from you." She made herself stop. She was explaining too much.

"I know," he said. He was watching her in the lamplight, warm, wholly present, giving her nothing to be afraid of.

"It didn't mean anything."

"Okay."

She wanted him to say more of that. She wanted a wall of it built around her.

He was quiet a moment, and his hand found her hair and pushed it back off her face, and when he spoke it was in the flat plain voice he used for the true things, the one with no push in it at all.

"Anything else you want to tell me?" he said. "About Randi."

She had her answer before he'd finished the question, and it came up out of the rule she'd lived by so long she'd never once had to say it out loud: it wasn't the doing that made you a cheat, it was the keeping of it — the lie in the dark, the phone turned face-down, the thing carried around behind your teeth where no one could reach it. And she didn't do that. She was here, wasn't she, at one in the morning, handing him every piece of it. She never kept anything from him.

And yet there they were: the goodbye kisses, dozens of them, the way they'd drifted months back from the cheek to the mouth to open and stopped being anything she noticed; and her own hand, earlier, in the dark of her room, on the new bare of her, and the name that had been in her head while it moved. But the kisses were just Randi, just how the two of them were, everyone knew they were like that; and the other was hers, a thing a body did alone, to itself, nothing to do with anyone. It sorted itself before it reached her mouth, and her mouth was already moving. "No," she said. "Nothing." No pause in front of it, no catch — level as a countertop.

"Okay," he said. And then, easy, idle, before she could get her feet back under her, like a man clearing the last small thing off a table: "Do you want to sleep with a woman, Vee?"

Not Randi. He didn't say Randi. She'd just told him whose the friendship was, and he'd stepped around the name like a gentleman stepping around a puddle, and she was so grateful for the room he'd left her that she walked straight into it.

"No—" Fast, reflexive, out before the question had finished landing. "I—" And then nothing. The reflex gave out and left her over open air, a half-second with no next word in it she was willing to say, and she felt the drop and got a floor down over it the only way she knew, talking. "It's not like that, Pace. I was so keyed up, all night, that's all. Saturday's mine with you, it always is, and my body just missed you."

"It was a stupid joke that got away from me. It was the drinks. We have so much of this, you and me, and I love it, I do — and it's not that it isn't enough, it's that—" too far in to stop it "—however much we get, I just want more." She heard how that could be taken, and reached for the hardest wall she had.

"I'm not gay."

"I didn't say gay," he said.

"You know what I mean." And because the ground was giving under her now, she did the thing she always did — grabbed the nearest thing that would take her weight and swung the whole matter off herself and onto him. "You're the one who wants this," she said. "You want to hear it. You want me to want—" and the name was out before she could stop it, the one she'd just so carefully folded away, "—you want Randi."

Something crossed his face — a flicker, the smallest thing — and then he answered her.

"You keep saying her name, Vee." Gentle, no edge in it anywhere. "Not me." He drew her in, his mouth at her hair. "It's all right. I don't mind."

He'd defended himself. In every argument they'd ever had he left her the whole of the room — let her say the unfair thing and the true thing both without once reaching to cover himself, so her anger found nothing to push on and spent itself and passed. It was one of the truest things about him. And this once, over this, he hadn't: he'd explained himself, needed her to know she had it wrong, and it mattered to him that much. It was the sweetest thing she had ever seen him do, and it settled over her like a warm blanket.

---

He tucked the blanket up around her, the soft grey one that lived at the foot of the bed, and he put the water in her hand and made her drink it, and he lay back down and drew her in against him, and it was warm, it was so warm, his mouth against her hair keeping her gently lit, and the relief of it came over her in a slow flood.

She'd gotten away with it. That was the first clean feeling she'd had all night. She had brought the whole dangerous thing in here and set it down and it had not gone off; he wasn't angry, he wasn't hurt, he believed her, and in the morning there would be coffee and it would be Sunday and it would all be behind her.

And then, warm and safe and held, with the pressure gone and nothing left to fight, she lay in the dark and thought about Tuesday.

Because Tuesday she would see her. Seven o'clock, Dr. Marsh's room, the two of them side by side the way they'd sat since the term started — close enough all evening to catch Randi's perfume and Randi's murmured aside, that smile that came up before you'd said a word, the lipstick always somehow perfect no matter the hour. The class Vee herself had talked her into, back in the fall, when a twice-a-week night with Randi had seemed like the smartest, luckiest idea she'd ever had. Two nights from now. She had two nights to find a face for it — a voice, a version, a way to sit down beside her and be a person. She thought about Randi's mouth. She thought about it doing what it had done, and then about it doing the things it hadn't gotten to. And Tuesday sat there at the end of the dark, circled, coming whether she was ready or not.

Pace's chest rose and fell against her cheek, slow, asleep, or near it. Her boyfriend. She had just had sex with her boyfriend, twice, good sex, the best there was — she had lost count of the times this weekend, five, six, since Friday, Sondra's table and then his hands and then his mouth and then the mixer and then this — and she was lying in his arms with her body finally quiet and her mind gone straight down the county road and back to campus and into a warm cluttered room with a girl in it, and she could not make it come back.

*What is wrong with me,* she thought.

She did not have an answer. She lay there a long time in the dark not having one, and outside the trees the cold went on being kept out. And whatever had hold of her was not, even now, anywhere near done.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 16: Coming Due — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (coming-due.md) =====

Sunday she didn't leave until the light was going. It should have worn her out and it didn't. There was always a lot of it with Pace — she'd stopped keeping the count that used to scandalize her back in the fall, the one she'd secretly gloated over — but Sunday ran past even that, and twice, which she almost never did, she was the one who started it: reaching for him in the gray before he was properly awake, and again in the afternoon, taking the book he was reading to her out of his hands. Each time something in her let go for as long as it lasted, and then, after, took itself back up. She put it down to the weekend, to greed, to the good long unhurried plenty of him. She drove home in the early dark full of him and not, somehow, filled.

Monday was worse for having nothing in it. No class with Randi, just a problem set, the dining hall with Cassie — and under all of it, all day, the wanting the weekend had set loose, low and constant, and a worry wound so smoothly through it the two came as one thing. It was still there at lights-out. She lay in the top bunk with the window's thin cold coming in and Cassie's breath going slow beneath her, wide awake and wired. She was trying not to think about the floor — Randi crushed to her front, hardly a thing between them, both of them gone hard through it — and her body answered the memory before she could stop it: her nipples tight and sore against her sleep shirt, standing up in the dark with nothing on the other side of the cloth now, no one pressed to her to want it gone.

She kept her hand on her stomach. Flat, still, an easy reach from where it wanted to be, and she would not let it go lower — not because anyone had forbidden it; if anything the message ran the other way, and Pace had made a point of telling her so, in the dark, in the way that stuck — but because she knew exactly where her mind would go the second she stopped minding it, and she was not going to hand it the room. She'd learned that the night of the dance — undressing in the cold, her hand coming onto herself half by accident, and the name that arrived with it before she could head it off. She'd taken the hand back that night like something caught stealing, and driven the county road to Pace to have it put out. It hadn't gone out — not even with him, not even after. Keyed up, she'd told herself. Sex-drunk. Needing him and getting her wires crossed. In the fall the excuses had held. Tonight they went through her hands like water, and there was no driving this one off. So she kept the hand where it was, a lid held down on a pot, and stared at the rough plaster a couple of feet above her face, and waited to be tired enough that the wanting would let her go — because as long as she didn't give it the thing that would make it true, it could stay only wanting. It took a long time, and it hadn't quite let go when she finally did.

By Tuesday she wanted her head cleared, and at four the pool gave it to her: the black line and her breath and the water over the back of her neck, her head going quiet, the whole tangle of the weekend left up on the deck with her towel. She climbed out clear.

The clear lasted as far as the closet. Dressing for a night with Randi at the end of it was usually the best of the day — its own keyed-up private gladness, the good sweater, the skirt that ran the arithmetic in her favor, deciding what Randi would get to see across a table and then getting to watch her see it. Tonight the gladness wouldn't come. What came instead was Tuesday itself — seven o'clock, the saved seat, an hour beside Randi — arriving all at once while she stood at the rail with a hanger in each hand, and her hands stopped being any use. She got the plum sweater halfway on and stood there with her arms caught in it, unable to say whether she was dressing to be looked at or dressing to be covered up, and the not-knowing was worse than either answer, so she pulled it on and left the question hanging and went.

She was late. A little late, which she never was — most nights she got there early on purpose, for the few minutes with Randi before class, though early never once got her there first: Randi was always ahead of her, however early she came. Tonight she'd cut it close, on purpose, and Randi was already in the seat she always took, back third, on the aisle, her coat folded over the chair beside her, holding it. She found Vee at once — she always found her at once — and the smile came up before it had anything arranged on it, and she lifted the coat off the saved chair and patted the seat.

"Hey, gorgeous."

"Hey, you." Vee dropped into it and got her own coat open, and Randi's hand came over and squeezed hers, once, and let go, and her knee came to rest against Vee's and stayed. The ordinary nothing of a shared armrest — except that nothing about it stayed nothing tonight. She was aware of Randi's knee against her own, acutely, the whole hour, and of holding her own leg still against it, and of what the holding-still was costing her. Randi smelled the same. That was somehow the worst of it: that Randi was in every particular exactly herself, right down to the uncapped pen at the ready, and nothing about her had changed and all of it landed different.

Marsh came in with the canvas bag and set something loose in the room, the same as every night — reciprocity, tonight, the three obligations, to give and to take and to pay back, some cold island where a man could beggar his whole house handing things away because a gift was a debt you laid on someone whether they wanted it or not. Other nights Vee would have gone right down into it and lost the hour. Tonight it slid off her.

Somewhere in there Randi leaned in, her mouth at Vee's ear, and murmured — did she have a Lifesaver. Vee came back to the room to find she'd never gotten them out. She'd handed Randi one every class so far; tonight she'd forgotten clean. She dug the roll from her coat pocket, wintergreen, gone soft, and thumbed one loose.

Randi's fingers found the inside of Vee's wrist on the way to taking it — a graze, nothing, the same as any of the few classes they'd been doing this — and it went through Vee low and complete. The wintergreen going between them, and the drag of a fingertip, and her pulse leaping up under it to be found. She nearly dropped the roll. She shut her hand around it and faced the front and did not take in one more word Marsh said. *Static,* she told herself. The skin was thin there, everyone's was, it meant nothing. She held very still, and it went on not being static.

After that she only watched the clock. She'd been dreading this ending for days, and now she got to see it come — the minutes climbing toward the one thing she hadn't solved: the hug and the kiss at the end of the night, waiting for her whichever way she went. Sit the dinner — two hours across from Randi, and how was she going to do that and keep her face together? — and it would be there when she stood up to go. Beg off, and it only came sooner. Randi's arms, Randi's mouth, the soft second press that ended every good night between them: she wanted it so badly that the wanting was the very reason she couldn't let herself take it.

At 8:20 Marsh let them go. Randi bent for her bag, and Vee's eyes went where they shouldn't — to the hollow where Randi's neck turned down into her shoulder, the soft place she'd put her mouth to twice now, both times before she'd decided to — and the guilt of it turned over in her, and she had not got her balance back when Randi said it.

"Having you at the dance was so great. I've wanted to do that for the longest time."

It reached Vee a half-beat late, the same lag it had had in his bed — the word arriving after the woman who'd said it had already moved on to her coat. *Having.* And behind the word, riding in on it, the thing it carried: that she was something Randi got to have. Had wanted to have, and for a long time. In his bed there had been him for it — the whole solid fact of him under her to pour the heat into, to set it against and call it his.

Here there was no him. Here there was Randi — her best friend, the one she told everything to — shrugging into her coat, smiling at her across the class notes and the soft roll of Lifesavers, over nothing anyone could have taken to a priest, and the heat came up for her all the same. That was the part Vee couldn't get to sit still: that it was Randi, that it was her *friend*, and that her body wanted her anyway, with nowhere to put it and no one to charge it to. She had no say in it.

The heat she could wait out; heat passed. But the heat was only the near edge of the thing. Saturday's joke had cracked open a door, and behind the door stood something larger than heat and more durable, that half of her strained toward, wanting to see it, while the other half would not look. What she felt of it was only apprehension: of something that had already happened and hadn't yet shown its size, a thing that reached, in that one word, everything she had.

She could not sit across a table from this tonight and hold still under it — not two hours of it — and she knew that the way you know a fever coming: not here yet, and already past arguing with. She got up to go.

Then they were up and out with the rest of them, and she went where Randi went. Randi was talking — the small easy after-class things, Marsh, the cold, Thursday — and Vee heard the sound of it and took in none of it, her eyes unfocused a little past Randi's shoulder, her head still full of the one word. She nodded where a nod seemed wanted, with no idea what she was agreeing to.

Out in the corridor, floor wax and radiator heat, this was the part where they walked in the cold to the welcoming place with the table and a bottle of red, their loft full of hushed secrets and laughter. Randi was already angling them toward the doors. Vee heard her own voice begin taking it apart — she was behind on the Marsh reading, her head was doing something, she thought she might be coming down with — and none of it came out the size of a reason, because there wasn't one she could hand across.

Randi didn't take it. "Oh, sweetie, no." Warm, sorry for her, already past the refusal and on to the fix. "Well, then I'll walk you to your dorm. We can chat, and I'll make sure you get home safe." And there was no saying no to that. She'd meant to buy her way out of Randi for the night; somehow she'd bought more.

So they walked it. The cold was clean and hard and Randi kept close against it, talking the easy stream of nothing Vee couldn't have repeated a word of, and Vee got herself through it — answering where an answer seemed wanted, her whole mind bent on the one thing waiting at the end, the thing she had to get through and not take.

At the door Randi pulled her into the hug, the ordinary one, and Vee went into it and let it be good. It was good; it was always good, the warm fit of her, the give. She stood there loving it — and, this time, feeling plainly, right alongside the loving, the other thing the hug was made of, the thing she'd spent the whole day outrunning, wide awake in her now with Randi's arms around her and no pretending left that she didn't feel it.

When Randi's face came in for the kiss, Vee turned her mouth away. Not an accident got up to look like one. She turned it, and took the miss on purpose.

Randi drew back an inch, reading her. "What's up?"

"I don't want you to get what I'm coming down with." It came out ready; she'd built it somewhere on the walk without knowing she was building it.

"Ugh." Randi huffed a laugh and let it go. "Keep your plague, then." But she didn't step out of the hug, and still holding her, she sighed it against Vee's hair: "I'll miss you Thursday, though. After class. Standards committee—" a martyr's groan "—I've got to go discipline a bad girl."

Vee turned her face away — some reflex, a looking-off at nothing — and that was when Randi's mouth found the side of her neck, soft, a wet bloom of heat right where the neck goes down into the shoulder, the exact place she'd put her own mouth twice and been told not to weaponize. The goosebumps went out over her in a sheet. A sound got out of her before she could catch it, and she came off the lips and out of the hug all at once.

Randi laughed, delighted, not sorry at all. "See?" she said. "How do you like it?"

Vee stood there with her heart going and the whole side of her neck ringing where the mouth had been, and out of that — out of the ringing, not out of any part of her that was thinking — came her own voice.

"Brunch." A beat, while the rest of her caught up. "Let's — let's have brunch this weekend."

Randi lit up like she'd been handed a present. "Yes. God, yes. Saturday."

Then she was going — a wave up over her shoulder, quick heels into the dark — and Vee still had the stairs to climb and the door to get through, and she climbed them with the neck still live and the want walking up beside her and the apprehension holding its hand, and not one name she'd give to any of it.

Cassie was on the lower bunk with a book against her knees, and she'd not been there long — her coat still on, the cold still coming off her. She looked up and took the measure of Vee's face for a second.

"What was that with you and Randi?" she said. Not the flat way she said most true things — there was a worry under it, and a thread of disbelief she didn't trouble to hide.

Vee opened her mouth for the easy thing — sorority stuff, you know how she is — and nothing came up to meet it, because she didn't have it. She didn't know. That was the whole of the trouble, standing there in her coat in the doorway: her oldest friend had asked her the plainest question there was, and she could not answer it.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 17: Some of Mine — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (some-of-mine.md) =====

She hadn't moved out of the doorway.

Her coat was still on. Cassie's was too — she'd not been in long enough to take it off — and between them the question sat in the warm room like a third person nobody had offered a chair.

She didn't know what Cassie had seen. That was the whole trouble of it. The cold was still coming off her in the warm room — she'd been outside, coming in, while down on the walk Randi's heels went off into the dark; and how close had she passed while the two of them stood in the light of the entrance saying the long goodnight? How long had the hug looked from a few feet off, how deep — and had she seen the rest, the mouth at her neck, the sound Vee hadn't caught in time to stop. Her neck was still live where it had been. She stayed aware of it the way you stay aware of a burn, and it seemed to her, absurdly, that it must show — that Cassie could read the whole night off her skin like a postmark.

"It's —" She reached for the easy thing, the sorority thing, the version that fit the room she and Cassie kept between them. "You know how Randi is. It's a whole —" There were too many words in her mouth and not one of them the size of an answer, and she heard herself setting them down anyway, one over another, the way you stack things over a stain. "It's a sorority thing. She's like that with everyone, it doesn't —"

She ran out. She never ran out.

Cassie let it sit — she had a way of leaving a silence open between you to see what you'd put in it — and Vee put nothing in it, because she had nothing.

"Is this what you want?" Cassie said.

Not what happened. Not what is she to you. The flat, plain way, the same way she'd once asked do you love him — except the worry was still under it, the part that wasn't flat.

And Vee went looking for the answer. That was the terrible part, the part she wouldn't have believed of herself an hour before: she actually went and looked, because Cassie had asked it so simply it seemed like a thing a person would just know about herself. She reached for it. There was a shape there, close and large, and she came up against the edge of it and stepped back off before she could make out its size. Her neck said yes. Her body had been saying yes all night, on the walk, on the stairs — but that was the body, that was nothing, a thing skin did on its own. The question itself had no floor under it she was willing to stand on.

So she reached for the nearest thing that would take her weight, and it was the old one, the one they'd agreed a long time ago not to run again.

"You don't like her," she said. "That's what this is. You've never liked her, you've never liked any of it — and you're allowed, but you don't get to stand there and look at me like that."

Cassie didn't take it. The argument was years old, and Vee had laid it out on the floor like a plank to walk out on, and Cassie stepped over it without once looking down. She only watched Vee a moment longer, and whatever was in her face she kept — the way she'd kept it the night of the skirt, the eyebrow that went up and stayed and said nothing at all.

"You didn't have dinner," she said.

It wasn't a question, and it wasn't quite a change of subject. It was just true, the way the things Cassie said were true. She reached down beside the bunk and came up with the box and held it out.

"Do you want some of my Cocoa Pebbles?"

"Yeah." It came fast and whole, the first whole thing she'd said since she walked in, because here was a want she could find — a want the size of a bowl. "Yeah. Thanks."

She crossed the room and took the box, and that was the size of what she could answer tonight, standing there in her coat: not the other question, the one still open on the desk with no floor under it — only this one, small enough to hold in two hands.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 18: Inversion — Unwritten vignette (inversion) =====

At Brooke’s Standards meeting, Randi—the sister who normally helps discipline others—is placed on the docket herself. The visible case is a ledger of missed obligations and her conspicuous behavior at the mixer; the unspoken case is the house’s growing suspicion around Vee. Randi accepts attendance probation and gives up Thursday dinners rather than invite scrutiny, then closes alone in her single with the cost of concealment. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Inversion; meta-note-inversion.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 19: Gesso — Unwritten scene (gesso) =====

At the delayed Saturday brunch, Vee shows Randi the photograph of her waxed, painted body; Randi names it “Gesso,” the white ground beneath paint. Their charged account of Still Life circles the one taste Pace withheld, and when Vee asks whether it is gross, Randi refuses to validate the shame. Vee flees to the restroom, unexpectedly imagines what Randi would taste like, returns certain she looks guilty, and finds Randi’s knowing smile waiting; she leaves early to avoid the goodbye kiss she wants. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Gesso; meta-condensed-gesso.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 20: The Bricklayers Game — Unwritten scene; title provisional (bricklayers-game-tbd) =====

Vee sits in the bleachers with Kayla and Meg to support Cassie at a Bricklayers game. It is a warm, nonsexual floor-world outing and Vee’s only real dodge of the post-Gesso week: Cassie’s game gives her an honest reason not to see Randi. The reader understands what that ordinary refuge is sheltering her from, while the scene resets the pressure without making anyone else privy to it. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/The Bricklayers Game.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 21: Safely Held — Unwritten scene (safely-held) =====

At the Brooks & Dunn tribute night at The Usual, Pace brings Vee into Sheri’s country-western world. Dancing progresses from floor work to lifts, but when Vee says the lifts are too heavy, he offers and waits rather than putting her in the air without a yes. That refusal to override her hesitation lets the later airborne intimacy move from shame into ease, quietly rehearsing the trust required for restraint. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Safely Held; meta-condensed-safely-held.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 22: Held Loosely — Unwritten SATC scene (held-loosely) =====

At their first dinner after a week of silence, Randi turns Brooke’s “too loose and sloppy” crackdown into a warm complaint, explaining the lost Thursdays without revealing that Vee is the hidden cause. Vee is relieved it was not her, though the new once-a-week pattern makes her want more of Randi while fearing that want. They laugh away the mixer kiss, then Vee recounts the dance and a jar that finally gave way under Pace’s deliberately gentle restraint; her voice changes, and Randi learns how central tenderness is to Vee’s attachment. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Held Loosely; meta-condensed-held-loosely.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 23: The Meg Beat — Unwritten scene; placement/title TBD (meg-beat-tbd) =====

After the game has established Meg as easy company, Vee tries to talk to her about sex and relationships. Meg is practical and untroubled—“it’s just sex”—and that normalization makes Vee’s experience feel too large to name. The conversation remains light and Meg remains unwounded, but Vee cannot say that what she has with Randi is not just sex without exposing what it is. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/The Meg Beat.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 24: Winter Wonderland — Unwritten scene; snow-window placement unfixed (winter-wonderland) =====

On a snow day, Vee and Pace browse a garage sale, where he buys an old Flexible Flyer sled, then take it onto the hills above Blacksburg. The point is their clumsy warmth and laughter in the cold rather than sex: one of the light outings that earns later darkness. It may be a garage-sale/sledding diptych, but its exact position is constrained only by the winter snow window. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Winter Wonderland. Underplanned beyond this.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 25: Grain #1 — The First Flicker — Unwritten vignette (grain-1-the-first-flicker) =====

During an overnight ritual at Pace’s drawer, Vee notices that the scrunchie form may sit lower than it should. Her own use could explain a decline, so the anomaly is neither provable nor speakable; it folds into her larger anxiety about what Pace feels and will not name. The reader receives no certainty beyond Vee’s first half-conscious decision to watch. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Grain #1.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 26: Grain #2 — Worn in Plain Sight — Architecture complete; prose not drafted (grain-2-worn-in-plain-sight) =====

From March onward, Randi wears deep-green scrunchies like Vee’s, and Vee reads the sightings as an ecstatic sign that the glamorous friend is adopting her style. On the first spoken instance, Vee asks whether she has converted Randi; Randi answers, “You’ve ruined me. I used to have standards.” The common style and buyable-but-uncommon color preserve deniability, while later appearances become mere set dressing—except for one post-Restoration delay before Vee’s delight arrives. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Grain #2; meta-condensed-grain-2-scrunchies.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 27: Randi — Social Price #2 — Unwritten vignette; slot TBD (randi-social-price-2) =====

Randi quietly audits what months of absence have cost her: skipped events, uncollected standing, and a chapter social fabric beginning to pull away. No one confronts her; the point is the accumulated private reckoning. She still chooses the arrangement despite its price. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Randi Social Price #2. Underplanned beyond this.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 28: Burn — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (burn.md) =====

"I won't get my brunch this weekend," she said. "Randi's got some sorority thing Saturday." She set it down lightly, over the last of dinner. "I know that's your gym time. I'll just run errands."

He looked up from his plate. "Come to the gym with me."

She almost laughed it off. The gym was his — four mornings a week, sometimes a fifth — and he'd never once asked her into it. "No," she said. "I'd only be a distraction."

"Come see what I do," he said, "while you have your girl time."

Offhand. As if it were nothing. She wanted to go, she found — more than she'd have guessed — and said yes before she'd finished deciding to.

It was in the industrial end of town, a long cinderblock building painted white so far back it had gone the gray of anything that lives beside a highway. The ceiling went up and up on steel girders, too high for what happened under it, built for some other work — a warehouse, a shop for machines the size of rooms. Along one wall ran a line of high bay doors, the rolling kind a truck backs up to, and they were newer than all the rest of it, blue fiberglass, bright and wrong against the old block. She and Pace went in at the side, through a plain heavy metal door with a bar across it and a faded sign for a fire exit.

Inside, it did not look like the campus rec center where she'd gone twice a semester with good intentions and a full water bottle. The mirrors were spotted black at the corners; the floor was bare rubber gone pale where men had stood. Along the far wall the dumbbells ran up a rack of crudely welded steel — built where it stood, plainly, to carry weights no store would rack — from small ordinary ones to black iron things so big they read as a joke someone had left lying out, heavier in one hand than she was in all of herself. And then a man picked one up.

There was no music. The absence of it sat in the room like a missing tooth the tongue keeps going back to. No sound system, no front-desk playlist laid over the top of it; just the room's own noise, the grunt and blow of men working, the flat clash of iron setting down on iron, a plate knocking a plate. Everyone who wasn't lifting had earbuds in or the big padded headphones on, sealed off, each man alone inside his own head in a room full of the same labor. Pace wore none of it. Pace, she was learning, did not seal himself off from a room. He read it.

They stretched first. He showed her without making a lesson of it — one arm straight out to the side, the hand flat to the post of one of the machines, his body turning slow away from it until the pull opened across his chest, and then the other arm the same, and then his shoulders rolling back and around to warm them. She did what he did a beat behind, a copy, and the copying was its own small pleasure, being led through his morning in his place, learning the shape of a thing he knew in his body and she didn't.

Then the bench. He went first, to show her how. He put a big plate on either side and lay down under it and pressed it for reps, a dozen or so, easy and unhurried, the bar rising and settling on one untroubled rhythm, warming the muscle. Then he racked it and stripped it down for her, pulling the big plates off, quick and unbothered, and put the small ones on in their place — not the very smallest — and even so they made her arms feel foolish before she'd so much as touched the bar. He set her under it as though it mattered, as though the little weight were something. He moved her hands out along the knurling and closed her fingers where he wanted them and pressed once on each to seat the grip, and walked her feet back flat under her knees, and set her shoulders down into the bench with the flat of his hand between them, and he did all of it with the same slow attention he'd have given a man about to press twice his own weight. That was the thing that got into her first. Not the weight. That he took her weight seriously. That the smallness of what she was doing bought her back none of his care.

Then he stood in at the head of the bench to spot her, and she was on her back looking up, and he was over her in nothing under the shorts, standing in close above her head with his hands loose over the bar, and she remembered.

She knew this about him. It was a settled fact of him, from months ago on a red-shorts afternoon with a shout of triumph in it. But she had never had the fact delivered from underneath, from flat on her back with him standing in over her face, and it arrived new. Only she knew. In a whole room of men not one of them knew what she knew, that he had come in here with nothing on under the thin cloth and was standing over her exactly like that, and the knowing went down through her warm and private and entirely wrong for a public bench.

She pressed the bar up and it went fine, once, twice. On the third he leaned in to spot her as he'd spot anyone at the top of a set, close, his hands riding the path the bar would take if it fell, and the backs of his knuckles came against her breasts because that was where the bar was and that was where his hands had to be, and there was nothing in his face that meant anything by it. He was working. And she, undone by his knuckles and his shorts and the whole upside-down fact of him, lost it, the entire thread of it, and started to laugh, and the bar wandered, and he took it off her clean in one motion and set it in the hooks and looked down at her laughing with one eyebrow up.

"Something funny?" he said.

"No," she said, which was a lie, and he knew it was a lie, and let her keep it.

Then it was his turn again, and now he loaded it in earnest. He slid the plates on and collared each side, deft, no motion wasted, until the bar sank and bowed a little across the hooks under the weight, three of the big plates a side. He lay down beneath it and set his hands and breathed, and pressed it up off the hooks, and brought it down to his chest, and drove it back up.

That was more than she weighed. The bar bowed under it, more than he weighed too, she thought, stupidly, and on the sixth and last press he drove it up on one hard whoosh of breath, working, controlled, nowhere near the end of himself: more than the both of them together. He racked it and sat up and wiped his face on his shoulder, hardly winded, and she looked at him and could not have said one word.

And only then did she see what he'd done. She could never have saved him if it had gone wrong — never lifted it off him — and so he had kept himself, the whole set, inside what he could put back down on his own. He had arranged the morning so that she was never once responsible for what she couldn't hold. She hadn't even felt the danger, because he had never let there be any.

They moved to the cages along the back wall, two of them side by side, steel uprights taller than either of them with the safety pins set through at the height of a squat. Again he stretched, the legs this time, the muscles they were about to use, folding down over one leg and then the other, and again she went down after him.

He went first. He loaded a couple of the plates, collared each side, and stepped under the bar and stood it off the rack onto his back and stepped clear, and squatted, counting under his breath so quietly she caught it only because she was close, down and up, down and up, unhurried, twenty of them in a long even file. She had seen him with nothing on more times than she could count. But she had seen him in bed and she had seen him at rest, and she had never seen his body doing this, working, under the bright ceiling light with a sheen coming up on him, the big muscle of his thighs bunching and letting go and bunching again, each one, it seemed to her and near enough to true, thicker through than her own waist. His thighs worked and she forgot to be anywhere else. It was not a thing she chose. It happened to her on its own, complete, and pulled her attention down to nothing but him.

When he was done he stripped the bar down, carried the big plates two at a time to the low pegs and hung them, and then took two of the small ones, the ones that had made her arms feel foolish on the bench, and slid them onto her bar. Against the memory of his they looked so slight on the long steel that they might have been hung there to be looked at rather than lifted. He collared them anyway, one side and the other, the same care he'd given his own loaded bar, and she nearly laughed.

"Do my tiny weights even need a collar?" she said.

"Everything gets a collar." He said it without looking up, and snugged the second one home.

In the cage beside them two young men had come in, students by the loose easy loudness of them, and set up to squat. They came in at the edge of things, two more loud bodies in a loud room, and then she caught Pace looking at their bar. One flat look, and something in his face closed and then opened again and let it go. She followed the look and saw a loaded barbell and two boys laughing about something, and could not have said what he'd seen — except that the ends of their bar were bare, and she knew now that his never would be. He was a man who would collar a five-pound plate in an empty corner where no one else collared anything. A little much, she thought, and not for the first time. She turned back to her own.

He set her under the bar as he'd set her on the bench, exactly, no faster for the weight being nothing. He moved her in until the steel lay across the meat of her upper back and shoulders, lower than felt right to her, so that she said so, and he said that was where it went, and put her hands on the bar out wide and told her how to take it, and she stood it up off the pins. It was heavier than the little plates had promised. It settled and found its seat across her shoulders and pressed down, and she stepped back out of the rack with it and shifted the load foot to foot as he told her, learning where it wanted to sit.

He stood in behind her, close, his hands flat on her back, on the broad muscle under her arms, and took her down through it with his own body, slow.

"Deeper," he said, low, at her ear. "Don't bounce it. Control it down and then it's yours to send back up."

She went down into it with his hands reading her, and up, and it was hard and it was good and she was concentrating so hard on the man and the weight and the low voice at her ear that she nearly didn't hear the other cage at all.

Nearly. She caught the shape of it a half-second before it went wrong, a glass already off the edge of the table with no time in the seeing to do anything but see it fall. The boys had started their set. One was down under his bar and the other said something to him with a laugh in it, *you want it in the butt,* and there was the smack of a hand, and the one squatting jerked and laughed and came up crooked, and his bar tipped. A plate came off the low end of it away from her and hit the floor with a flat enormous bang, and the bar with the weight gone off one side pitched the other way and threw its plate loose toward her, and it came down and rolled, iron over that flat old carpet, into her cage, to her feet.

She flinched. That was all it took. Under the bar with the weight seated across her shoulders she jerked away from the thing coming at her, twisted from it sideways and back all at once, and something in her neck caught and tore its small tear, a wrongness that went white and hot up into her skull, and a sound came out of her she hadn't decided to make.

And then his hands were on her, around her, one flat across her chest and one at her shoulder, and he had her, and he stood her up straight and walked her the two steps back into the rack and set the bar down into the pins in one long controlled motion so that the weight went off her and onto the steel and she was out from under it, empty-shouldered, held.

"Easy," he said. "I've got you. Let it go."

He got the bar settled and got her turned and walked her out of the cage with an arm around her and sat her on a flat bench a little way off and crouched down in front of her. He looked at her, at her eyes and then at her hand where it had come up on its own to the side of her neck. He put his hand over hers there a moment, light. He didn't ask if she was all right. He looked at her neck under her hand, and a decision moved across his face.

Then he stood and turned around and put himself between her and the other cage, and the size of him changed without his moving at all.

He didn't shout. His voice went the other way, down and quiet and very exact, and it carried the space between the cages without being lifted at all.

"You hurt her," he said. "Your carelessness hurt her."

The two of them had gone still. The one who'd been squatting came toward Pace with his hands already up, sorry, man, we're sorry, we didn't see her, and Pace let him get that far and no further.

"Apologize to the woman you hurt," he said.

They turned to her then, both of them, and the one who'd done the smacking looked younger than he had a minute before and a good deal less sure, and they were sorry in the plain scared way of people who have just understood that a thing they did for a laugh had a body on the far end of it. Was she okay. Was she hurt. Did she need anything. They asked it over the top of each other.

She said she was fine. She didn't fully understand what had happened to her, only that a weight had come at her out of nowhere and she'd twisted off it and there was a hot wire in her neck now, and against the size of their fright and Pace's cold quiet and the men beginning to glance over, she wanted only for it to be finished, for all of it to stop pointing at her. It's okay, she said. I'm okay. It was an accident. She meant it, and it reached them — but it was not to her they looked. They looked to Pace, and waited, and only when whatever was in his face eased and let them go did they sag with the relief of being let off, and said it once more, sorry, to her, and meant that too, and went back to their cage and took up the collars they hadn't bothered with and put them on, chastened, quiet, and after that the room went back to being only iron and breath.

Whatever he'd been holding stayed in him a moment past her words, a beat behind them, and then went out of his shoulders and was gone.

He came and crouched in front of her again and looked at her neck, and his face closed on what he saw there, and he said nothing. Then he went and stripped her bar, her two slight foolish plates, and carried them to the pegs and hung them where they went, square and quiet, everything put back where it belonged, and he picked up his shake and her jacket and the towel.

"I'm fine," she said. "Go on and finish. I've wrecked enough of your morning."

He didn't answer the offer in it. He didn't tell her she was hurt. He didn't argue with her at all. He settled her jacket over her shoulders and picked up the bag.

"We're done," he said, easy, about the workout and not about her, and it was decided without ever having been a discussion.

And sitting there with her hand still at her neck, the truth her mouth had gotten out ahead of began to arrive: the muscle along the side of her neck stiffening and setting now that the fright was draining out of her, tightening by the minute into a thing she was going to have to own up to, so that by the time she stood to follow him out she already knew, as surely as she'd once known about the long way she still had to fall, that she was hurt — really hurt — and that he had known it before she did.

***

The drive was fifteen minutes of bright afternoon highway, and every one of them told her something the gym had let her ignore. One side of her neck was getting shorter. ===== BOOK TWO ITEM 29: Massage Table — Unwritten scene (massage-table) =====

After Vee strains her neck at the gym, Pace buys—not builds—a massage table for her recovery. The deliberately ordinary, purchased apparatus is the anti-bench: he keeps her still because movement hurts, making restraint a medical, tender gift rather than a demand for proof. It also gives her relief from the shame of what her hands might involuntarily reveal, while introducing a restraint vocabulary that remains protective. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Massage Table.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 30: Into the Dark — Unwritten scene; broad date window (into-the-dark) =====

Vee and Pace take a day trip to a Virginia cavern, moving together through cool formations with the world above shut away. The outing’s closeness is the point, not a literal blindfold or a set-piece of sex. Their descent becomes a quiet thematic prefiguration of later surrender into darkness and being led where she cannot fully see. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Into the Dark. Underplanned beyond this.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 31: Grace — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (grace.md) =====

The gravel ran out the last of itself under the tires and the truck came up into a clearing, a meadow gone tawny and flat with winter, and at the top of it the cabin stood waiting for them.

She hadn't known what to expect — something rented and charmless, maybe, a laminated card of rules tacked by the door — and it was not that. It was built board-on-board, the outer planks weathered to a soft silver-gray and gone darker, almost to pewter, along the seams where the damp sat longest; here and there a board stood a shade off from the ones around it, newer, set in at some point by a hand that had cared whether the place lasted. The wood had shrunk over its years, so that fine lines of shadow ran down between the boards, and a few of the edges had begun to check and splinter where the weather got at them. At the corners the battens were plain and square and true. A masonry chimney climbed one end of it. The windows were shuttered, blind. The whole of it had the particular dignity of a thing built well and plainly and then left to age into itself, and she thought, before she had quite decided to, that it was the sort of place he would choose — that it looked, somehow, like something he might have made.

She got down out of the truck and the cold came up to meet her, cleaner and harder than the cold in town, with an edge on it the valley took off the air down there.

"You said rustic," she said, looking up at it. "Somewhere past Christiansburg I decided rustic meant a lean-to. A tarp and good intentions. I'd made my peace with it."

"It's rustic," he said, mild, like he was standing by the word.

"It is not. It's—" she gestured at the whole of it and gave up on the sentence. "You don't get to call this rustic. There are people who'd kill for your lean-to."

And then the quiet got her, and she stopped. She stood a moment inside it. No road, no one — only the small tick of the cooling engine and the wind working in the dry grass and, past that, nothing at all. "I don't think I've ever heard it be this quiet," she said, lower now. "There's just — nothing."

"There's a lot," he said. "None of it's people."

He went up to the door with the key and it did not want to give. The frame had settled, or the door had swollen into it across some wet season, and he worked it without hurry, a shoulder to it and a lift on the handle and then a long steady push, until it came free all at once and swung in.

He carried his case and the grocery bags. She took her overnight bag, and then, because he asked her to, leaned back into the cab and drew out from under a folded blanket a picnic basket she hadn't known was riding behind them the whole way.

Inside, it was nearly one room. A chair, a chest of drawers, a bed, a fireplace laid and ready, and along the far wall a kitchenette behind a counter that was the only thing setting it apart from the rest. A door at the back stood open on a small bathroom, a shower. The furniture was old and local and good — hardwood gone dark and close-grained and satiny with handling, the joints still tight, a chest whose drawers she somehow knew would run true if she pulled them. Wear on everything and nothing failing anywhere. It was warmer in than out, though not by much, and the air had the held, shut-up stillness of a place that had been standing empty, waiting to be come back to.

She set her bag down and put her arms around him, there in the middle of the floor, and held on a beat past what a greeting needed — glad, plainly and uncomplicatedly glad, to be here, with him, alone, the whole long weekend still in front of them.

He went around the room opening the shutters, one window and then the next, and the light came in and filled the place — the thin watery blue light of a winter afternoon, cool and clean, lying pale along the floorboards and across the foot of the bed. Then he crouched at the hearth and put a match to the fire that had been laid there ready, and held it under the kindling until the flame caught and ran and started to climb, and fed it up a little at a time until it was burning on its own and the first of its heat came out into the room. He left it going. It would have the cold beaten back by the time they wanted it.

They were hungry, and it had been a long way, and the last of the day was going fast. He took up the basket again and she brought the two heavy quilts down off the foot of the bed, and they went back out, onto the porch this time, and sat down side by side on the top step under the overhang, wrapped to the chins, looking out over the meadow with the cold all around them and the fire warming the wall at their backs.

He opened the basket and took out a thermos and unscrewed the cup and poured, into two enameled bowls, something that steamed and threw up a smell of warmth and spice she had no name for. He handed her one. It was thick, a deep red-gold, and she had a spoonful of it before it occurred to her to ask anything, and it was wonderful — round and full, the heat of the spice coming up late and slow at the back of her throat and staying there, good against the cold settling down over the meadow in front of them.

"What is this?" she asked. "It's so good I'm a little angry about it."

"Chicken tikka masala."

"Did you make it?"

"Yes."

She looked at him, and then down at the bowl, and then at him again. He had stood in his own kitchen and made this, and let it cool, and carried it up a mountain in a thermos so that it would still be hot for her on a freezing porch at the edge of nowhere. "What's in it?" she said. "It tastes like about nine things at once."

He named them for her then, drawn out of him by the asking; he gave it up plainly once you wanted it, like facts he was glad to be holding: garam masala, which was its own little crowd, cinnamon and cardamom and clove, cumin, coriander, black pepper; and turmeric over that, and more coriander and cumin, and a little chili for the heat; and under all of it yogurt and tomato and cream, and garlic and ginger.

"I understood about four of those words."

"You don't have to understand it to eat it."

"That's the most relaxing thing anyone's ever said to me." She had another spoonful.

Then from a second thermos he poured them each a cup of something else. A cider, she saw, but not the sweet cloudy kind from a cold stand by the road; this was warm and dry and spiced, all the sweetness cooked out of it and only the spine of it left. She drank, and the dryness cut clean through the richness of the food and set her mouth back to the beginning, and she made a small surprised sound into the cup.

"That should not work," she said. "Cider and — that. Those should not be friends." She took a bite, and then the cider after it, on purpose this time, to feel it happen again — the warm full spice and then the dry edge wiping it clean and sending her straight back to the bowl. "But it does. It really does."

She would never have put the two of them together; it would not have crossed her mind that they belonged anywhere near each other, and that he had known they would was of a piece with all of it — so plainly considered, so plainly meant for exactly this, the cold cabin and the long road behind them and her, that something low in her chest went soft and open.

"You planned the drinks," she said, when she'd surfaced. "You sat at home and planned which drink went with dinner."

"I wanted to please you," he said.

He said it simply, and it went into her and warmed a place the cider hadn't reached. Days ago, in his kitchen, with her nowhere in the room, he had stood there and thought about what would please her. He carried her into rooms she wasn't even in. It was the warmest thing she knew about him. And under the warmth, quick and almost nothing, the old wish moved in her — that he would say the larger thing, the thing she had reached for in December and not been given — and it surfaced and went back down before she could close her hand on it. She let it go. She was good, now, at letting it go. She leaned her shoulder into his instead, warm under the quilt, and let that be the answer. It nearly was.

They finished and sat on with the cider going cool in their hands, and watched the evening come. The sun went down red behind the far ridge and took the last of the warmth with it, and the wind came up and worked in the dry grass and sent a few brown leaves skating out across the meadow, end over end, and there was no other sound anywhere — no road, no voice, nothing but the wind and the two of them and the small tick and settle of the cabin behind them. She had never been anywhere so quiet in her life. She leaned into him and he brought his arm around her inside the quilt, and she stayed there against him while the light failed, until the cold had worked its way down past where the cider could reach and the first of the shivers started in her, and he felt it before she said a word.

"You're cold," he said. "Come in."

They carried in the basket and the bowls and the cups, and the room had changed while they were gone. The fire had eaten the cold out of it; the windows had gone dark and the firelight had come up in their place, low and orange and moving on the walls, and the small space held the warmth the way the porch had held the cold. She stood in the middle of it with her hands aching and the heat coming at her all at once, almost too much, enough to sting her eyes.

He set the things down on the counter. Then he came to her and worked the top buttons of his flannel shirt open, three or four of them, taking his time, and reached for her hands.

"No — Pace, don't, they're freezing." She tried to pull them back, curling them in against herself. "You don't want these anywhere near you, I'm serious, I'm like a popsicle—"

"I know what they are." He took them anyway, both of them, easily, as he took whatever she tried to keep back, and drew them in under the shirt and laid them flat against his bare chest. The shock of it went through both of them — her ice on his warmth — and she made a sound, half apology, and tried once more to take them back, and he held them there. He did not flinch from the cold of her the way another man would have. He kept her hands against the slow steady heat of him until the ache in her fingers began to loosen and let go, and she stopped pulling, and stood there with her palms on him and her eyes coming up to his.

"There," he said. "Not a popsicle."

She left her hands where they were, on the warm breadth of his chest, and tipped her face up, and it was the easiest thing in the world to kiss him. He kissed her back, unhurried, no part of him anywhere but here, and the warmth went on building between them, hand to chest and mouth to mouth, until it was its own thing in the room with them.

There was nowhere to be and nothing to rush toward. He undressed her slowly in the firelight and she let him, and he took his time over her, the slow care he gave everything that mattered to him, and the cold weekend and the long winter and the whole hard tangle of December went off somewhere far away and left them alone. It was warm and unhurried and sure. Afterward they lay folded together under the quilts with the fire burning low across the room, and she listened to it tick and shift and settle, and to him breathing slow against her hair, and thought she could not remember ever being so warm, or so far from everywhere, or so entirely where she wanted to be.

---

Saturday came up clear and hard and bright, the cold of it ringing, and they took their coffee out onto the porch to meet it. He had carried pastries up the mountain too, wrapped in waxed paper and gone a little flat from the trip, and they ate them out of their laps on the top step where they'd had the cider, with the meadow blazing white-gold under the early sun and the frost still holding blue in the long shadow the cabin threw.

When the pastries were gone he went inside and came back with something in his two hands and set it in her lap without a word about it, the way he did most things, and sat back down beside her.

It was a box. Oiled wood, small enough to close both hands around, and she knew the hand that had made it before she knew what it was: the close-fitted corners, the grain run unbroken across the lid, the whole of it brought to that soft satin warmth she had felt under her palms once before, on a bench, in what seemed now another life. He had made this. He had stood at a bench and made her a box.

She worked the lid up. Inside, on a small pillow of silk so pale it was nearly not a color, an oyster paleness, lay a key. A plain brass key, new-cut, the bright burr still standing on its teeth. She had the odd passing sense she had felt that silk against her skin somewhere before, and could not think where, and the not-knowing slid off her as soon as it came, because there was the key.

"You're more than a guest in my house," he said. "I've never given a key to anyone."

And there it was. Not the word she had reached for in December, not the larger thing said out loud into the air between them; but a key cut to his own door, and a box he had made with his hands to carry it up here and set in her lap, and her chest did the thing it had done on the porch the night before, the old wish rising and this time meeting something solid under it to stand on. He had said house. Some small exact part of her, the part that had never been quite sure what to call home, marked the word and waited half a beat for the other one, the one with a hearth in it. The other one didn't come. She let it go, the way she was learning to let it go, because the key was already warm in her palm from his hands, and a key was a thing she could close her fingers around, and the word she wanted had only ever been air.

She set the box down on the step and turned and kissed him, the gladness in her too big now to stay sitting still under, and the kiss went long and then went past itself; and they left the coffee going cold and went back in, into the bright cold room with all the shutters open and the fire not yet built up. He laid her down in the plain white morning light with nothing drawn over the windows and no dark anywhere to keep her, and he didn't reach for one. He looked at her the whole of it, in the unsparing daylight, looked at her as though the light were a thing he had set out on purpose to have her in, and she let herself be looked at, glad, the gladness needing somewhere to go.

In the afternoon they walked down to the lake. She had her own boots this time, worn to her own feet, and the path off the meadow ran easy and downhill and open, no wall of woods pitched up in front of her to read with her legs before her eyes could, only the tawny grass giving way to scrub and, far below through the bare trees, the grey gleam of water. The sky stayed clear. Nothing came down on them. She walked it sure and dry and warm from the moving, the key riding in her jacket pocket where she could feel it knock at her hip with every step, and somewhere on the way down it came to her without weight that the last time she'd done this she had been soaked to the skin and shaking and certain to her bones she had no business on the side of a mountain. None of that was true of her anymore. She went where he went now, and her feet had learned the ground.

The lake lay flat and steel-colored and rimmed at the shallows with a lacework of ice, and they stood at the edge of it and didn't do much and didn't say much, and the cold came up off the water clean and enormous. He stood behind her with his arms crossed over her and his chin in her hair, and she watched her own breath go out and spread and vanish over the ice, and felt, with no complication in it anywhere, that she was exactly where she wanted to be.

They came back up as the light went long and gold, and the climb that would have undone her once only warmed her through, and she was glad of the cabin when its chimney showed over the rise. He built the fire up again. While he was crouched at it she saw that sometime before they left he had laid something out for her along the foot of the bed: a fold of plum silk over the white quilt, deep and rich against it, nothing said. "For tonight," was all he gave her, when he caught her looking. She didn't go to it yet. She let it lie there being a promise, and went to run herself a warm shower instead, to take the cold of the lake out of her legs before the evening came.

She had thought she knew what the evening was going to be. Beside him Randi listened, sleepy, and a smile came up on her face soft and almost tender — and then it woke the rest of the way and turned, and she slid down under the green and took him in her mouth.

At first he thought she was reliving it — going back over last night with her mouth, the taste of the two of them and everything they'd done, trying to catch the intensity of it again and keep it. Then the method showed. Thorough, complete, her mouth working over every part of him not to draw the night out but to take it off — every trace, until there was nothing of last night left on him at all. She wasn't keeping it. She was below him, wiping her own night off him with her mouth, cleaning herself away trace by trace, making him over into something clean and ready for Vee's use — putting herself under the other girl, handing him back to her washed of everything that was Randi's. And it went through him — her under him, cleaning herself off for the other girl — and he twitched in her mouth, and she did not hurry.

"It's all right," he said.

"It is not, I'm mortified. But Randi—" and her voice went warm, went soft with the particular love she kept for her friend "—Randi was so good to me. Drove me all the way home, got me out of my fancy clothes, put me to bed, left me water and everything. I don't know what I'd do without her."

He shut his eyes and held the phone away from his mouth and breathed very carefully. She cleaned him and Vee thanked her for it, both at once, and he lay in the middle of the two of them and let it happen.

"—so I'm going to shower and pull myself together," Vee was saying, "and then I'm coming out there to make it up to you properly. I owe you a surprise. Don't make plans."

"I won't." It came out rougher than he meant. Vee didn't notice. Randi did, and hummed, pleased with herself, and took the last of it.

He got off the phone.

Later, when Randi had gone and the drive was empty and the house was his again, he stripped the bed — the green with the whole night still in it, her scent, all of it — and carried it down to the machine, because he did not want Vee walking into anything that held any of it. He turned the dial to Speed Wash and pressed the button, and took the night off the green — every trace of it, until there was nothing left in the house for Vee at all, clean long before she ever reached the door.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 42: Grain #3 — The Restoration — Architecture complete; prose not drafted (grain-3-the-restoration) =====

Alone at the drawer, Vee sees the form has gone up: roughly four scrunchies have returned, an impossible direction in an arrangement where Pace never touches her things and she never rethreads them. She briefly wonders whether he quietly noticed she was running low, then rejects the comforting story because his respectful noninterference is precisely what the drawer means. With no outward explanation available, she concludes she must be wrong about what she saw, learning to distrust her own accurate readings. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Grain #3; meta-condensed-grain-3-restoration.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 43: The Found Hair — Architecture complete; prose not drafted (found-hair) =====

Vee finds a long dark hair on Pace’s couch or in his kitchen, not in the bedroom. Her body flinches first, then she immediately files it under Sheri—the safe woman—and does not investigate whether Sheri has ever actually been in the house. The trace resolves into a warm, fond thought, showing how thoroughly Vee has learned to wave off an anomaly when an innocent story is available. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Found Hair; meta-note-the-found-hair.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 44: Ordinary Hang — Unwritten vignette (ordinary-hang) =====

Randi and Vee simply spend time together—studying for anthropology finals, sharing a night in Randi’s single, or taking a walk. It is explicitly not a debrief: nothing major need be said. The reader instead feels how a friendship can be genuine and dangerous at once, with Randi’s steering invisible because the relationship has its own ordinary momentum. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Ordinary Hang. Underplanned beyond this.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 45: The Scar — Architecture complete; prose not drafted (scar) =====

Vee returns to the scar she first asked about on the porch, still half-looking for a vulnerability in Pace’s armor. This time he tells her, flatly and without dramatizing it, that his father tied his bike to a car and dragged it because he thought it would be funny. Vee receives the disclosure as a reason to love him more, while the account’s sealed temperature and casual cruelty leave the reader with the colder resonance. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/The Scar; meta-note-scar-reveal.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 46: Cassie — May — Unwritten vignette; title provisional (cassie-may) =====

Just before Cassie leaves for her nursing internship, she and Vee celebrate over lunch: each has earned a summer fieldwork or internship placement, and their friendship is briefly rendered as a mutual peer relationship. Cassie offers a small, funny dating story of her own, an ordinary invitation to reciprocal disclosure. Vee cannot match it, because her own coin is untellable; the gap remains quiet rather than accusatory. Source: meta-plan-chronology Spring/Cassie May.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 47: First Taste — Unwritten scene; exact title/slot TBD (first-taste-slot-tbd) =====

Just before Vee on the Bench, Vee crosses the year-long boundary against tasting herself, and the crossing must be pleasant as well as chosen. Wearing Pace’s costly ivory chemise with pearls and no panties, she tests his whiskey/white-wine language against her own tongue after he offers her a taste; “A shot?” lets her enter through play rather than retreat. He extends her pleasure until need helps overcome the remaining resistance, then gives her release immediately, leaving the act as real liberation with an unresolved instrumental edge. Sources: meta-plan-chronology Spring/First Taste; meta-note-taste-thread.

===== BOOK TWO ITEM 48: Vee on the Bench — DRAFTED RAW PROSE (vee-on-the-bench.md) =====

She texted him Thursday night. *What are we doing this weekend?*

The answer came in the morning. *Come over Friday at 7. Wear a skirt.*

That was all. These put her over him. He liked her over him. Choosing to be looked up at was a thing she could give him before she had even arrived, and she had spent the whole week wanting to give him things, and so she gave him the heels.

He opened the door before she knocked. He always seemed to know. The house met her with its warmth. He warmed it for her before she came; she knew that by now. The first time she had taken her top off under his roof the room had already been warm for it, and the two or three times she had turned up unannounced the house had been cooler, ordinary, a man's house left at whatever temperature suited a man alone, so that she had pieced it together without his ever saying a word: that he turned the heat up ahead of her arrivals, for her comfort, before she was even there to feel it. She came into him and put her arms around him and tilted her face up and he bent and kissed her, the kiss he gave her in the doorway, warm and unhurried and brief.
